0,2026-10-11,"Donald Trump has claimed that Russia and Ukraine have agreed to an ""energy ceasefire"", with Kyiv saying it would support such a move but Moscow yet to comment.
The US president insisted that it was ""effective immediately"" but provided no details.
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky initially expressed surprise at the announcement, before saying Kyiv would support it if Moscow did. He also accused Russia of striking Ukrainian energy facilities after Trump's post.
Trump has been urging Ukraine to stop strikes on Russian oil infrastructure - which Kyiv says are vital to curtailing revenue for Moscow's war machine - so Russia can fulfil a recently-struck deal to release diesel to the US and the global market.
The diesel deal, temporarily undoing sanctions on Russian imports, is attracting growing criticism. Zelensky called it a gift to Russian President Vladimir Putin. European officials and some US politicians, including from Trump's Republican party, have said they do not support it.
The deal is Trump's latest effort to lower domestic fuel prices ahead of the midterm elections, having spent months grappling with the political consequences of the war with Iran which has pushed up global fuel prices, particularly those for diesel.
That has soured Trump's standing with the American public, who have been stung by the higher pump prices and knock-on effects that led to across-the-board inflation.
This video can not be played
Watch: What's behind Trump's energy ceasefire claim?
Zelensky said it was ""news"" to him that an energy truce was in effect in comments to news site Axios, before writing on social media that his officials ""look forward to clear details of America's dialogue with Russia"".
""Ukraine is ready not to strike at Russian energy and we count on Russia's similar readiness and on the agreement of the president of the United States of America on this,"" he said.
The BBC has asked the White House for further details.
UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said Britain ""strongly supports President Trump's call today for a sustained energy ceasefire to start now"", while France, Canada and the EU also expressed support.
Zelensky later said Russia had hit a power plant in Kyiv with a drone, causing power cuts, followed by strikes on energy facilities in the north-eastern Kharkiv region.
""All of this happened after the president of the United States made his statement about an energy ceasefire,"" he said.
For months, Ukraine and Russia have been exchanging strikes on energy infrastructure.
Moscow said the installations it hits were legitimate targets because they supported Ukraine's military operation, while Zelensky has characterised Ukrainian attacks deep inside Russian territory as part of an effort to ""reduce Russia's ability to finance the war"".
The attacks have disrupted energy supplies on both sides.
Huge plumes of smoke seen rising in the skies from the site where the Slavneft-Yanos oil refinery in Russia is located on 6 August
Earlier on Sunday, UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting told the BBC that the UK did not support it, while EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas echoed Zelensky's criticism of it on Saturday.
The deal has also attracted criticism from politicians of both main US parties.
Seven senators, including three Democrats and four Republicans, have published a statement saying Trump's deal ""runs directly counter"" to a law that had explicitly banned Russian energy imports.
""Congress was clear: the US should not import oil from Russia until it ends its aggression against Ukraine and ceases threats against our Nato Allies,"" they said, urging the Trump administration to ""reverse course"".
Republican Senator Thom Tillis, one of the seven, told NBC News that the deal ""absolutely will"" put American lives at risk.
On Saturday Trump accused Zelensky of wanting to ""make problems for the world"" by continuing to authorise attacks on Russian refineries following the Ukrainian leader's criticism of the diesel deal.
The US president suggested that it was ""time for Ukraine to get a new president"".
Zelensky pledged to hold elections - despite them being suspended by the Ukrainian constitution during wartime - if Russia agreed to a 60-day total ceasefire.
The Ukrainian president previously resisted calls from the US for fresh elections, pointing to complications with holding a vote in parts of the country that are active war zones or under Russian occupation.
Trump, however, has repeatedly accused the Ukrainian leader of using the war to stay in power.
Meanwhile, strikes and fighting on the ground continue in the four-year-long war.
Six people were killed in attacks on two Ukrainian cities on Sunday, regional officials said.
One person was killed in Russia's Belgorod region, which borders Ukraine, while another man was killed in a drone attack in the Russian-occupied Donetsk region, officials said.

1,2026-10-11,"Alexandr Wang said he had trouble focusing during recent Meta meetings.
On his mind: New memes to post about the company's popular new AI assistant.
""I would just like think of a meme and I'd be like 'Oh God, I have to get this out,'"" Wang said in a recent interview with podcaster David Senra, adding that his coworkers were ""somewhat surprised"" at the buzz his posts were generating.
""It actually made an impact on growth,"" he said of the posts, ""which is kind of hilarious.""
Much has been said about Meta's chief AI officer and his chaotic X feed, which the 29-year-old has crammed with occasionally off-color yet invariably eccentric references to Muse, Meta's personal AI assistant that sent the company's stock soaring and redefined the market for AI tools.
https://t.co/TXOR8iI4MK pic.twitter.com/NnBHKMdj8X
In his interview with Senra, though, Wang said that his often spur-of-the-moment posts served a broader goal — to make Meta ""cool again.""
""Despite having like these incredible pieces of cultural software like Instagram and Facebook, historically, Meta was not cool,"" he said. ""I remembered that the internet rewards risk.""
After launching on September 8, Muse shot to the top of Apple's app store 10 days later, surpassing ChatGPT. Meta says Muse can help users research, fill out forms, buy items online, book restaurant reservations, find a dog sitter, and connect with services such as email, calendars, Spotify, Instagram, and OpenTable.
The app marked Meta's first successful breakthrough in AI tech, after its Llama and Spark LLM models failed to find niches already filled by widely used models from OpenAI and Anthropic.

With Muse, Meta found a largely untapped gold mine — though competing AI agents from OpenClaw and Instinct already existed in Silicon Valley circles. Muse, however, had the advantage of coming from a popular company with several billion potential customers already signed up on Instagram and Facebook.
And the internet, weary of staid pronouncements from CEOs who were perhaps once cool but are now entering their 40s (Mark Zuckerberg is 42), welcomed the boundless energy of a young executive newly endowed with power in the AI era.
""It was a lot of fun,"" Wang said. ""There were like a few tweets where I just didn't think that much about it and just said, 'Fuck it, post.'""

2,2026-10-11,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Junead Khan, a 25-year-old entrepreneur in Seattle. He worked at Microsoft before resigning last year to focus on his startup, Treasury. The following has been edited for length and clarity.
I had a really good experience at Microsoft when I first joined full-time in 2023.
It was my first job out of college, and I joined right in the middle of the AI boom. I was involved in the rollout of data centers across the world. It was a great opportunity.
Then Microsoft experienced some service outages, and by early 2025, I saw a greater emphasis on security and service stability. It felt like shipping things got slower. That started to take some of the fun out of the job for me.
At the same time, I was watching new AI coding tools emerge. I wanted to experiment with them and get access to them faster. I was making very good money at Microsoft: I earned more than $200,000 in 2024, including stock and bonuses. But I didn't feel like I was learning all the AI skills I wanted to learn.
I started to think more seriously about leaving the company and pursuing an AI startup I'd been working on on the side.
Still, I wasn't ready to quit immediately.
My business idea came to me during my senior year of college.
I was born and raised in the UK but came to the US in 2019 to pursue a bachelor's degree in computer science at the University of North Carolina. I had money to manage in both countries, and I felt like the existing financial tools weren't working well for me.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Around the same time, AI models were getting much better. I started wondering if I could use the technology to build a financial advisor tool for the average person who might not have access to one or want to pay thousands of dollars a year for one.
So after graduating from college and before starting at Microsoft later that summer, I started building Treasury, which I describe as an AI money coach.
For roughly the next two years, I worked on the business at night and on the weekends alongside my day job. Eventually, I started thinking seriously about leaving Microsoft.
Jacob Zinkula writes about how workers in tech and other industries are navigating their careers. Share your story with him by filling out this form, emailing him, or reaching out via Signal at jzinkula.29. Follow his work by clicking here.
Last year, I applied to a few startup accelerators, including Y Combinator. My thinking was that if I got accepted, I'd quit Microsoft and work on Treasury full time.
But I didn't get accepted.
At that point, I decided I didn't want to wait for somebody else to give me permission to go all in on Treasury. So, I started thinking of the money I'd earned at Microsoft as my own seed funding.
I also wanted to be strategic about when I left. I waited for some of my stock to vest and until I'd been at Microsoft long enough that I wouldn't have to repay part of my sign-on bonus.
Last August, after about two years at Microsoft, I resigned and officially launched my company two months later.
When I left Microsoft, I started documenting what I was building on social media, primarily on TikTok and Instagram, but I also posted on LinkedIn and X. Part of the calculation was practical. If I wasn't going to raise venture funding and spend money on paid advertising, I needed to find another way to help people discover the product.
I also thought that, as AI made software engineering more accessible, having a personal brand and an audience would become increasingly important to my career. Even if Treasury didn't succeed, I thought documenting my journey could put me in a better position if I decide to look for another job one day.
The problem was that I had basically no social media following at the time, but I had to start somewhere. I began sharing my whole business journey on social media: the highs, the lows, and even failed product launches. I wanted to show what it was actually like to build a startup by myself without VC funding.
I think that approach resonated with people. Over the past year, my following has grown to around 30,000 across platforms.
About a year ago, I had fewer than 500 followers on TikTok. Today, I have more than 9,000.
My advice for growing your social media presence is to focus on telling your story in the way that suits you best. People might assume that means making TikTok or Instagram videos, but that won't be the best fit for everyone.
For some people, it might be writing articles or making long-form videos. Find the platform and content that work best for you and your style.
One reason I felt comfortable trying this on my own was how much AI had changed software development.
A few years earlier, I think I might have needed a cofounder to help me build the product behind the business. But today, Claude Code and Codex allow me to code so much faster. Building a basic version of a software product without a cofounder or VC funding felt achievable.
If I eventually decide to raise money, I'd rather do it after I've had a chance to validate the idea with an audience on my own terms than give up equity before I've really tried.
Today, I'm still the only person working on Treasury.
Treasury now has paying customers and has generated more than $6,000 in revenue.
It's still very early. I don't pay myself a salary. The money the business makes stays in the company, and I've been living off the savings I accumulated before leaving Microsoft.
When I initially quit, I told myself I'd give the company a year and then see where things stood.
I've continued because I believe I've built a good product and have an audience that wants the company to succeed. Now I'm focused on figuring out how to reach more people and grow the business.
Leaving a well-paying job without paying customers or outside funding was a risk. But one thing I've learned through the process is not to wait for somebody else to give you permission to pursue something.
I used to think: If I get into this accelerator, then I'll quit. If this happens, then I'll do the next thing. Sometimes we look for those things as validation.
I've learned that sometimes the only permission you need is your own.
Do you have a story to share? Reach out to the reporter via email at jzinkula@businessinsider.com, or via Signal at jzinkula.29.

3,2026-10-11,"Apple is preparing new displays and large numbers of demo units for the iPhone Duo's October 23 launch, but supply will be limited, according to Bloomberg's Mark Gurman.
Ahead of the October 23 launch, Apple plans to set aside a section of its Fifth Avenue flagship store for the ‌iPhone Duo‌. Most locations will add an extra spotlight table for the device, using Apple's white display tables with central posts. Gurman said a new fixture will show how the phone folds and unfolds.
The number of demonstration devices will vary considerably between locations. Fifth Avenue in New York is expected to receive more than 150 units for customers to try and for other displays, compared with nearly 100 at the Apple Park Visitor Center. Non-flagship stores will receive approximately 20 units each on average.
Three other prominent stores will fall between those allocations: Chicago's Michigan Avenue, The Grove in Los Angeles, and Manhattan's Upper West Side. Each is expected to have approximately 30 to 40 devices.
Visitors will be able to explore StandBy on untethered phones in the Avenue display areas along the sides of stores. For comparisons on store tables, Apple has designed a MagSafe stand that accommodates an open ‌iPhone Duo‌ and two conventional iPhones.
The retail preparations come as Apple faces tighter supply than it had anticipated. According to Gurman, around two months ago the company believed production constraints could prevent sales from starting until later in November. It has since assembled sufficient inventory to proceed with this month's release, although stock is expected to remain limited.
Apple is also equipping stores with ""Presto"" machines adapted for the ‌iPhone Duo‌. Apple's wireless system can install software updates without opening an iPhone's packaging, and will also add the buyer's name to the greeting displayed during the device's first startup. Gurman previously said the personalized greeting will be offered in U.S. Apple Stores in English and Spanish.
‌iPhone Duo‌ pre-orders open October 16 at 5 a.m. Pacific Time, with sales starting on October 23. The 256GB model starts at $1,999 in the United States.

After going quiet for over a month following the Grand Theft Auto 6 extended reveal on Netflix, the account Cyberleek has been busy over the last few days with fresh gameplay leaks and threats. The latest leak shows the game’s female lead driving around the city, engaging in hand-to-hand combat, and visiting an ATM. It also spends a significant amount of time with the Parachuting side mission and mini-games teased in the previous gameplay reveal. The footage also looks janky in parts, leaving viewers to wonder just how old the build it’s from is.market cap

7,2026-10-11,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Daniel Harnack, a data analytics professional. It has been edited for length and clarity.
About a month ago, I was almost tricked into falling for an elaborate AI scam. A scammer stole a recruiter's LinkedIn picture, linked to her profile, and created a fake email address for her so they could send me a job listing created with the help of AI.
The role was perfectly tailored to my experience, and it sounded like a dream job. Also, the pay was double the reasonable salary for that kind of role.
All the person needed was my résumé to submit to the hiring manager. When, instead of taking my résumé and suggesting I apply for the role on their career site, they suggested I meet with a ""career consultant"" to suggest changes to my résumé, that sealed the deal in my mind that this was definitely going to be a scam where they asked for payment.
This is just a reminder to everyone that AI is making these very individualized scams much easier to pull off.
This was such a surprising scam because of how personalized, tailored, and realistic it was.
The red flags that I'm used to — vague promises, fake names, fake companies, and fake-sounding job titles — had all been addressed or covered up. Unless someone really pays attention, it would be easy to get caught up in a scam like this.
The person used a real recruiter's name, organization, and LinkedIn profile. They put together a job description that matched my experience and looked reasonable, and they responded to my questions without typos or grammar mistakes.
I really think that the current way job seekers are putting themselves out there — noting they are open to work, applying to large numbers of jobs, and publicly posting about the difficulties of the job search — makes us exposed to these scammers who are seeking out vulnerable and eager people looking for jobs.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The unfortunate thing is that I don't think there's a good way to avoid this in the current job market. The fact that the job market requires us to apply for so many roles before finding one that connects means our information is likely out there for scammers to harvest.
Because there is no great method of prevention, the best course of action is awareness and scrutiny over folks reaching out to you.
The core of any scam is the scammer's desire to get either your money or your information (so they can exploit that information for money). The best thing we can do is to never send banking information or payment to these people.
No recruiter should be asking for payment to find you a job. Recruiters should be paid by organizations to fill positions, not by job seekers for the opportunity to be seen. Services exist to review your résumé and help you draft applications, but those services should be transparent that the service is the résumé review, not the allure of a dream job.
My background leading data analysts and scientists helped me identify the scam relatively fast.
I reached out to the real person on both LinkedIn and by sending an email to their work email, but she never responded. I took that to mean that the scammer and the real person were definitely not the same.
Do you have a job-related scam you fell for or avoided that you'd like to share? Email this editor, Manseen Logan, at mlogan@businessinsider.com.

8,2026-10-11,"AMD processors are great for gaming, and there are several generations to choose from. AMD makes some of the most powerful gaming processors on the market, but you may not need the fastest option if you're only playing specific games. There are budget, high-end, and mid-range options for every build out there.
The Ryzen 7 9800X3D is one of the best gaming CPUs. If you're into the latest AAA games, its performance will be more than satisfactory, as it can even outpace more expensive alternatives from AMD. But it's not necessary if you're just playing games like ""Counter-Strike 2"" or ""League of Legends,"" since they'll be able to run at high frame rates even on low-end systems. 
Moving down to lower price points, the Ryzen 7 7700X is a solid mid-range contender that can handle most new games — just not as smoothly as the 9800X3D. If you just need a barebones CPU for casual gaming, there are budget options like the Ryzen 5 7600X, which still performs great in big multiplayer titles.
While it's been topped by the most powerful desktop CPUs in workload-intensive tasks, the Ryzen 7 9800X3D is still one of the best CPUs you can buy for gaming. It can handle absolutely anything you throw at it, allowing your computer to reach above triple-digit frame rates regardless of what you're playing. As long as you've got a really powerful graphics card, the Ryzen 7 9800X3D is the perfect pairing to go with it. At the time of writing, it'll set you back $479 at full price, but deals are becoming more common.
In demanding games like ""Cyberpunk 2077,"" the 9800X3D can reach average frame rates of around 200 FPS at high 1080p settings. The same applies to ""Star Wars Outlaws,"" which can easily reach over 150 FPS even at 4K with ultra settings. You'll even see benefits in less graphically-demanding games like ""Counter-Strike 2"" since those tend to be limited by CPU performance rather than GPU performance. At 1080p, the Ryzen 7 9800X3D squeezes nearly 700 FPS on average out of ""Counter-Strike 2."" The average FPS drops slightly when you bump the resolution to 4K, but the processor will still net over 600 FPS in that case.
Even AMD's budget processors can still handle the latest AAA games as long as you're willing to make a few compromises. The Ryzen 5 7600X is the best of AMD's budget CPUs, and it has some impressive benchmarks for its relatively low price point. Looking at ""Cyberpunk 2077,"" the Ryzen 5 7600X can easily manage a locked 120 FPS at 1080p with medium settings when paired with a decent mid-range graphics card. Not bad for a processor that only costs $219 at retail and regularly drops below that.
It's also great for multiplayer games if you just need a new CPU to jump into shooters with your friends. The Ryzen 5 7600X nets over 300 FPS on average in ""Counter-Strike,"" and its 1% lows are still well above 200 FPS. That helps to avoid stutters, which are usually seen as a common problem with AMD processors. The processor can even handle more demanding shooters like ""Rainbow Six Siege"" with flying colors.
If you're looking for something between a budget-oriented pick and something high-end, the Ryzen 7 7700X is a great sweet spot between price and performance. Taking yet another look at ""Cyberpunk 2077,"" the Ryzen 7 7700X pulls in an average of roughly 150 FPS at medium settings. With the settings raised higher and a high-end graphics card, the 7700X could still easily perform around the 60 FPS line even at 4K. It's a bit pricier, however, coming in at roughly $300 at most retailers. "

Most product teams struggle because the structure around ideas and team talent isn’t optimized for impact. Here’s how to get it right.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
The most common way startups burn through financial runway isn’t a bad hire or a botched launch. It’s building the right product, but for the wrong reason, at the wrong time and without enough validation that customers will actually want it. I’ve seen it happen at companies with great teams, strong funding and real market opportunity. 
Here’s what we’ve learned about keeping a product team oriented toward impact and away from the kind of well-intentioned “busywork” that can kill off startups.
Most founders are rigorous about validating products before launching them. Fewer apply that same discipline to how their product team actually operates. They convene large groups, whiteboard a process, roll it out… and then watch it fall apart under real conditions because nobody pressure-tested it first.
The solution is to treat your process itself like a product. Start with a small group — perhaps two or three people with genuine cross-functional exposure — then run through real decisions fast. What you’re looking for are the questions that surface every single time you evaluate an opportunity. Those recurring questions become the foundation of the evaluation framework. As CEO, your job is to help make sure that framework exists and that the team is working within it, not to be in every room where decisions get made.
For example, a two-person team at a software development company might discover that every feature discussion comes back to one question: “Will our enterprise clients’ legal teams actually approve this?” Surfacing issues like this early and building them into the evaluation framework saves weeks of wasted effort. 
Or consider a consumer app team that kept building features users said they wanted in surveys, only to see flat adoption at launch. This could be because they never validated whether each feature fit the actual usage context and wasn’t just answers to a survey.
The goal is a process blueprint you can pressure-test and evolve as needed.
From a founder’s perspective, one of the most consequential hiring and organizational decisions you’ll make is who sits at the center of your product process. Product teams naturally play a connector role between engineering, client success, legal, marketing and operations. But that role only works if the people filling it have genuine exposure across those domains, not just a passing familiarity. 
The most valuable contributors in your product process aren’t necessarily the most senior people in a function. Instead, the people who wear enough hats and can represent multiple perspectives, anticipate friction from teams they don’t officially belong to and make judgment calls that account for a fuller picture are the ones you want driving the conversation.
Be intentional about identifying those types of team members. They’re often not the ones with the most obvious titles. But they are often the ones identifying early issues in your roadmap.
Runway is finite, and every build decision is actually a capital allocation decision. One of the most expensive mistakes is a well-built feature that nobody wants. By the time teams discover a post-launch product-market fit problem, they’ve already spent the time building it, absorbed the opportunity cost and potentially confused target customers.
Before committing serious build time, pressure-test every idea against a few essential questions: Does this solve a real problem your customer actually has and not just one they described in a survey? Is there a plausible path to revenue generation? And relative to the level of effort, does it have meaningful revenue potential?
Rank ideas by impact divided by effort. It doesn’t need to be a sophisticated formula; a simple high/medium/low estimate on both axes can get you most of the way there. And complete the customer validation before you build, not after. By the time the product ships, you should already know who will want it.
CEOs monitor revenue. But if you’re only measuring what ships and what it earns, you’re seeing the outcome without understanding the process behind it. The health of your product function shows up earlier in the pipeline. If you’re not watching those signals, problems compound before they show up in the P&L. 
The most useful process signal to track is adoption rate at launch. If validation was done correctly upfront, customers should already be lined up when it ships. A feature with no adoption is basically just sitting in a “warehouse;” unsold and unused. 
Earlier in the product pipeline, track the volume and quality of ideas moving through each product validation stage. A well-functioning product team should be like a machine, consistently outputting impact. And as CEO, you should be able to see how well this machine is running before it impacts your financials.
The word “process” makes founders cringe, because processes can often be bureaucracy in disguise.
The only process worth keeping is one that makes your team faster at value creation. Every checkpoint in a process should exist because it demonstrably reduces wasted effort downstream. If it doesn’t, cut it. And trust your team to interpret process frameworks intelligently. Empower them to skip a step when it’s warranted, as long as it’s flagged so everyone stays aligned. 
The common thread across all five of these principles is intentionality: being intentional about who’s in the room, what gets built, in what order and why. Most product teams struggle because the structure around ideas and team talent isn’t optimized for impact. As CEO, it’s ultimately your responsibility to get structure right. 
A framework is a set of best practices, not an ironclad rulebook. The teams that get this right understand why each step exists, but also know when to use judgment instead. Orient everything toward impact because that’s the only metric that actually matters.
The most common way startups burn through financial runway isn’t a bad hire or a botched launch. It’s building the right product, but for the wrong reason, at the wrong time and without enough validation that customers will actually want it. I’ve seen it happen at companies with great teams, strong funding and real market opportunity. 
Here’s what we’ve learned about keeping a product team oriented toward impact and away from the kind of well-intentioned “busywork” that can kill off startups.
Most founders are rigorous about validating products before launching them. Fewer apply that same discipline to how their product team actually operates. They convene large groups, whiteboard a process, roll it out… and then watch it fall apart under real conditions because nobody pressure-tested it first.

10,2026-10-11,"Tokenized stocks are already delivering two of crypto’s long-promised benefits: round-the-clock trading and the ability to buy a fraction of a share. They are also markedly less liquid and more volatile than the conventional equities they track, according to a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) study.
The IMF’s latest Global Financial Stability Report, titled Scaling Tokenization: New efficiencies and new vulnerabilities, examines the five most actively traded tokenized U.S. equities, including Tesla (TSLA), Nvidia (NVDA) and Alphabet (GOOG) as well as measures such as the Nasdaq 100 Index, across centralized and decentralized venues.
More than half of trading took place outside regular U.S. market hours, the IMF found. About 80% of trades were for less than one share. For the report, those figures are evidence that investors value 24/7 access and lower entry points, not just the technology behind a tokenized stock.
The study also found that overnight moves in tokenized stocks carried useful information for the underlying shares. Once U.S. markets opened, more than 85% of the overnight movement in tokenized shares was reflected in their traditional counterparts within five minutes, the IMF said.
“Moving assets onchain is only the first step,"" said Bitget CEO Gracy Chen. ""The bigger question is how efficiently that capital can work once it is there.”
The IMF’s message is that while the use case is real, the market is still nascent. It said the tokenized real-world asset (RWA) market has grown rapidly, estimating it at about $65 billion as of July 31. Tokenized equities accounted for roughly $2.3 billion of the total. That compares with a 2025 global equity market capitalization of just under $160 trillion, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association (SIFMA), the trade group for the U.S. securities industry.
Still, the tokenization project is growing. Bullish (BLSH), a Gibraltar-based crypto company and CoinDesk’s parent company, introduced tokenized equity trading in August. Earlier this month, OKX and Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), which owns and operates the New York Stock Exchange, filed plans for a venue offering round-the-clock trading in tokenized U.S. shares.
They are not alone. Crypto exchanges Coinbase Global (COIN), Kraken and Binance also offer tokenized stock trading, as does Robinhood Markets (HOOD).
According to the IMF, tokenized stocks were about 1.5 times as volatile as the equivalent shares on traditional venues and significantly less liquid.
The problem is not simply that blockchain markets are small. Tokenization needs enough issuers, investors, trading venues and settlement assets on compatible systems to deliver its promised savings in cost and time. Today’s market is split across private platforms, public blockchains, custodians and settlement tools that often do not work together.
The IMF said tokenization could replace some of the manual work firms do to reconcile records, automate tasks such as dividend payments and speed up collateral transfers. It warned that automated margin calls and liquidations, collateral moving between platforms and 24-hour trading could make a market shock harder to contain.
The risks are still small because tokenized markets remain small, the IMF said. But its report argues that legal rules on ownership, safeguards for liquidity, links between systems and settlement arrangements need to be developed before the market grows much larger.

11,2026-10-11,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Denise Woodard, founder and CEO of Partake Foods. The following has been edited for length and clarity.
I spent my career in consumer packaged goods and spent significant time at The Coca-Cola Company. Then my daughter Vivienne came along.
She's 11 now, but right around her first birthday, we had a couple of scary incidents that alerted us to the fact that she had multiple life-threatening food allergies. She was allergic to foods I consider to be super simple: eggs, tree nuts, bananas, and sesame.
I started to realize that food allergies were going to become a big part of our everyday lives. I was really frustrated with the options from a taste and nutritional perspective. Most of all, I thought about how, emotionally, she wouldn't be able to partake in so many social events like birthday parties, playdates, and school snack time, and that she'd always feel excluded and othered.
I wanted to create a solution for that.
In June 2016, I incorporated my food company as Vivi's Life LLC, because I just wanted to make my daughter's life easier.
We officially launched in the New York market, in independent stores, in August 2017, and from then all the way through 2019, I was knocking on doors and selling cookies out of my car.
Our marketing strategy was super grassroots: local trade shows and events, and demos in grocery stores. We didn't have any extra dollars, and those things cost our family time. It was a full-family adventure. We used to drive around with a demo table in the back of our car, and if we were in the city for the day and went to a store that wasn't selling cookies, we would literally pull up and do a demo in that moment.
The storytelling and the tasting of the product were our entire marketing strategy for the first couple of years. Even as we've scaled and moved away from demos, storytelling is such a huge part of the business, and not just the consumer marketing.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
When I think about how we got our first investors on board, like Marcy Venture Partners, the fund that Jay-Z co-founded, it was the storytelling of why I was doing what I was doing and what I wanted it to stand for that sold them.
If I were launching today, I'd still lean into a grassroots approach, but there are more scalable ways to tell your story now. There are only so many people I could talk to in a natural foods grocer in New York, whereas on TikTok, millions of people can literally see your story.
As the world gets more automated, I think human touch and storytelling are even more valuable.
When we had an opportunity, we went after it, and I think we went after it too boldly in terms of distribution. In 2019, we were in 300 stores, doing $350,000 in revenue. We finished 2020 in 5,000 stores, doing 10 times that amount, and then we doubled our door count after that.
It costs money, and it takes infrastructure and process, and it requires brand awareness that we couldn't pay for. That became an almost deadly challenge for the business. Rather than jumping in both feet first, we should have waited to prove some things out.
Like so many startups, we also got caught up in a growth-at-all-costs mentality. So many investors and other founders shooed away the focus on unit economics: you'll get profitable when you scale. Then the market turned, and all of a sudden it was profitable at all costs. That's not a switch you can flip on and off without affecting people's careers and the trajectory of the business.
I wish I had taken a more temperate approach.
It's really important to get multiple perspectives, but as the founder, I know more about this business than anyone else. Someone's years of experience doesn't equate to them having more knowledge than you do about what's right for your business.
Sometimes the advice wasn't even given to me. I have such admiration for what Daniel Lubetzky built at KIND and Hamdi Ulukaya built at Chobani, but both of those were in a very focused category. I watched their journeys and then applied the way they built to my own company in a way that I shouldn't have. We're operating in a very different category. And I wish that we had innovated sooner.
Now Partake Foods is launching a protein and fiber granola later this fall and soft-baked individually wrapped cookies in Q1 of 2027. Protein is on fire in all the data, and fiber-maxing is the next hot thing.
In September, we announced that the Girl Scouts cookie program chose to partner with us, and the cookies will be available for purchase from the Girl Scouts' Digital Cookie mobile app in January 2027.
The Girl Scouts program is over 100 years old. I'd argue they probably have their choice of partners, but I think they chose us because of the brand equity we've built with the food allergy community. This has taught me that just because you're not the biggest company doesn't mean you don't bring value to much larger partnerships.
To me, cultural capital is what's important. It's when a person or brand has earned enough relevance, trust, and authenticity that they don't just participate in culture — they have the ability to move it. For Partake, the cultural capital I want us to build is around inclusivity.
We started with food allergies, but I've always believed the idea is much bigger than that.
Our board is 80% women of color, the team is extremely diverse, and we have a 501(c)(3) aimed at increasing diversity in the food industry. It's all around this ethos of building a bigger table. I have this plus-one mentality: How can you always bring one more person into the room, make one more introduction? Somebody saying yes or making an introduction has completely changed the trajectory of my business and my life so many times over.
Size doesn't necessarily matter when it comes to having influence. We slid into Ben & Jerry's LinkedIn DMs in 2020 when we were a team of five people. In January 2023, we announced our partnership with them to provide the gluten-free oatmeal cookie pieces inside their varieties of Oatmeal Dream Pie available in pints and scoop shops.
I was one person selling cookies out of my car. What business did I have thinking I was going to go find Jay-Z? Dream big and shoot your shot. If you don't, it's not going to happen.
Do you have a small business lesson to share? Email the editor, Manseen Logan, at mlogan@businessinsider.com.

13,2026-10-11,"

      Scott Horsley
    


                In a sluggish hiring environment, people who are unemployed are taking longer to find new jobs. In many cases, unemployment benefits run out long before people return to work.
                
                    
                    Joe Raedle/Getty Images North America
                    
                
hide caption

Evan Smith lost his job at a Colorado RV dealership this past spring after the company was sold. He's applied for numerous jobs since then, but hasn't found much traction.  
""You find something that you're excited about or something that you think fits your profile and you go in and over 100 people have already clicked 'applied for it' in the last two weeks,"" Smith says. ""So you just feel like you're a number.""
In today's stagnant hiring environment, the numbers are not encouraging.  U.S. employers added just 29,000 jobs last month.  That's one job for every 244 unemployed workers.  The average worker is taking longer to find a new job, and that means more people are exhausting their unemployment benefits before any new paychecks begin. 
To be sure, the unemployment rate has remained very low: just 4.2% in September.  But that's little consolation for those who are stuck on the outskirts of the job market, knocking on doors that rarely open.
""When you're one of the people that doesn't have a job, it doesn't really matter what the percentage is,"" says Cheryl Huff. ""It's impacting your life in a big, big way."" 

                Cheryl Huff has applied for dozens of jobs since getting laid off by the state government in Colorado four months ago. She's landed only a handful of interviews.
                
                    
                    Cheryl Huff
                    
                
hide caption

Huff was laid off from a state government job in Colorado four months ago. Since then, she's applied for dozens of positions, spending hours on each one, tailoring her resume to the specific job requirements. She's gotten only a handful of interviews —all by telephone — and a lot of what seem like automated emails.
Huff once got two messages at once from the same employer, one saying her application was under review and the other saying the job had already been filled. 
""That's one of the frustrations that people are having,"" Huff says. ""Did you even look at my stuff?"" 
In the meantime, Huff is paying $1,300 a month to maintain her old employer-provided health insurance. Her husband is retired, so their family budget is tight.
""It's Social Security for him and unemployment for me,"" Huff says.  ""It's amazing how little money that is when you go to the store and you see a bag of granola is $8.50.""
The clock is ticking. Colorado offers six months of unemployment benefits, and Huff's are set to run out in early December. In many states, benefits expire even faster. 
North Carolina offers less than three months of unemployment. Keke Moore in Raleigh has been out of work for almost a year.

                Keke Moore lost her job as a meeting planner in Raleigh, North Carolina last December, and since then she's struggled to find a new position.  The state offers only 12 weeks of unemployment benefits.
                
                    
                    Keke Moore
                    
                
hide caption

""With so many people being out of work, we're all out here, it feels like to me, fighting for scraps,"" says Moore, who lost her job as a meeting planner last December. 
Even when she was receiving unemployment, North Carolina benefits are capped at $350 a week. Now that even that limited aid has expired, Moore's 21-year-old son has been helping out with the household bills.
""Bless his heart, he does have a part-time job, so he's been doing what he can to help his mama out,"" Moore says.  ""But I mean, I feel like a failure as a parent, because it's like I don't want him worrying about bills.""
Of the more than 7 million unemployed people in the country, nearly 2 million have been out of work for six months or more.  
Aside from the financial stress, there's an emotional toll to being out of work so long. 
Evan Smith, the laid-off RV worker, says job-hunting itself is an exhausting full-time gig.  Sometimes he goes for a hike just to give himself a break.
""My wife, usually on a Friday when we're sitting on the back porch after work — after she gets done with work — she's like, 'How are you feeling?'"" Smith says.  ""Keeping a good attitude, I think, is huge. Getting discouraged is really freaking easy.""

17,2026-10-11,"It's been over a year since Hannah Bates, 31, was sued by her private student-loan lender — a legal battle she called lengthy, expensive, and confusing.
It started in October 2024, when Sallie Mae notified her that she'd made the final payment on her outstanding balance of $55,000. Bates knew that was a mistake. In fact, she was falling behind on her $1,500 monthly payments and had years' worth left. When she tried to clarify with Sallie Mae, she said she received conflicting information on how to avoid default. Six months later, Sallie Mae sued her to collect.
When she's not working her 12-hour nursing shifts, Bates has represented herself in the lawsuit, with the help of ChatGPT. She said it's taken a significant emotional and financial toll, and after multiple unsuccessful attempts to reach a payment agreement, the court ordered that her wages be garnished.
She lost $884 from her roughly $2,700 paycheck for two pay cycles, prompting her to pick up extra shifts at her hospital.
""It's the weight of the world,"" Bates said. ""Some days are better than others, but I didn't think I'd be here.""
More student-loan borrowers could soon find themselves in Bates' situation. Private student loans comprised only 10% of the $1.85 trillion student-loan market in 2025, according to Consumer Financial Protection Bureau Data. But President Donald Trump's repayment overhaul, which took effect July 1, is expected to drive more borrowers to private lenders like Sallie Mae, which lack substantial federal oversight.
Business Insider's previous reporting revealed widespread confusion in lender communication as borrowers moved toward default and litigation — about one in four complaints filed with the CFPB in 2025 were about private student loans.
A report from advocacy group Protect Borrowers said that private lenders sued more than 100,000 borrowers between 2012 and 2016. Bates said that her legal process taught her that she's ""very much not alone.""
""At first, I was really embarrassed and ashamed to talk about it,"" Bates said. ""Now I'm absolutely not.""
This story is part of Business Insider's ""Student Debt Spiral"" series, which explores how rising student debt is changing the financial futures of millions. Do you have a story to share? Fill out this form, and we'll be in touch. Read more of our student-loan coverage here:
Before defaulting, Bates was paying what she could afford — around $560 a month — but was unable to make her full $1,500 payments. She tried and failed repeatedly to negotiate a lower monthly bill, and didn't know how to return to good standing after she defaulted.
""I didn't hear anything for months,"" Bates said. ""I had contacted them once or twice throughout this process, waiting to hear back, and no one ever had an answer for me.""
A Sallie Mae spokesperson previously told Business Insider that it could not comment on Bates' account and that the company ""proactively and consistently"" communicates with its customers, including sending notices when a customer defaults, when that defaulted loan is sold, and informing them of the new owner.
After Bates was sued, the judge encouraged her instead to reach a settlement or agree to a payment plan with Sallie Mae. Bates said Sallie Mae offered her a payment plan of around $1,600 a month — higher than the original payments that had led her to default in the first place.
""That's not feasible,"" she said after the hearing. ""I am more than willing to pay back what I took, but it needs to be a payment that is manageable, and that's not going to put me on the streets.""
Ayelet Sheffey covers all things student debt, from the latest Education Department news to stories of borrowers navigating their loans. To stay up to date on her coverage, sign up to get email alerts for her new stories here.
She requested a lower payment plan and waited months for Sallie Mae's sign-off. She saw in August that a garnishment notice had been added to her court docket. Weeks later, she lost a chunk of her paycheck.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
""It's hard going to work knowing that with everything you deal with and go through, you're not getting paid the way that you ultimately deserve,"" Bates said.
At the end of September, a judge signed off on a plan allowing her to make $600 monthly payments toward her balance until it is paid off. It won't be easy, she said, but it's preferable to losing about $1,700 a month to wage garnishment.
""Home-wise, with bills and finances, it's not 100% feasible,"" Bates said. ""But it's given me some sort of empowerment because at least I have the ball back in my court, and the money won't just be taken from me.""
Bates will begin making payments through the court's payment system on November 1. Looking back, she wishes she had better understood how lengthy litigation can be, so she could have better prepared herself for the legal battle ahead. While she still has years of payments to go, she's relieved to close this chapter.
""I'm optimistic,"" Bates said. ""I feel a lot better mentally because it's not consuming my life as much as it was.""

18,2026-10-11,"After eight years in the same home, my family needed a change. We planned to rent while we built a new house, imagining a brief transition between the two. Instead, the rental market had other plans.
Apparently, it was a very popular time to be looking for a new place to live. With multiple families vying for each house, landlords could be choosy. Being self-employed with dogs put us near the bottom of the list. In the meantime, we were living in an extended-stay hotel. Still, I wasn't discouraged because…well, how long could it take to find a match?
What I thought would be a two-week hotel stay stretched into nearly three months. During that time, my husband, our 1-year-old daughter, Talia, two dogs, and our cat learned what it meant to live with little space. More surprisingly, hotel living changed how I parented — and eventually, my views on home.
We moved everything we owned into two storage units, and I packed for a short stay. We took what fit in our SUV: a large suitcase, a bag for my daughter, stuffed with pajamas and outfits, a few toys and books, a box of diapers, a litter box, the travel stroller we kept in our car, and a travel wagon that would become a lifeline.
By the second week, I was making weekly trips to storage to rotate my daughter's toys. Living in a hotel can make toddlers bored fast.
I also started feeling boxed in. I missed my home, hated walking the dogs at all hours, and dreaded the slow elevators that occasionally got stuck. Some days, I'd climb four flights of stairs or take my high-energy dog for runs. Talia and I frequented the supermarket just to keep from going crazy.
I also had to rethink some parenting rules. At home, I was against both junk food and screen time. But the hotel breakfast buffet had limited vegetarian options. When Talia pointed at the plain donuts, I let her have one. She usually took one bite. She also asked for my husband's vegan sausages, so she had one with him most mornings.
Screen time became another compromise. I still needed to work, and concentrating while sharing a hotel room with a toddler was nearly impossible. Fifteen minutes of Miss Rachel or Gracie's Corner gave me enough time to put on my noise-canceling headphones and dump a thought onto the page before I forgot it.
When a storm ruined our beach plans, a friend suggested we use the hotel's conference room for a change of pace. It became part of our routine, with Talia playing peekaboo around the tables with the sales manager.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Not everyone in an extended-stay hotel is a long-term guest. There were vacationers, business travelers, military members waiting for traditional housing, truck drivers, and people whose employers maintained corporate accounts.
Then there were families who simply had nowhere to go.
I met veterans and families navigating the housing system. Agencies sometimes place families in hotels until they find housing. Extended-stay hotels can accommodate entire families — including the dogs and cats that can make rental searching even harder.
I also bonded with the staff. One woman spoke only Spanish, yet somehow, I started picking up on what she was saying, which allowed me to help translate for other guests. I chatted with second-shift front-desk workers, who shared their aspirations with me and looked the other way when I snuck into the kitchen for cornflakes. Talia earned the nickname ""spicy water baby"" because she and I shared seltzer from the bar.
I spent weeks worrying about what she was missing by not being home and what bad habits would stick. Now that we've moved, she still asks for ""Pad,"" but only needs a few minutes before wandering off to color or read. And every day feels like 15 minutes of Christmas as she rediscovers another toy from storage.
When we finally left the hotel, I expected relief. Instead, I felt a strange tug of homesickness.
In those nearly three months, my daughter had gone from saying a random word or two to speaking full-on toddler gibberish. She stopped face-planting in the hallways and learned to run.
Talia was living her life, enjoying playing in the conference room, splashing in the pool, and making friends with the people who knew her name. Maybe that was what I hadn't understood when we checked in with one carload of belongings and a two-week plan: Home is wherever your family is.

19,2026-10-11,"Where should you go in California for delicious food, sprawling vineyards, and the satisfying sound of a freshly uncorked wine bottle?
Let me guess, you're thinking of Napa.
But what if I told you there's a hidden gem on the central coast where you can get all of the above with fewer crowds, lower prices, and cooler tasting experiences?
Welcome to Paso Robles.
Growing up in the Bay Area, Napa Valley quickly became a special place for my family. With no relatives living in the US, my Greek parents took us there every Christmas to make the holidays feel special.
I've visited Napa over 20 times, but over the past few years, Paso Robles has stolen my heart.
Paso Robles is almost exactly in the middle of Los Angeles and San Francisco, about 200 miles each way. Parallel to the mountains and the sea, the region has a unique terroir due to big temperature swings. The topography's distinctive microclimates are perfect for growing a variety of grapes, helping Paso distinguish itself in a crowded California wine market.
""The diversity of this area really separates itself from the monoculture that Napa has become,"" Andrew Jones, the owner of Field Recordings Winery, told me.
Adding to that diversity is the low barrier to entry in the Paso Robles wine industry. Most of the wineries are small and family-owned, and the winemakers are often on-site to guide you. This refreshingly laid-back approach makes tastings feel intimate and approachable, and they're at a far more accessible price point than Napa, where the high cost of winemaking has trickled down to tourists. Silicon Valley Bank's 2026 direct-to-consumer wine report found that the average tasting fee in Napa is $79, while it's $27 in Paso Robles.

                                    Every time Anneta publishes a story, you'll get an alert straight to your inbox!

Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
""Napa has a taller order; they have to live up to a brand that's worldwide,"" said Neeta Mittal, one of the first Indian women to open a winery in the US. ""Where we thrive in Paso is we're able to etch our own identity, and that's a great nimbleness that we're allowed.""
Mittal and her husband, Kunal, own LXV Wine, which offers the only wine-and-spice pairing in the US. I visited LXV on my first trip to Paso Robles and was delighted that my tasting included cheeses covered in various spices. I learned how ginger and black truffle salt can bring out the flavors of Cabernet and why Szechuan peppers and Nebbiolo pair so well.
I also love the tastings at Field Recordings, which prides itself on making wines that ""are all about the flavor — with none of the pretension,"" as its website reads. Jones specializes in small-batch, single-vineyard wines. They're fun and funky, decorated with colorful labels and names like ""Kitties"" or ""Super Gnario.""
Field Recordings is in Tin City, my favorite part of Paso Robles. The converted industrial hub features over 20 wineries, all within walking distance of each other, where tastings typically cost between $20 and $30. It's perfect for a wine crawl, but there's also a brewery and the very fun Tin City Distillery if you're craving cocktails instead.
Another reason I keep going back to Paso Robles is the fantastic restaurant scene. I never skip a dinner at The Alchemists' Garden, which has a fun cocktail list and some of the best fries.
My parents still rave about the meal we had at Il Cortile Ristorante. Its pappardelle is handmade and paired with a rich, luscious Bolognese, and the branzino is light, bright, and cooked to perfection. But the star is the risotto covered in white truffles, a holiday splurge I've never forgotten.
The Restaurant at Justin, which earned its first Michelin star in 2022, bested any fine-dining experience I've had in Napa. I still have pictures saved from that dinner, where my fiancé and I feasted on maple-leaf duck breast, prime filet, and various veggies picked from their 26-acre farmstead.
And no visit to Paso is complete without a stop at Brown Butter Cookie Company, which has mastered the salty-sweet combo in a little treat. The hand-rolled cookies are made locally and available in a variety of flavors.
On my last visit, I grabbed a dozen bags for my family, friends, and myself, of course.
Blessed with California sunshine, Paso Robles is a great vacation year-round. I've gone in the fall, summer, and winter — where it's festively decorated for the holidays — and have always been charmed by the small-town vibes and friendly locals. (Don't miss karaoke nights at the Pine Street Saloon.)
The winemaking world is slowly starting to learn about Paso Robles, but the town itself remains a hidden gem.
For now, at least, this is California's best-kept secret.

20,2026-10-11,"More than 116,000 trees were planted in Surrey in the last financial year, according to a report
More than 800,000 new trees have been planted across Surrey over the past five years, exceeding the council's own target, according to a report.
Surrey County Council's report found 116,359 trees had been planted in the 2025/26 financial year alone.
It said the total planted between 2021 and 2026 was around 885,000, surpassing its initial target of 600,000.
However, Elmbridge Borough Council recently passed a motion calling for the future East Surrey council to update its tree strategy to consider survival rates.
It said many trees planted in the borough were dying within a year as a result of not receiving the right aftercare.
""There is little point celebrating the number of trees we plant if a significant number of them are dead a year later,"" said councillor Katerina Lusk. 
Surrey County Council has committed to 1.2 million trees being planted by 2030, representing one for every resident.
The authority's report looked at the progress of its climate change delivery programme over the past five years. 
It said the council was ""broadly on track"" to meet its 2030 net zero targets, stating that its carbon emissions have been reduced by around 60%.
The report said: ""A major success of the programme has been supporting residents to reduce energy bills and improve their homes.
""More than 1,438 low-income households received energy efficiency improvements, including insulation, solar and low-carbon heating measures.""
It also acknowledged challenges ahead in continuing to pursue decarbonisation policies. 
The programme was ""significantly affected by external market conditions, supply chain constraints and wider economic pressures"", it added.

22,2026-10-11,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Erin Keating, an executive analyst for Cox Automotive. It has been edited for length and clarity.
People should scare themselves. In public.
I tested that theory out in my mid-40s by taking five seasons of improv classes at the DC Improv Comedy Club. My goal was not to become a comedian — I wanted to take on a challenge that I could easily fail at.
And to add to the pressure, I had to perform in front of my family.
I work as an executive analyst in the auto industry at Cox Automotive. I distill data to explain the industry's market dynamics to automakers, dealership groups, lenders, and journalists. The material I cover can quickly get boring if I'm not careful.
Some of those conversations happen in small rooms, others happen in high-pressure settings: on stages in front of hundreds or thousands of people, on live television, at product unveilings, in podcasts, you name it.
I also consider myself an introvert. I didn't always feel so comfortable in front of people — and I certainly haven't always been the funniest person in the room.
At my first major corporate presentation, I couldn't stop sweating. My colleagues said I was good, but I was talking too fast. Their feedback was basically, ""You did fine. Slow down.""
I improved over time and got less sweaty when talking in front of people. There was still more I could learn.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
I decided to take the improv classes in 2018 because, honestly, I was a little bit bored. I had just left a marketing communications job and was trying to figure out what to do next.
I knew I liked talking: I was hosting a successful podcast about books, but I wanted to be better at having conversations. Loving to talk is definitely not the same as being a good conversationalist.
So, I joined my first class. I was there with a biology teacher, a German woman who was really funny, an older woman who had done a ton of onstage acting, and a handful of people (like me) who were nervous about participating. The majority of us stuck it out for our five consecutive seasons.
At the beginning, I fought against my preconception that I had to be funny.
I remember playing a game called ""Die, not today,"" where we had to mime throwing a hammer or a spear at each other. If the object was heading your way, you had to come up with how you'd survive the attack on the spot. Most people just tried to shield themselves, but one woman staged a dramatic scene where a spear nearly struck her nose. She grabbed it at the last second, saving her (improv-ed) life. It was amazing.
Improv is really about paying attention to what another person is giving you. You have to build upon the scenario they set up, a principle called ""yes, and."" You accept the premise someone introduces, and add something to it.
You also learn about status. In a scene, you might be the character driving the action. In your next, you could be supporting the other person's arc. Scenes are most interesting when you watch people respond to each other and create together through those statuses. Some of the worst scenes are when two people are just trying to compete to be the funniest.
All of that applies to work and your life more than you might realize.
Before I speak at big conferences, I talk to people in the audience to understand the energy in the room and what is on their minds. I don't like working from a script. It can get too prescriptive and keep you from responding to the people in front of you. I'm listening for the thought that is begging for a ""yes, and.""
The same goes for one-on-one conversations. When someone talks, improv has taught me to follow the unexpected and respond to what was actually said.
Those skills also matter a lot for office politics. Those classes taught me to recognize an awkward moment, assess the reaction, and apologize if I misread it. I am now comfortable saying, ""Thank you for receiving that with the intent it was given,"" when I know I don't express myself well.
The final big lesson was authenticity. Near the end of my third improv season, I jumped into a scene and used the New York Jewish-grandmother voice I'd always been able to pull off (do not ask me to do other accents — even when I think I sound like a señorita, I always revert back to that Eastern European drawl.)
I'd avoided that voice because I thought, ""This is not acting, I'm not supposed to do that."" But for that scene, it was exactly what my partner needed. My classmate asked me why I had never done it.
That voice was a breakthrough for me. I realized I could bring my whole self to that stage.
Human skills are becoming more important, especially as AI changes how we work.
Sure, technical skills will remain important. But I think people who can listen, relate to others, read a room, and recover gracefully from an uncomfortable moment will have a real advantage.
Those are human skills that you can't fake by memorizing a script.
So, I suggest doing the thing that scares you: get onstage, take a class, give the speech. You may sweat, and you may talk too fast. But you'll get through it — and learn something about how you show up for the people around you.
I plan to keep testing that theory myself. My first stand-up comedy class starts on Tuesday.
And I'm terrified.
No comments right now, check back later.

24,2026-10-11,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Investing in a speaker system should be an exciting adventure for you and yours. There are numerous audio manufacturers on the market, and most of these brands are renowned for more than one product or platform. But before you blow the dust off your old CD or vinyl record collection, you'll want to lock down what type of speakers you plan on investing in: powered or passive. While one isn't necessarily better than the other, the passive speaker route will probably be the pricier option for a first-time speaker owner. Why, you may be wondering? Because you still have to buy all the stuff that sends audio to your speakers.
Passive speakers aren't amplified in any way, shape, or form — it's up to you to provide the amplification and audio signals. That means factoring in the cost of even a relatively basic two-channel stereo amplifier and speaker wire, at least to start. You'll also need to make sure that the amp you purchase has all the audio connections you're looking for (e.g., RCA, phono, digital optical, etc.). The $250 Sony STR-DH190 is a top-rated stereo receiver that's been on the market for years, and it's particularly great for listening to music. 
It outputs up to 100 watts per channel and includes several audio outputs; it even offers Bluetooth support for wireless playback from a phone or laptop. Fortunately, you can grab a spool of speaker wire for as little as $10 (thanks, Amazon Basics!), but once your state's sales tax is figured in, you'll be spending close to $300 for power and audio — that's before you've even selected a pair of passive speakers.
While you can certainly find a used or refurbished pair of passive speakers for a rock-bottom price, you shouldn't sacrifice quality for cost. Fortunately, brands like Polk Audio and Klipsch make great pairs of bookshelf speakers — a common type of passive speaker — that won't break the bank. In most cases, though, you'll still be spending close to $300 for a decent pair (although we did find these $180 Polk Audio monitors).
Now, you could definitely end up spending $300 or more for a pair of powered speakers, too; the difference is that you won't absolutely need to invest in an external amp or AV receiver. Most powered speakers include several audio inputs, and some of the more advanced models can even be connected to Wi-Fi, allowing you to access platforms like Spotify or Tidal directly from the speaker — no phone required. Closed ecosystems like Sonos or Bose's Lifestyle lineup are a couple of fan favorites for networking, but we're also big fans of the $600 Edifier S1000W.
The latter can be interfaced with Wi-Fi or Bluetooth and supports features like AirPlay 2, as well as Spotify and Tidal Connect. The combo is even outfitted with RCA inputs if you want to hook up a record player, CD carousel, or other analog audio equipment. Yes, $600 is a big sticker price, but remember: If you don't already own a source of amplification, a passive speaker setup may end up costing the same, if not more."
