27,2026-10-11,"my scruples
There is a popular idea that substantial wealth belongs mainly to entrepreneurs. We celebrate founders, investors and business owners so loudly that a career person can begin to believe that employment is merely a way to pay bills, while ownership is the only credible path to prosperity.
I do not believe that is true. I have watched several of my friends build remarkable careers through traditional nine-to-five employment. Some began with excellent university results, which made it easier for them to enter reputable organizations. Others did not have exceptional grades, but they developed qualities that eventually became more important than the classification written on their certificates. Over time, I noticed that the strongest people in both categories began to look alike.
They had grit. They did not merely allow work to happen to them; they made things happen at work. They became known for solving problems, learning quickly and producing results. They used the energy of their early career years to build competence and credibility. They became useful beyond the boundaries of their departments. They continued their education, formally and informally. Most importantly, they did not treat their salaries only as money to spend. They converted increasing income into assets. That last point is where a successful career begins to become wealth.
A good career is an engine, not the destination
Your career can be one of the most reliable wealth-building engines available to you. It gives you income, training, relationships, credibility, exposure and, in some cases, pensions, bonuses, equity and other benefits. However, an engine is valuable only when its output is directed somewhere.
A high income is not the same thing as wealth. Income is what flows to you for your work. Wealth is what remains under your ownership and continues to serve you after the salary has been paid.
This distinction explains why somebody can earn an impressive salary for twenty years and still remain financially fragile. If every increase in income creates an equal increase in lifestyle, the person may look wealthier without becoming wealthier. A larger house, a newer car and more expensive holidays can all be enjoyable, but they do not automatically create financial independence.
Morgan Housel writes in The Psychology of Money that “wealth is what you don’t see.” The visible car may have been financed; the invisible portfolio may be creating genuine security. Wealth is often the money that was not converted into an immediate display.
So the career person has two related assignments. The first is to increase the value of their work. The second is to retain and invest a meaningful part of the value they receive.
Grades can open the first door, but they cannot do the job for you
Many of the successful career people I know had good grades. That mattered, particularly at the beginning. Strong academic results can help a young graduate pass screening criteria, enter a respected graduate programme or receive an opportunity to be interviewed. At that stage, employers possess limited evidence about what you can do, so your academic record becomes one of the signals available to them.
The broader data also shows that education can improve earning prospects, although the result differs by country, discipline, institution and individual. A World Bank review estimated that the average private global return associated with an additional year of schooling was about 9% annually. In the United States, 2025 data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics showed median weekly earnings of $1,578 for workers with a bachelor’s degree, compared with $966 for people whose highest qualification was a high-school diploma. The respective unemployment rates were 2.8% and 4.3%.
Those figures describe population-level associations; they do not promise that every degree will be profitable or that education alone causes the entire difference. Family background, occupation, location, ability and opportunity also matter. Still, the evidence supports a sensible conclusion: education can expand access and earning capacity.
But after the door opens, performance begins to replace potential. Your employer will not continue to reward the grade you received five years ago if you cannot solve present problems. The graduate who began with an ordinary result but becomes dependable, commercially aware and excellent at execution can overtake the classmate whose academic distinction became an excuse to stop learning. Grades can introduce you. Results must keep you in the room.
Make things happen at work
The career people who progress fastest rarely behave as though their job description is the outer limit of their contribution. They understand their responsibilities, but they also pay attention to what the organization is trying to achieve.
When they encounter an obstacle, their first instinct is not to explain why the task is impossible. They ask what can be changed, who needs to be involved and what information is missing. When a process breaks between two departments, they do not simply say, “That is not my job.” They help the right people find one another and move the work forward.
This does not mean becoming the unpaid solution to every problem or allowing an organization to exploit you. Boundaries matter. It means developing an ownership mentality: understanding that your value is connected to outcomes, not merely to activity.
Cal Newport describes this in So Good They Can’t Ignore You through the idea of “career capital”—the rare and valuable skills that give a person greater control and opportunity. Career capital is built when you do difficult work well, learn what the market values and accumulate proof that you can create results.
Early in your career, this may require extraordinary effort. You may need to prepare more deeply, volunteer for difficult assignments and spend time learning the business beyond your immediate role. Youthful energy is an asset. Use it to build capabilities that will continue paying you when energy alone is no longer a competitive advantage.
The goal is not to become permanently overworked. The goal is to compress learning into your early years and emerge with judgment, relationships and a record of delivery.
Become important, but do not try to become indispensable
There is a difference between being valuable and making yourself a bottleneck. The strongest employees become important because people trust their judgment and execution. They are invited into consequential discussions. Colleagues across the organization know that they understand the issues and will follow through. Their absence is noticed because their contribution matters.
However, an employee who hides information, refuses to document processes or prevents others from learning may appear indispensable while actually limiting their own advancement. If nobody else can perform your current work, management may hesitate to move you into a larger role.
Real value includes the ability to build systems, transfer knowledge and make other people effective. You should aim to leave every role stronger than you found it. Train someone. Document the process. Improve the dashboard. Clarify the decision rights. Develop a successor.
Your value should come from your capacity to solve increasingly important problems, not from keeping the organization dependent on you for one recurring task.
Be useful across the organization
One quality I have observed repeatedly is cross-functional usefulness. High performers do not remain mentally imprisoned inside their departments. A finance professional learns how the product creates value. A product manager understands sales objections. A salesperson learns enough about operations to avoid making promises the company cannot fulfil. An HR leader understands revenue, cost and the operating model.
This does not require interfering in everybody’s work; it requires curiosity about how the parts connect.
Organizations reward people who can see the whole system because senior leadership is fundamentally cross-functional. The higher you rise, the less sufficient narrow expertise becomes. You must understand trade-offs between customers, people, capital, technology, risk and time.
Build relationships before you need favours. Offer thoughtful help. Ask intelligent questions. Learn the language of other functions. When you solve a problem, understand who benefited and how the result affected the wider business.
Your network inside an organization is not merely a social circle. It is an information and execution network. It helps you see opportunities earlier, coordinate faster and build a reputation beyond the person who signs your appraisal.
Become a person who produces evidence
Hard work matters, but undocumented work is easy to forget. Career people should develop the habit of translating effort into outcomes. Do not merely say that you supported a project. Record what changed because you were involved. Did revenue increase? Did costs decline? Did processing time improve? Did customer complaints fall? Did the company enter a new market, reduce risk, retain important employees or deliver a project ahead of schedule?
Keep a private record of your work that contains no confidential company information but captures your responsibilities, decisions, measurable outcomes and lessons. Update it monthly rather than trying to reconstruct several years of contribution when you need a promotion or a new role.
Where direct financial measures are not available, use responsible operational evidence: turnaround time, adoption, quality, reliability, customer satisfaction, team capability or risk reduction. Do not invent precision. Explain the context and identify your actual contribution rather than claiming the entire team’s achievement.
This evidence strengthens your CV, promotion case and salary negotiation. It also helps you understand which of your skills the market values most.
Learn faster than your role changes
The people I have watched build strong careers are learners. Many pursued second and third degrees. They added qualifications, studied new areas and prepared themselves for responsibilities beyond their current positions.
Continuous education is important, but every additional degree should answer a serious question: What capability, access or earning opportunity will this education create?
Do not collect credentials merely because progress feels easier to measure in a classroom than in the market. Consider the tuition, time, lost income and alternative ways of acquiring the same capability. A master’s degree may be an excellent investment when it is required for advancement, provides valuable technical depth, changes geography or network, or creates entry into a new profession. In another situation, a certification, apprenticeship, project or six months of focused practice may produce a better return.
Education should increase capacity, not merely decorate a résumé. Learning also extends beyond formal programmes. Read books. Study your industry. Follow changes in technology and regulation. Ask senior people how they make decisions. Volunteer for assignments that expose you to budgets, customers and strategy. Learn to write clearly, present persuasively, analyse data and manage people. Artificial intelligence will change many tasks, but people who can define problems, exercise judgment and use new tools responsibly will remain valuable. The qualification may expire in relevance; the capacity to learn does not.
Move strategically, not emotionally
Loyalty is valuable, but passivity is expensive. There are times when a person must leave an organization to expand their responsibility, earning potential or exposure. There are also times when remaining is wiser because the role continues to provide unusual learning, sponsorship or long-term upside.
Do not move merely because a new salary is slightly higher. Compare the total opportunity: compensation, pension, equity, health benefits, flexibility, manager quality, brand value, learning, geography, job security and future progression. A role that pays more today may weaken your career capital; a role with apparently modest compensation may put you near outstanding leaders and valuable problems.
At the same time, do not remain indefinitely in a place where your contribution is consistently undervalued and the learning has stopped. Test your market value periodically. Build relationships outside your employer before you need another job. Understand what comparable roles pay, and negotiate using evidence rather than resentment.
Never make the company your entire identity. Organizations change strategy. Managers leave. Markets contract. Roles are restructured. Your security comes partly from employment, but it also comes from portable competence, reputation, relationships and financial reserves.
Convert salary into ownership
Career achievement creates income; wealth requires ownership.
The basic equation is straightforward:
Income − consumption = capital available for saving and investment. That capital can be used to build an emergency reserve, reduce expensive debt and acquire diversified assets appropriate to your goals and risk capacity. Depending on your country and circumstances, those assets might include regulated funds, pension accounts, government securities, public equities, property or carefully evaluated private businesses. Every option carries different risks, costs, liquidity and tax implications.
The principle is more important than any single product: own assets that can grow or produce income without requiring every future hour of your labour.
The SEC’s Investor.gov guidance summarizes the long-term mechanism as regular investing plus time. It suggests consistently investing a percentage of income or a fixed affordable amount, increasing contributions as income rises and using diversification to reduce concentration risk.
For a career person, automation is powerful. Arrange for saving and investment to occur immediately after payday, before discretionary spending expands to consume the balance. If you receive a bonus, decide the investment allocation before the money arrives. When you earn a promotion, increase your investment contribution before upgrading your lifestyle.
Your savings rate matters, particularly in the early years. Investment returns cannot compensate for the absence of capital. The first task is to create a gap between earnings and spending; the second is to put that gap to productive work.
Avoid lifestyle inflation
One of the greatest threats to career wealth is the belief that every visible aspect of life must rise at the same speed as income.
A promotion arrives, and immediately the car must change, the rent must increase, the wardrobe must expand and the social expectations must be upgraded. Soon the new salary feels as tight as the old one. The person has more status but no more freedom.
This does not mean refusing to enjoy the fruit of your work. Money should improve life. The question is whether enjoyment is deliberate or automatic. If each salary increase is divided intentionally among investing, giving, important goals and lifestyle, you can enjoy progress without sacrificing the future.
Beware especially of high-interest consumer debt. The SEC notes that interest on such debt can outweigh the return available from most investments. Paying down expensive debt may therefore be one of the most reliable improvements available to your financial position.
Privacy can also protect wealth. You do not need to display every promotion or disclose every increase in income. A quieter life makes it easier to choose investments based on purpose rather than social pressure.
Build more than one form of capital
Money is only one kind of capital. A durable career produces several:
These forms of capital reinforce one another. Skill produces results. Results build reputation. Reputation attracts relationships and opportunities. Opportunities increase income. Disciplined ownership converts income into financial capital. Financial strength then gives you more freedom to choose meaningful work, negotiate well, survive transitions or start something later if that becomes your path.
Trust may be the most important bridge between them. Keep your word. Do not manipulate figures. Give credit to colleagues. Protect confidential information. Admit mistakes early. A person with strong competence and weak character eventually places every other form of capital at risk.
Let your job finance your freedom
Your salary should do more than maintain your current life. It should gradually purchase options for your future.
Build an emergency fund so that one difficult month does not force you into destructive debt. Protect yourself against risks that could erase years of progress. Contribute to appropriate retirement or pension arrangements. Invest consistently and diversify sensibly. Review fees, inflation, taxes and currency exposure, particularly if your future obligations may exist in more than one country or currency.
Do not chase returns you do not understand. Be cautious when a colleague presents a “guaranteed” opportunity that promises unusual profit with little or no risk. Career people are attractive targets for bad investments because they receive predictable income but may not have time to examine every proposition. Your diligence at work should extend to your money.
If you eventually invest in a business or begin a side venture, treat it professionally. Check whether your employment contract permits it. Separate personal and business accounts. Understand the possibility of loss. Do not damage the primary engine that is currently financing your future.
A practical career-to-wealth system
You can think of the process in five stages. First, enter well. Use your grades, projects, relationships and preparation to secure the best learning environment available to you. If your grades are not strong, build other credible evidence of ability.
Second, become excellent. Learn quickly, take ownership, solve problems and produce measurable results. Become useful across functions without becoming a bottleneck.
Third, increase your earning power. Acquire relevant education, negotiate intelligently and move when the total opportunity justifies it. Build rare, valuable and portable capabilities.
Fourth, create a surplus. Control lifestyle inflation, eliminate high-interest debt and protect the difference between what you earn and what you spend.
Fifth, convert the surplus into assets. Automate regular saving and investing, diversify appropriately, manage risk and allow time to compound your ownership.
Repeat the cycle. As your competence grows, your income should have the opportunity to grow. As your income grows, your investment contribution should grow. As your assets grow, your dependence on the next salary should gradually decline. That is how a career becomes a wealth-building system.
Become a star player—and an owner
The most successful career people I know did not spend their early years waiting to be discovered. They made themselves useful; they learned. They delivered. They went above and beyond when it mattered, built relationships across the organization and developed the confidence of people who could entrust them with larger responsibilities.
They became star players. But the final lesson is that a star player must still become an owner. You may not own the company where you work, although employee equity can sometimes provide that opportunity. You can still own your savings, investments, pension, property, intellectual assets and future choices.
Do not despise the nine-to-five. A good job can give you access to people, problems and training that would be expensive to obtain alone. It can introduce you to industries, teach you discipline and provide the capital with which you build your future.
Work diligently, but do not merely work for money. Make the money you have earned begin to work for you.
Your grades may open the first door. Your grit may move you through the building. Your results may take you to the highest floors. But it is the discipline of turning income into ownership that allows the career you built to become wealth that lasts.
References and further reading
This article provides general education, not individualized investment, tax or legal advice. Returns are not guaranteed, and the suitability of any investment depends on the reader’s circumstances.

29,2026-10-11,"Jolly wants to help make your life easier — and be cute while doing it. The mascot for Meta's Muse AI is a slightly rotund, plush creature with rosy cheeks, big eyes, and a toothless grin. It can wear clothes or little headphones when he's ""working"" on tasks for you.
As someone who once went through some (embarrassingly) elaborate hoops to acquire a Labubu, I must admit that I was immediately enamored by the creature's debut. I'm not alone — much ink has been spilled on how cute the little avatar is. Jon Stewart even devoted a whole segment to debating the cuddly guy's true intentions.
Jolly joins a growing group of creatures vying for the hearts and minds of potential AI users. There are the round, mouthless dots of OpenAI, the fluffy Genspark lobster, and Grok users have adopted a little teal alien named ""Groklet."" All adorable, all plushy, and all debuting as the general populace was starting to worry that AI would maybe kill us all.
The idea behind Jolly and the rest of the mascots is simple: make us forget about all that AI doom-and-gloom and learn to love it. They play on our most human, primal instincts, distancing us from the fact that they represent something distinctly non-human. This round, fun little creature is your friend! He's going to work on your behalf! The fuzzy little charms also tap into our sense of nostalgia. Everyone I spoke to for this piece immediately had a connection to the Muse avatar: Some said it reminded them of a Tamagotchi, or MySpace avatars, or Baymax, the lovable robot from Big Hero 6.
""Seeing something cute stimulates the pleasure centers in your brain before you've even had a chance to think about it,"" Joshua Paul Dale, the founder of the niche academic field ""Cute Studies,"" and a professor at Chuo University in Tokyo, tells me. ""You're predisposed to act socially towards something that you find cute.""
It's not the first time that cuteness has arisen in response to a collective sense of unease. In the past, the trait has been wielded as a comforting salve, a PR pivot, or to put a smiling face on something more complex. Cute has proven to be a powerful tool, and AI companies hope they can ride that wave into America's hearts — and computers.
The most visible — and successful — use of cute culture was in Japan following post-World War II. Fluffy mascots, manga, and stationery were used to shift the narrative about the country away from its aggressive, imperial history toward one of an approachable cultural destination. This shift is best personified by Hello Kitty, the now-ubiquitous cat-like mascot created in 1974 as an emblem of kawaii, the Japanese term for cute. Designed to appeal to Japanese women with newfound spending power — and their children — the cat avatar has since become a literal cultural emissary, bringing comfort to Japan's citizens and promoting the country's image elsewhere.
Beyond Japan, the deployment of cute as a culture-shaping force seems to repeat across the years. Gen Zers embraced Y2K nostalgia during the pandemic — a marked contrast to their Zoom-first era. Another 2020 hit was the video game ""Animal Crossing: New Horizons,"" as gamers stuck at home clamored for the coziness of tending to animated life. Cultural theorist Sianne Ngai calls cuteness ""an aestheticization of powerlessness"" — for a consumer to feel something is cute, they need to feel they can wield power over it, giving them a sense of calm control. On the grim end, cuteness was used by Purdue Pharmaceuticals in its campaign for OxyContin: the company handed out plush toys to practitioners whom they wanted to convince to prescribe the drug. The Drug Enforcement Administration later said the tactic was ""an indicator of Purdue's aggressive and inappropriate marketing.""
AI companies clearly appreciate the persistent power of cuteness. It's easier for you to ask a question to your little buddy than a gray box — and why not buy a few more tokens so Jolly can book your next vacation or sift through your finances. For University of Iowa business analytics professor Thiago Serra, the branding has echoes of Tesla. Rather than consumers feeling like they have to take their eco-conscious vitamins and pony up for an electric car, Tesla made cars that looked and felt good — the environmental thing was a nice added bonus. For agents, adding a mascot shifts your thinking from the scary AI that might kill you or take your job to a cute buddy that can be helpful in your personal life.
Rebecca Kowalewicz, a digital marketing VP and millennial-Gen-X-cusper in Philadelphia, dabbled with a few AI platforms, but none were as fun as Muse. Jolly played on her sense of bygone days, reminding her of choosing a MySpace avatar. And Kowalewicz went one step further: She fed the software a photo of her late dog, Doctor Watson — a rescued coonhound pointer mix — and it quickly generated an animated version of him to replace Jolly. Now, Doctor Watson smiles at her, types, and moves his head. She's so endeared to the agent that she feels more inclined to stick with it.
""It's almost like having a work bestie,"" she says, except it won't push back and ""you don't have to take it out to lunch.""
In addition to personifying inanimate ideas or technologies, coziness and cuteness can also psychologically soothe us in uncertain times. Take Japan: already steeped in postwar cuteness, the country saw a renewed boom in coziness after its property bubble burst in 1990. People said it felt healing, Dale tells me, temporarily easing their woes amid financial and job-market upheaval. Similarly, agents can endear themselves to users by solving a task that causes dismay — with a little smile.
That's been the case for edtech consultant Tonja Brunn, a Gen Xer who — like many white-collar Americans — is on the job hunt. That search is how she and ""Monkey,"" her Muse agent, first ""connected."" Monkey has ""revolutionized the job search"" for Brunn, and she's fallen in love with how cute her newest ""employee"" is. Seasonally appropriate, Monkey now wears a little shirt with a pumpkin and two cherubic ghosts.
A friendly avatar helping you plug away at all of the worst parts of the job search is the latest attempt by AI companies to target a new segment of customers: Everyday folks who were turned off by the early, austere chatbots and spooked by the rush of AI doomer news.
When Cole Benefield, the 25-year-old founder of Servo, an agentic firm for service businesses, first saw the Labubu-esque creature, he thought it was silly. Then he saw the promotional materials for Jolly, happily typing away at a keyboard. The business owners he works with probably wouldn't be too amused or charmed by the fuzzy guy. But the greater mass appeal of Jolly revealed something: While other AI firms signal their tech-industry bona fides with clever model names and sterile interfaces, Meta's years of honed consumer prowess have allowed it to reach a wider swath of consumers. Benefield says Meta's use of Jolly means non-technical users never have to think about what's happening under the hood.

""Their goal is to make it to where they never even have to think about that,"" he says.
Kaitlyn Murphy, a 26-year-old tech publicist in Massachusetts, questions how much AI agents could improve her day-to-day life — especially given the access they require. Despite the skepticism, when she first saw Jolly, she felt tempted to make her own version.
""I love the idea that I could watch it typing while I'm sending it on a task. I actually do think it's really cute,"" she said. The Jolly aesthetic could definitely appeal to cute-obsessed Gen Z, she said, but it would have to go hand in hand with a meaningful attempt to address the larger AI doom-and-gloom concerns many in her generation still hold.
The fact, though, that someone like Murphy is swayed shows the potency of personifying these agents. They also come at a time of tanking consumer sentiment and increasing worry about the economy, making the idea of a pinchable, smiling face even more enticing.
""I do have psychologist colleagues who are speculating that Japan might've hit peak cuteness, that people are being overstimulated by it, and that's certainly possible,"" Dale says. ""But my answer to that is that outside Japan, it's only getting started.""

30,2026-10-11,"File photo of gold prospectors in northern Sudan
At least 72 civilians who had been prospecting for gold in a border region of Sudan have been killed in air strikes, a regional rights group says.
Emergency Lawyers, based in the Sudanese capital, Khartoum, said jets targeted the so-called artisanal miners at a popular gold mining sites in Sudan's River Nile state on Saturday.
Neither the Egyptian government nor the de facto Sudanese authorities have so far commented. 
Cairo has previously faced accusations of deadly strikes on gold prospectors as part of a crackdown on illicit activity along its southern border.
Emergency Lawyers noted the previous alleged attacks and said they represented not only a violation of Sudan's sovereign territory but demonstrated the government's lack of control over the region.
""The deliberate targeting of unarmed civilians violates international humanitarian law and the right to life,"" it said in a statement on Sunday. ""These acts may constitute war crimes.""
It noted that its death toll was provisional and could rise. 
The Paris-based Sudan Tribune reported that the death toll could far exceed the number reported by Emergency Lawyers, citing a survivor of Saturday's attack who said many more people had been injured.
Eyewitnesses said the attack began around 06:00 local time (04:00 GMT), with the aircraft firing more than five missiles directly at the miners' camps, destroying them and excavation pits with people still inside, the newspaper wrote.
The Sudan Tribune reported claims in June that Egyptian forces had used drones and artillery to target artisanal mining sites in the same region, killing several, after sporadic incidents of Egyptian border guards of burning equipment, torching temporary shelters, and firing live ammunition to disperse mine workers.
Days later, Egypt's border agency announced that it and the military had begun a sweeping security campaign along the border to foil illegal migration, drug smuggling and illegal mining.
At the same time, Sudan's de facto leader announced an investigation into the alleged strikes, which were said to have killed dozens.
Gold lies at the core of the border tensions. 
Sudan's ongoing civil war has led to a surge in artisanal mining - an often risky practice conducted by civilians with rudimentary tools and without official permission - particularly in remote areas near its borders. 
Gold-rich areas such as those in River Nile state lie along porous borders in terrain historically associated with cross-border smuggling routes, with artisanally mined gold potentially feeding into those channels. 
Egypt's border force said in June that it had seized 86 tonnes of gold ore along with amateur prospecting equipment.
Artisanal mining has also reportedly continued in Sudan's conflict zones and smuggled gold may be a source of income for armed insurgent groups.
This has raised concerns of illicit Sudanese gold undermining the legitimate Egyptian gold market.

‌Billionaire entrepreneur Elon Musk has said he would price his Starlink satellite internet service affordably ⁠in India to take ⁠on Mukesh Ambani’s low-cost Reliance Jio, escalating his attacks on the rival tycoon days before Jio’s parent launches the country’s largest-ever initial public offering (IPO).
Jio Platforms, ⁠which owns the telecom arm, is targeting a valuation of $106bn in the offering. Much like SpaceX-owned Starlink, Jio’s satellite venture is also awaiting final ⁠security clearance to launch.




Cheap data is the playbook that built Jio in India, and the one Musk has used in markets like Kenya to unsettle local telecom players. SpaceX now plans to enter the mobile carrier market itself, starting in ‌the United States.
Musk did not specify prices for India, where mobile data costs about rupees 7.74 ($0.08) per gigabyte, among the lowest in the world.
“Obviously, if Starlink is too expensive in India, no one will buy it, so SpaceX will need to make pricing affordable,” he said in a post on his social media platform X on Saturday.
“The reality is that ⁠competition would greatly benefit the average citizen in India by ⁠giving them more choices, as it always does. Ambani’s profits will be lower, but he is not going out of business,” Musk added.
 

 
Musk’s remarks were the latest in ⁠a series of X posts in which he accused Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government and Ambani of stalling Starlink’s entry ⁠into one of the world’s biggest internet markets to ⁠favour market leader Jio.
The posts have marked a sharp turn for Musk, who has long enjoyed a warm public rapport with Modi.
India’s government has denied any bias in its approval process, ‌noting that the three satellite ventures seeking to launch, including Starlink’s and Jio’s, are all awaiting final security clearance.
Late on Friday, Musk taunted Ambani directly, addressing ‌him as “Prime ‌Minister Ambani” and asking whether he would “consider allowing Starlink to compete”.
Reliance has not responded to Musk’s remarks.

Peter 
11 October 2026


Weekly poll Samsung

Last year, the global tablet market was up 8.5% in Q1 as people started to replace devices they bought during the pandemic. Apple led the quarter with 13.7 million units shipped, up 14.0%, while Samsung was around half that with 6.6 million units shipped, down 5.2%. Samsung usually starts shipping its new flagship tablets in late Q3 (the Tab S11 models were available in September) or early Q4 (the Tab S12 series launched earlier this week), so shipments declining by 5.2% in Q1 was a bad look for the Tab S11 slates.
So, now our attention turns to the Samsung Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra – the Ultra series is the only Tab S model that gets regular yearly upgrades. That said, they are not particularly exciting – a quick comparison with the Tab S11 Ultra shows that Samsung upgraded the chipset, dropped the base 128GB version (which was cheaper and was actually usable since the tablets have microSD slots) and found new shades of gray with which to paint the slate.
And the chipset isn’t even the latest from MediaTek. Instead, Samsung picked last year’s Dimensity 9500. According to official numbers, the recently unveiled Dimensity 9600 Pro is faster and significantly more power efficient than the 9500 (up to 61% more efficient in multi-core tests). But perhaps the 9600 Pro wasn’t ready in time for the Tab S12 series launch.
The Dimensity 9500 is no slouch – officially, its Arm C1-based CPU promises a great 32% improvement in single-core and 17% improvement in multi-core performance compared to the older Tab S11 Ultra chipset with amazing efficiency gains of 55% on average during peak load. The G1-Ultra GPU is 33% faster and 42% more efficient.



Samsung Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra
The Tab S11 Ultra already had great battery life and while Samsung kept the battery capacity at the same level, 11,600mAh, we were hoping for some gains from the chipset. However, the new model posted a nearly identical result to its predecessor – for more details, read our review of the Tab S12 Ultra. That’s still a great result, to be fair.
And that’s it for the Samsung Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra upgrades – just the chipset. The slate costs $1,400 / €1,540 / ₹150,000 for the base 12/256GB model. But before we get to the polls, we have to look at the other new model.
The Samsung Galaxy Tab S12+ replaces the two-year-old Tab S10+ due to Samsung’s leapfrogging release cadence for the vanilla and plus tablets. That means a bigger performance leap over the Dimensity 9300+ that was inside the 2024 plus tablet. However, even two years of accumulated upgrades don’t amount to much besides the chipset.




Samsung Galaxy Tab S12+
The plus models have had a 12.4” display since the line was introduced back in 2020 with the Tab S6+. This one goes a bit larger, but only marginally so – it has a 12.6” display, which works out to around 3% more surface area.
Additionally, the battery is bigger, but again only marginally so – it has 10,600mAh capacity, up from 10,090mAh. That’s not majorly impressive at a time when some phones have 11,000mAh batteries. Strangely, Samsung decided to omit the 8MP ultra-wide camera that was on the Tab S10+. We don’t think it matters much on a tablet, but it’s worth noting.
The Samsung Galaxy Tab S12+ starts at $1,200 / €1,300 / ₹130,000. An 11” iPad Pro also starts at $1,200 and that one has an Apple M5 chipset and a tandem OLED display. It would have been a killer tablet if it ran macOS, but even iPadOS has good multitasking skills these days. The 13” iPad Pro starts at $1,500, by the way, and note that it has a smaller display than the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra, which has a 14.6” panel.
Alright, time to vote starting with the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra. Do you have a use-case for a large flagship tablet and is the Tab S12 Ultra that tablet?
What about the Galaxy Tab S12+? That one is a bit cheaper while offering the same performance and features – but wouldn’t you pay extra to get the Ultra?
Rocking my oneplus pad 3. Great tablet for casual usage. You get super long battery life and great performance. Also speakers are awesome. But there are some cons too. No oled screen. No vibration. No sim card slot. And only 3 major updates. Overall ...
No I look for life variant tablet Samsung Is am use tablet only for media consumption streaming you tube maybe simple browsing
MicroSDXC card slot support is ironically the biggest reasons Samsung Galaxy Tab S series  (unlike their phones where it has been axed since S21 and now A series phone is also doing the same) always have been the major selling points on their tablets...

Apple Smart home Various

The smart streaming box/stick market is a bit weird for sure. There are all sorts of options out there, but each with distinct problems and limitations. Generic Android boxes are plentiful and can be powerful, but often offer a suboptimal software experience and format and technology support. There are cheap and easily available reputable options out there like Amazon's Firestick line, though that might be getting even more locked down with the move to Vega OS. Walmart has its ""onn"" line, but it is kind of US-only. Anyone actually serious about this device either swears by the Nvidia Shield TV Pro, dating back to 2019 or an Apple TV. The latter does have more recent iterations, like the 3rd-generation Apple TV 4K from 2022.
We already know that Apple is gearing up to unveil a whole bunch of new smart home devices on October 13 and apparently that will include at least one Apple TV device. This has been rumored already but is now further confirmed by the fact that Apple recently relegated the original Apple TV 4K from 2017 as a ""vintage"" device. In Apple vernacular, ""vintage"" means over five years have passed since a product stopped being available for sale, while there is also ""obsolete"", which means over seven years have passed since sales discontinued. Obsolete devices are pretty much impossible to service officially, while vintage ones are difficult, but still possible.
What does that mean if you own a 2017 Apple TV 4K? Most likely you will be able to continue using it just fine if it meets your needs and if it is working properly, that is. If the latter is not true and you have one sitting in a drawer for some reason, you might want to get it to an Apple service center soon. Or, of course, you could just wait and get whatever new Apple TV is released in a few days.

      Commentary
  



      Commentary
  

SGX says it should not be faulted if stocks have poor liquidity. It has a point, but the bourse can do more, says former editor and financial journalist Ven Sreenivasan.
FILE PHOTO: Singapore Exchange's (SGX) logo is seen in the central business district, Singapore, April 7, 2020. REUTERS/Edgar Su/File Photo/File Photo

       This audio is generated by an AI tool.
    
SINGAPORE: Singapore Exchange (SGX) chairman Koh Boon Hwee stirred up some chatter recently with his comments on liquidity in the local stock market.
Speaking at an event on Oct 1, he said small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) should not depend on SGX to boost their valuations, as the bourse does not create liquidity nor demand and supply.
Instead, listed firms should proactively communicate their strategies so that investors can better understand their businesses and growth prospects.
“The exchange can’t do that for you, neither can it guarantee that your counter will be liquid,” Mr Koh said.

These comments must have been painful for the many small and mid-cap stocks on SGX that continue to see thin trading despite the broader gains in the local market.
The benchmark Straits Times Index (STI) has surged from around 3,800 at the start of 2025 to well over 5,000 points today. Yet, this has been driven largely by the three local banks, which together account for more than half of the index’s weightage.
This masks the perennial issue that many small and mid-caps, especially those with market capitalisation of below S$200 million (US$156 million), remain rangebound and thinly traded.
Poor liquidity prevents proper price discovery, which in turn makes it difficult for these companies to use their shares as currency to raise capital or engage in meaningful merger and acquisition activity.
Mr Koh is right to say that companies have to take responsibility for raising market awareness of their worth. Good investor relations and communications can yield results but for many SMEs that have put in significant effort such as by holding detailed investor engagement exercises, their shares can still be thinly traded for days at times. Companies like ISOTeam, Nordic Group and Sasseur REIT come to mind.
The bourse has done its part by profiling several of these companies through investor education activities, including seminars.
Wider market revitalisation measures are also under way, such as the S$6.5 billion Equity Market Development Programme (EQDP) which have brought on a burst of liquidity in the local market. Two weeks ago, the Monetary Authority of Singapore appointed the third batch of five asset managers under the programme, placing a total of S$1.45 billion with the likes of Amundi and Franklin Templeton.
But while the EQDP has created primary demand for initial public offerings and share placements, it does not seem to have done much for secondary trading and liquidity on SGX.
Maybank, in an Oct 2 report, noted that while S$5.4 billion of the EQDP funds has since been allocated, it has “yet to meaningfully appear as institutional buying in the market”.
Year to date, the market’s outperformance has been limited to large-cap financials, which are up 42 per cent, while the rest of the STI is flat. The iEdge Singapore Next 50 index, which is SGX’s new mid-cap index, is 12 per cent higher, but still significantly trails the financials, the report said.
In his recent comments, SGX’s Mr Koh also made the point that privatisation could be an option for those whose shares are thinly traded. This echoed what he wrote in his annual letter to SGX shareholders days before that “privatisation is not a tragedy but a rational and often healthy outcome”.
To be sure for such firms, the benefits of going private outweigh the gains of remaining listed, which would involve forking out large compliance costs and management efforts to meet the regulatory requirements.
While there may be no shame in delisting, it should not be a default option.
A listing still carries some prestige value, especially for companies engaged in global business. It raises visibility of the company and lends credibility in the financial and investor arena.
It also enables a company to raise capital more efficiently for acquisitions and strategic transactions. Listed shares can also be valuable for employee reward, remuneration and retention.
In short, the true challenge is to lift overall secondary trading liquidity in SMEs on the SGX, and more can be done.
One possibility is to remove SGX clearing and trading fees to reduce friction costs for proprietary traders and market makers. Encouraging and attracting proprietary trading and market making to the SMEs can help improve the bid–ask spreads – the difference between the highest price a buyer is ready to pay and the lowest price a seller is willing to accept – and improve liquidity and trading activity.
Another could be to encourage SGX member brokerage firms to hire in-house proprietary traders who trade using a firm’s own capital, rather than client funds, and can provide trading liquidity to the market, as was the case during the heydays of the market prior to the 2013 penny stock crash. To be sure, some level of regulatory policing might be needed to prevent the creation of a false market.
Lastly, reporting templates in SGX announcements could be created for half and full-year results where companies can provide more explicit and detailed forward earnings guidance and outlook. These fields in the template can be made compulsory.
Delisting may make sense. But before making this the default way out, other options should be looked at. Investors deserve that much, at the very least.
Ven Sreenivasan is a former editor and journalist who has covered financial markets, economic and corporate news and aviation for more than 30 years.

36,2026-10-11,"The Digital Asset Market Clarity Act’s failure to advance in the Senate has shifted the next phase of U.S. crypto regulation to the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), opening prospects for more trading and investment to move onchain while leaving a central question unresolved: how much certainty can regulators deliver without Congress?
Interviews with senior executives and legal experts suggest broad agreement that regulation will keep moving. Their expectations diverge over whether agency action can become a durable foundation or leave businesses adapting to rules vulnerable to political and legal change.
Lev Breydo, an assistant professor of law at William & Mary Law School, said Clarity exposed divisions within the industry, from ethics provisions to the yield fight with community banks.
“A coalition that looked unified against Gensler found out definitions create winners and losers,” he said.
Despite the setback, agencies are moving quickly to fill the regulatory gap.
The SEC’s five-year “Innovation Exemption” introduced September 17 lets qualifying venues trade tokenized U.S. stocks through blockchain liquidity pools while it works on permanent rules.
The CFTC followed on October 5, seeking feedback on rules for leveraged retail crypto trading and a new registration category for crypto markets. That starts a lengthy public comment and rulemaking process, rather than putting rules into effect.
Meanwhile, the SEC’s October 1 custody proposal would let state trust companies safeguard client crypto and allow advisers and funds to hold it themselves under certain conditions.
Looking into 2027, Breydo said he expects the SEC to focus on completing offering and custody rules and building on its tokenized-stock exemption. He sees the agencies’ March joint interpretive release as an important foundation, superseding earlier guidance and allowing coordination within existing law.
But a key gap remains: ordinary, unleveraged spot trading still lacks comprehensive federal oversight, beyond the CFTC’s anti-fraud and anti-manipulation powers. Closing that gap was a central goal of Clarity.
For some executives, the legislative setback could produce faster commercial opportunities.
“The SEC and CFTC are already moving proactively to provide the regulatory certainty markets need, and that’s unlocking a wave of M&A across digital assets, traditional financial services, and fintech alike,” said Paul McCaffery, head of digital assets at investment bank KBW.
Bitwise Chief Investment Officer Matt Hougan said he sees the agency approach as more favorable in the short term than legislation that would have required years of follow-up rulemaking. He also expects more protocols to adopt token buybacks, particularly buy-and-burn models, following SEC clarification he said gives investors greater confidence.
Developers are watching whether regulators distinguish writing software from running a financial intermediary. Cathy Yoon, chief legal officer at Solana-focused research and development firm Temporal, said she hopes the SEC will recognize developers as software creators without turning their work into a securities enterprise.
“The fact that the SEC staff is even willing to say these things explicitly is a win,” Yoon said, while noting staff FAQs are not SEC rules.
Michael Lie, global head of digital assets at market maker Flow Traders, said he sees comprehensive regulation as inevitable as finance moves toward 24/7 trading. He is watching innovation exemptions and changes to transfer-agent rules, with European and Asian regimes already advancing.
Legal experts see progress arriving in stages, with implementation the hardest part.
“The biggest gap is implementation,” said Derek Lowrey, head of legal at Newton Labs (formerly known as Magic Labs). Without legislation, overseeing venues, intermediaries, decentralized finance (DeFi) and spot markets remains harder, he said. Existing anti-money laundering, sanctions and record-keeping obligations nevertheless give compliant teams a basis to keep building.
Kevin Kreuser, general counsel at domain-name tokenization firm D3, said tokenized real-world assets particularly need clearer jurisdictional boundaries.
“Agency action is welcome, but it does not provide the same long-term certainty as legislation,” he said.
Jim Petrila, chief legal officer at Dromos Labs, which develops Aerodrome and Velodrome, takes a more bullish view: growing liquidity and tokenized securities on public blockchains could make reversal impractical within two years.
“For DeFi, the signal is bullish,” he said.
Still, the benefits may be uneven. Hougan argued Clarity's failure preserves a regulatory moat for Coinbase (COIN), Kraken and other incumbents, delaying competition that could lower costs. The emerging outlook is faster experimentation, but unresolved questions over who can compete and how firmly the new rules will hold.
Breydo said only Congress can deliver a comprehensive framework with statutory durability.

38,2026-10-11,"Last year, Kuxiu was the first to make chargers that perfectly match the Cosmic Orange iPhone. They released their 3-in-1 X40 turbo and their S4 Solid State battery pack. They both matched the orange color so well that it seemed as if they were 1st-party accessories. This year, the new hero color for the iPhone 18 Pro lineup is burgundy, and Kuxiu is back at it again with a new matching burgundy D5 5-in-1 charging station. With its premium build quality, perfect color matching, built-in cooling, and price, this charger is everything you need from a performance and aesthetic standpoint.
Be sure to check out our hands-on video below!
I reviewed their original D5 a few months back, and it was one of my favorite chargers of the year. Now they have brought it all back with some awesome-looking accents and features. The Kuxiu D5 is advertised as a 5-in-1 charger because it charges three devices but also features a built-in cooling fan and a diagnostics display. You get:
While it is not as compact as something like their X40 Turbo, there is still some thought put into portability and travel with this charger. The Qi2.2 charger can fold down flat into a small cube you can take wherever you need, and the Apple Watch charger retracts into the charger as well. For me, it has worked best as a permanent desk charger, but it’s nice to know that it is also portable. 
As I mentioned, I have been using the original black color for a few months as my main desk charger. It is reliable, functional, and simple to use. So when Kuxiu announced their Burgundy version, I knew I had to check it out, especially since I was looking for some Burgundy color-matching accessories for my new iPhone 18 Pro. 
Since I knew this charger was already great, I wanted to see how closely the color matched the iPhone 18 Pro, and I was impressed by how identical it was. The aluminum parts of the D5 match the Burgundy on the iPhone 18 Pro, and they add other burgundy accents across the charger’s different materials. 
Beyond the aesthetics, my favorite part is the built-in cooling fan because it adds tangible value to the setup. I am someone who constantly installs new iOS betas, tests new features, and puts my phone through situations where it tends to get warm. So, having the active cooling system built directly into the charger has been game-changing in keeping my phone efficiently cooled and helping it sustain 25W charging for longer, since it does not get warm. The fan is also dead silent, but if you do want to turn it off, you can do so with one tap. Then, of course, you have the diagnostics display that shows you all the charging stats you need to see from all the active chargers. 
The Kuxiu D5 is available today on Kuxiu’s site in the original Space Grey and their new Burgundy color. You also get the required 45W charging brick and a braided USB-C cable. It is currently on sale for $59.99. 
For under $60, I challenge someone to find me a high-quality 3-in-1 charger that offers all the features above and includes all the charging accessories you need to use it at its best. That is why Kuxiu is one of the brands I recommend the most; it’s the performance-to-price ratio that sets them apart. So if you just picked up a new Burgundy iPhone or you are in the market for a new 3-in-1 charger, I highly recommend checking this one out. My only wish is that the next version has a USB-C port so you can charge a 4th device, like a smart ring or something. Otherwise, this thing is great.

39,2026-10-11,"Closing on a house brings a strange urge to fill the garage. Every Home Depot trip after moving in walks you past six-tool combo kits stacked on pallets, and each box looks like the responsible buy. Most of those tools would sit in their bags for years. 
The first year of homeownership asks for a narrower set of skills than the kit boxes suggest: mounting things to walls, cutting a few boards, patching drywall, and fighting the weather. After building my whole workshop around Ryobi ONE+, I'd tell any new homeowner to start with four tools on that platform and hold off on the rest until a project demands it. All four share one battery, and every one of them has earned its hook in my garage.
The PCL1200K2 kit lists at $149, and Home Depot drops it to $99 often enough that paying full price feels like a mistake. Inside, you get a 1/2-inch drill/driver, an impact driver, a charger, and two compact batteries. Those last two items are the real purchase. Every ONE+ tool you add later can come home bare, and that's where Ryobi pricing gets genuinely cheap.
The two tools split the work cleanly. The drill bores pilot holes, runs hole saws, and sets drywall anchors without chewing them up. The impact driver takes anything that has to bite deep into framing. My folding squat rack from PRx Performance is the heaviest thing I've hung in this house, and the impact driver sank every lag screw on its wall brackets without bogging down. A drill would have stalled partway, or wrenched my wrist trying.
TV mounts, closet shelving, and garage storage hooks all follow that same pattern. The compact packs look undersized, but they're light enough to hold overhead and outlast a typical mounting job.
I own exclusively Ryobi power tools, but I won't be getting this one. 
An oscillating multi-tool doesn't spin its blade. It swings the blade back and forth through a tiny arc thousands of times per minute, so it can plunge straight into a flat surface and stop exactly on a line. That's why mine came out when I hung drywall. Every outlet and switch box needs an opening, and a jab saw leaves ragged edges that cover plates only partly hide. The multi-tool let me cut along my marked lines and pop out openings that fit the boxes without extra trimming.
The same trick shows up all over a house. It undercuts door casing so new flooring can slide beneath, trims shims flush with the wall, and scrapes old caulk out from around a tub. With the sanding pad attached, it gets into corners a palm sander can't touch.
Ryobi's brushed PCL430B runs about $79 bare, and bundles with a 2.0Ah HP battery regularly fall close to $50. Pick up a multi-pack of blades while you're there. They dull faster than most people expect.
New DIYers tend to assume real woodworking starts with a table saw. I still don't own one. My ONE+ circular saw handles every rip and crosscut I need, and it hangs on a wall instead of taking over a corner of the garage floor.
Its biggest job so far was a desk I built from scratch. The saw cut the top and supporting pieces to size, and a hole saw on the drill finished the cable grommet openings. Nothing in that project called for a stationary tool. A pair of sawhorses, a clamped straightedge, and a decent blade covered all of it.
Beginners get the best results by guiding the saw instead of freehanding it. Holding a speed square against the board keeps crosscuts square, and a straight board clamped alongside the line acts as a fence for longer rips. That setup handles garage shelving, a replacement fence picket, or lumber for a raised garden bed.
Expect to pay around $119 for the bare tool with a 6-1/2-inch blade. If plywood is on your list, swap the stock blade for one with more teeth.

Northern Indiana winters have a habit of freezing locks solid, usually on mornings when I'm already running late. Hot water seems like the quick fix, but whatever runs inside the mechanism can refreeze and leave the lock worse off. A heat gun thaws it without adding any moisture. I keep the nozzle moving a few inches from the lock body, and the ice lets go before long. Take it slow around painted metal or plastic housings, since the air leaving the nozzle is hot enough to blister a finish.
The rest of the year, it covers any job that needs controlled heat. You can shrink plastic insulation film tight across a drafty window, soften old adhesive so labels peel off in one piece, or loosen stubborn caulk before scraping it. If your home was built before 1978, have any flaking paint tested for lead before pointing a heat gun at it.
Ryobi's version usually runs $80–$90 bare, under half of what Milwaukee asks for its heat gun.

Four tools and two compact batteries handle most of what a new house throws at you in year one. Once those batteries live in your garage, every future Ryobi purchase can be a bare tool, so the fifth costs far less than the first did. Wait until a real job asks for it. A tree limb over the fence might justify a reciprocating saw, and a leaf-covered driveway could make the case for a cordless blower. Until then, a little care keeps the four you already own running reliably for years.

40,2026-10-11,"Reopening your apps after a restart is the easy part. Getting back to the folder, tabs, notes, or even the URL you were working from takes longer. PowerToys Workspaces helps by reopening your apps in their saved windows, but that doesn't necessarily bring back what you were doing inside them.
I've been using a free, open-source tool for a couple of weeks to bring back those tabs, folders, and notes. It also has a gap I had to learn the hard way.
A magical tool that launches and arranges all your apps with a single click.
Rewind is a free, open-source tool that lets you save what you have opened on your PC and restore it later with a click. To save a state, you click New, add the name and description for the state, and click Create. Then, you can click Save to save your first state.
One of my saved states is for video editing. It has CapCut open, Brave with multiple stock video sites, and Paint for quick edits. Another is for writing. I have five states like these, covering video editing, writing, research, and casual browsing. So, when I'm done with one, I save it, close everything else, and restore another setup instead of opening everything again.
Rewind doesn't literally freeze or pause the desktop. It saves what's open and opens it again later from that point. So it works more like a save point in a game.
One save surprised me. I had just four apps open, but Rewind captured 47 applications, which turned out to be nine workspace items and 38 background apps. When restoring, I can untick any of them to keep them from coming back.
The first time I used it, I had spent a long time sorting about 40 Firefox tabs into Sidebery trees, one for each thing I was working on. I saved a Rewind state, assuming that covered everything, and closed Firefox. When I restored the state later, Firefox came back as a plain window. The trees were gone, and so were the tabs. I had to rebuild them from scratch.
The reason is that Rewind relies on a browser extension to restore your tabs. Other apps and folders need nothing extra, but Rewind can't see your tabs without it. There’s also no Firefox version. The extension only supports Chromium browsers like Chrome, Brave, and Edge.
The setup takes a little more work than installing an ordinary browser extension. You go to the Extensions page, turn on Developer Mode, then click Load unpacked. Open the Rewind installation folder and select the extension folder. After adding it, turn on the extension. Once I switched it on, Rewind could capture the tabs and bring them back.
I wanted to know what actually comes back when I save the same setup in both tools. So I opened Chrome, Brave, File Explorer, and Sticky Notes, saved the setup, closed everything, and restored it with each tool.
PowerToys did what it's built for. It put the windows back in their saved spots. Chrome and Brave opened to a new tab, though, and File Explorer opened at Home instead of the folder I had been using. Only Sticky Notes came back with its content.
Rewind behaved differently. It brought back my Chrome tabs, Brave tabs, the folder I had opened in File Explorer, and the note.
It even picked up and listed PowerToys’ own background processes, because they were running when I saved. The windows weren't arranged in the same spots as before, so I had to move them back into place myself.
While you can pass launch details like a web address to an app in Workspaces, I didn't try that, so this comparison covers the setup as it comes. So PowerToys put my windows back, and Rewind put my actual work back into the windows.
 Getting the work back doesn't mean everything returns exactly as I left it. My 15 Chrome tabs came back across three windows, while my six Brave tabs were split across two.
The pages reload as they open, although into the state they were in before closing them. The windows didn't return to their old spots either, so I had to rearrange them myself.
There's also no undo button, which is an irony for an app named Rewind. If you close or change the workspace and click Update, it replaces the saved state with whatever is open at the moment.
Despite all this, I'm starting with my apps, tabs, folders, and so on instead of blank windows or starting all over. For the kind of work I do, that saves more rebuilding than the minor window rearranging costs.
System Restore is good, but this new feature is even better.
I changed one habit after losing those Firefox tabs. Since Rewind doesn't support Firefox, and Firefox is my main browser, I enabled session restore on it while Rewind handles the rest of my workspace.
Unless I'm working on something new, Rewind is now one of the first apps I open, if not the first, anytime my PC is on. Once I decide what to work on, I open Rewind, then restore my saved state to continue from where I left it.

41,2026-10-11,"Kodi isn't just another streaming app like Netflix or Hulu. It's a powerful open-source media center that allows you to organize, stream, and play almost any type of content, including movies, TV shows, live channels, music, and even photos. I love that Kodi is entirely customizable and keeps everything arranged within a single, intuitive interface.
Unlike the built-in apps that come preloaded or are installed on most smart TVs, Kodi doesn't confine you to a single brand's ecosystem. If you have your media files stored externally, whether on a hard drive, NAS, or online, it organizes all your files into a sleek and attractive library. However, its real strength lies in its multitude of add-ons. These transform any smart TV into a fully-fledged entertainment hub, which, once installed, saves you from having to juggle between all your different apps.
Installing Kodi isn't complicated; however, the process varies depending on your smart TV brand and its specific functionality.
If you have an Android TV, such as a Sony, Hisense, or TCL model, this process is the most straightforward. Simply open the Google Play Store, search for Kodi, and install it just as you would any other app.
If you have a Samsung, an LG, or another brand that doesn't support the Google Play Store, then the process requires a few extra steps. Because you cannot add Kodi directly to their app stores, you must use an external streaming device (I used an Amazon Fire TV Stick 4K). Installation was easy via the downloader app, and I was up and running with the whole Kodi experience within minutes.
Roku users can mirror or cast Kodi from an external device, such as a smartphone or computer. This is not as seamless as a native installation or streaming stick, but it gets the job done. The main takeaway here is that Kodi can be installed on any TV in a relatively short time, but some methods are more effective and straightforward than others.
Most smart TVs come preloaded with apps or facilitate the download of popular streaming apps like Netflix and Prime Video, but they all operate independently of each other. Kodi seamlessly integrates all your favorite services into one intuitive and easily customizable hub. From here, I can access my entire media library, including apps, movies, music, live TV, and even my Google Photos account. I can easily change themes, or ""skins,"" rearrange menus, and organize metadata precisely as I want, and it looks uniform across all my accounts.
But Kodi's real power lies in its add-ons. These are built-for-purpose apps that you can install as required, transforming the platform into a media powerhouse. I installed The Movie Database (TMDb) and YouTube add-ons, which make discovering and playing new content effortless, and Plex for Kodi, which organizes my local files with promotional artwork and metadata. There are even several curated live TV channels and on-demand shows, adding more flexibility and variety to your Kodi entertainment center.
As with any powerful tool, Kodi is most effective when properly managed. I have experienced issues like buffering and add-ons not loading correctly. However, I found that these issues are usually fixed by clearing Kodi's cache or updating the add-on.
Another thing worth mentioning is security. As with any centralized system that utilizes third-party add-ons, it is essential to ensure that you download only from trusted sources. I have only ever downloaded from the official Kodi repository or those that have been reviewed as safe by the wider Kodi community. As an additional layer of security, I installed the ExpressVPN app on my Fire TV Stick, which is accessible through Kodi's interface, allowing me to stream regional apps and protect myself if I ever stream from public or unverified sources.
Finally, I ensure that I keep Kodi up to date. The platform features an active developer team that continually releases new versions, fixes bugs, enhances performance, and introduces new features. Unfortunately, there's no facility to update Kodi automatically, so this must be performed manually by sideloading the latest version or via Google Play or the Amazon App Store.
Installing Kodi has changed the way that I use my smart TV. Not only is it more flexible, powerful, and fun to use than my Samsung's stock interface, but it has everything I need in a customized, centralized hub. Even if you are a casual media collector, having everything at your fingertips, with artwork and metadata for essential information, makes a world of difference to the user experience. Once you try it, you'll likely wonder how you ever watched TV without it.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Japan has a stationery obsession, and the rest of the world is finally catching on. The country treats writing tools and paper goods the way it treats everything else: with an almost unreasonable level of care, precision, and quiet restraint. What looks simple in the hand rarely is. These eight pieces are proof that back-to-school shopping has a ceiling, and most of us haven’t come anywhere close to hitting it.
Every item on this list earns its place for the same reason: it solves something. Not in a Swiss-Army-knife way, but in a quietly elegant way that makes you wonder why the version you’ve been using for years never worked this well. Stock up your stationery collection with these unique essentials, and feel the difference they make to your everyday routine.


Most clipboards are an afterthought, a plastic spring hinge you buy simply because you had to. The MagBoard is different in the way that good Japanese design tends to be: the function is unchanged, but the thinking behind it is completely rebuilt. A magnet and lever mechanism holds up to 30 sheets of paper in place without the usual clip aggression, and the hardcover backing is stiff enough to write on comfortably while standing, no desk surface needed at all.
The surface is water-resistant and easy to wipe clean, which matters when it travels between meetings, job sites, or a classroom. At A5, it fits in most bags without crowding them, and the cover opens completely flat so there’s no awkward curl mid-note. For anyone who moves through their day between different spaces and needs to take legible notes rather than just keep a surface dry, this is the clipboard that actually earns a permanent spot in the bag.
Click Here to Buy Now: $45.00


The Kakusta looks like a soft pencil case until you set it on a desk. Unzip it, fold the lid back, and the whole thing becomes a stand that props your pens at a 60-degree angle, everything visible, everything reachable in one motion. Sonic, the Japanese stationery brand behind it, didn’t add a separate stand component. The fold itself is the stand. That kind of thinking, where the packaging is the product, is what separates it from anything you’d find at Target.
The triangular cross-section when open isn’t accidental. It creates a stable base that doesn’t tip or slide even on slightly uneven surfaces, and when class or the workday ends, everything folds back into the case in seconds. For students who shift between desks, libraries, and coffee shop tables, not having to manage a separate pen holder is genuinely useful. The Kakusta doesn’t ask you to change how you work. It just eliminates one more thing to carry and set up.


The 365 Days Notebook wasn’t designed for bullet journaling that requires stickers and color-coded spreads. It was designed for editors: people who use a notebook to capture ideas, track timelines, make lists, and sketch rough diagrams all in the same session, sometimes on the same page. The cream-toned dot grid is subtle enough that it doesn’t compete with what you’re writing, and at A6 the whole thing fits in a coat pocket without any compromise.
With 368 pages, it holds a full year of daily writing without exhausting itself by October. The binding opens completely flat without tension, which means pages stay where you put them rather than creeping closed between entries. For someone who writes in transit, between calls, or at lunch, a notebook that opens flat and stays flat is worth more than any feature list could describe. This one simply gets out of your way.


The Levitating Pen stands upright on its base without batteries, electronics, or tricks. It balances vertically through a precisely calibrated magnetic pedestal that holds the pen in a stable position, and the whole arrangement is quiet enough that it reads as a desk object rather than a gadget. Picking it up takes a half-second more than reaching for a pen lying flat on a desk, but that pause changes how you think about the act of writing. It feels deliberate in a way that cheap ballpoints never do.
Write with it, and the ballpoint performs exactly as expected: smooth, consistent, without drama. The benefit isn’t in the ink delivery. It’s in what the pen does to the space around it. A desk with a Levitating Pen on it looks considered, and that visual cue tends to set a tone for the work that follows. For someone who moves between focused writing sessions and longer thinking stretches, having a tool that signals “this is where the work happens” is worth more than most productivity hacks.
Click Here to Buy Now: $79.00


Most timers count up or display numerals. The King Jim VBT10 does something simpler and more useful: it shows a row of large LCD segments representing remaining time, and as the countdown progresses, segments disappear from right to left. No digits to read, no beeping at intervals, just a visual progress bar legible from across a room at a glance. It’s the kind of design decision that seems obvious once you see it, but that no Western manufacturer thought to build first.
For anyone who works in timed blocks, whether that’s the Pomodoro method, writing sessions, or class periods, having a physical timer rather than a phone or laptop notification means focus stays on the task rather than the device. The VBT10 sits on a desk without demanding attention, and the countdown is readable enough to track peripherally. For students and remote workers who need structure without constantly checking a screen, this is a more disciplined option than anything most productivity apps currently offer.


Shachihata makes the Consolidated Number Stamp as a set of individual pieces: 31 dates and 7 days of the week that snap together along two different axes to build any calendar configuration you need. The stamps connect cleanly, ink together evenly, and produce a compact, legible date block that a printed sticker or pre-made date stamp can’t match, because those are fixed and this one isn’t. You build the date you need, stamp it, then rearrange for the next entry.
It’s a genuinely analog solution to a problem most people solve with digital tools or pre-printed labels, and it’s more satisfying than either. For planners, journal keepers, and anyone who archives physical documents by date, having a stamp that adapts rather than repeating the same fixed format is a meaningful upgrade. The ink is self-contained in each piece, and the stamps align precisely enough that the resulting date block looks intentional rather than assembled. That precision is classic Shachihata, and it shows.


A book end that holds books upright when neighbors are present is not a remarkable product. One that lets a single book stand on its own without leaning against anything is. The Lihit Lab Firm Book End achieves this through a gravity-activated stopper that flips down when the book end is upright, supporting the book from behind. Remove the book and the stopper lifts back up, held out of the way by the book end’s own weight. Nothing to adjust manually.
The mechanism means you can remove a single book from a shelf without the rest collapsing, a small but real frustration that anyone who uses bookshelves regularly will recognize immediately. At A4 scale, it handles textbooks and binders as well as standard books, making it useful for student desks and office shelves alike. For people who work daily with physical reference materials and are tired of the toppling-book routine, this is one of those objects that solves a problem so quietly you forget it ever existed before it arrived.


ITO Bindery has been making books by hand in Hachioji, Tokyo since 1938. The Drawing Pad that came out of its collaboration with Yanko Design carries that lineage in the details: binding glue applied by hand with a specialized brush, microperforations inspected sheet by sheet by experienced craftspeople, and a construction that holds through daily use without stiffening at the spine. Each size is limited to 100 individually numbered pads, available exclusively through YD Select, making it as much a document of craft as a writing surface.
Each sheet tears away cleanly along the microperforation, leaving the page intact and ready to pin up, share, or arrange alongside other work in progress. For designers, illustrators, or anyone who treats drawing as a thinking tool rather than a performance, a pad that separates cleanly and doesn’t fight the process is exactly the right kind of equipment. The paper handles pencil, pen, and light marker work without bleed-through. At this build quality and limited-edition scale, $40 is an argument for paying more attention to where your tools come from.
Click Here to Buy Now: $40.00
What makes Japanese stationery worth attention isn’t the aesthetic. It’s the reasoning. Every product here started with a specific problem and arrived at a solution that feels like it was always obvious, even when it clearly wasn’t. The attention to material, mechanism, and user behavior that goes into something as ordinary as a clipboard or pen stand reflects a standard of care that changes what you expect from your tools.
None of these require a lifestyle overhaul or a dedicated setup. They slot into how you already work and make that work feel slightly more resolved. Back-to-school season is usually about quantity: more notebooks, more pens, more highlighters. These eight argue for the opposite. One good clipboard beats five bad ones. One pad that holds together properly lasts longer than anything that falls apart by Thanksgiving. Buy less. Buy better.
