Sen. Thom Tillis, R-N.C., told NBC News’ “Meet the Press” on Sunday that President Donald Trump’s decision to ease sanctions on Russia to allow it to release diesel fuel to the global market may be breaking the law.
“I do believe there’s a legitimate case for saying the president’s not following the law,” Tillis said.
Trump announced Friday on Truth Social that Russia would “immediately supply” hundreds of thousands of tons of diesel fuel to the global marketplace, with more than 1 million additional tons soon on the way. The move drew sharp condemnation from Ukraine and critics of the Trump administration.
Following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine, lawmakers took a series of steps to try to financially punish the Kremlin. In 2022, the Senate unanimously passed a bill to ban Russian oil imports, and Congress has not changed the law to reflect Trump’s announcement.
Asked by moderator Kristen Welker whether he believes Trump is breaking the law with his Russia deal, Tillis said, “I believe that he may be.”
“We were very clear that Russia is the aggressor,” he added. “Russia is the one who can end this war by backing out of territory that they occupied.”
The White House did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Tillis’ remarks.
Tillis referred to year-end congressional funding negotiations when he was asked what the Senate could do if he believes Trump is breaking the law.
“If the president wants my support, which I believe he should get for funding military operations and year-end funding beyond baseline funding, I think the president needs to understand this is a problem, and I’m not the only Republican concerned with this,” he said.
Asked whether Trump’s deal with Russian President Vladimir Putin was putting American lives at risk, Tillis said, “It absolutely will,” pointing to Russian funding of mercenaries and the Kremlin’s relationship with Iran.
Tillis has just months left before his term expires. His retirement from the Senate created a high-profile battleground opening. Democrats are eyeing his seat as a prime target to flip as they seek control of the chamber.
Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., voted against the Russia sanction bill in 2022, and he backed Trump’s decision. Massie said Sunday on X that he voted against the sanctions because they would lead to higher prices in the U.S.
“Glad to see Trump came around to that reality this week,” Massie said in the post. “We should lift sanctions on all petroleum products, and on raw metals like copper and aluminum.” 
Massie has frequently criticized Trump during the second Trump administration, and he lost his re-election bid to a Trump-backed challenger. 
Tillis, too, has been highly critical of aspects of the Trump administration, including the performance of Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth. Tillis, who voted for Hegseth’s confirmation, now says he regrets his vote.
Tillis on Sunday condemned the Pentagon’s plan to livestream the execution by firing squad of Nidal Hasan, who was convicted in the 2009 mass shooting at Fort Hood, Texas.
Tillis said the livestream plans would radicalize people and be used by terrorist groups as a recruiting tool.
“The Fort Hood shooter should be put to death, but there’s a way to deal with this that’s proper, and there’s a way to deal with this that’s more improper than I have ever observed in my life,” he said.
“This is not who we are, and it’s another example of why Pete Hegseth, in all of his bravado, needs to get the hell out of the Department of Defense, because he is a failed leader, and we need strong leadership supporting President Trump right now,” he added.

Weekly poll vivo

As if foldables weren’t expensive enough, the skyrocketing costs of memory chips raised their prices even further – and that’s a problem for the vivo X Fold6. Not just for it, of course, the other foldables that it is competing with are in the same boat.
Still, even among those who want a foldable phone, the largest contingent is people wishing for a price cut. In last week’s poll, we listed several price points for the X Fold6 from around Europe. Alas, its launch came too late to participate in the Prime Day sale this week, but some discounts will come.
That said, don’t expect major cuts – we’ve been tracking the prices of the vivo X300 flagships for months and they never got deep discounts. Part of this is vivo’s strategy – the company wanted to build a no-compromises flagship with top-notch performance and endurance, plus a powerful camera despite the slim build. In other words, this is a “money is no object” flagship rather than one that trades on value for money.
The vivo X Fold6 will certainly face some competition from the new wide foldables. That said, the market is in flux – consumers are still making up their minds about which design is better. The number of people looking for a book-style foldable (that wasn’t the X Fold6) was almost even with the number of people that want to try out the newfangled form factor.





vivo X Fold6
There were some concerns brought about the change from the Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 that powered the vivo X Fold5 to the Dimensity 9500 Super inside the new model. Also, some feel that Android apps could use improvements to better support the usual sizes and aspect ratios brought about by foldables.
Availability will be an issue, but a fairly minor one. With durability mostly solved, high prices remain the biggest hurdle to the adoption of foldables.
And there's not even an Indonesian release!

46,2026-10-11,"Whether you're a new reader or just missed it the first time around, we’re digging into the Bike EXIF archives to revisit some of our all-time favorite builds. This article was originally published on January 11, 2021
The Kawasaki ER-6F ticks all the right boxes as a daily runner, but it's not much of a looker. And it's an uncommon choice for customization, too—we've featured a handful of custom ER-6Ns on these pages, but never the fully faired 'F' model. It takes imagination to look beyond its vanilla bodywork and standard-issue 'sport commuter' style, and see potential.
So this 2006-model ER-6F spent most of its life as a loaner, parked in Alex Gao's garage. That is, until he needed a custom donor in a pinch...
Alex runs Cowboy's Chopper in Taipei, Taiwan, and he kept the Kawasaki for clients to ride, while he worked on more worthy machines. Then he was invited to exhibit a bike at Taiwan's massive Ride Free show. So he dusted off the ER-6F, put it on the bench, tore it down, and tried to visualize the final product.
With its clothes off, the ER-6F is a weird mish-mash of design styles. The tubular front frame looks good, but the boxy asymmetrical swingarm and side-mounted shock are a little weird.
Alex picked up on these quirks and the frame's swooping lines, and loved them. So he decided to accentuate as much of the Kawasaki's engineering as possible, turning it into a cheeky street tracker.
The subframe had to go though, so Alex hacked it off and fabricated a more level setup, with a neat kick in the tail. (It reportedly took a number of attempts to get it just right.) It's capped off with a brown suede and leather saddle, a hand-made stainless steel fender, and a taillight from Heiwa.
Next up, Alex shaped a new fuel tank out of aluminum. It's an intriguing design: curved to match the Kawasaki's modern frame, but with knee indents reminiscent of older Brit bikes. A pair of polished side panels adds an extra visual hit, and there's a hand-made aluminum gas cap up top.
Other hand-shaped bits include the front fender and its mounts, and the offset headlight nacelle. It wraps around a vertically mounted rectangular headlight, from the Japanese custom shop 2%er. Lower down is a small LED-powered fog light, mounted to the left fork lower.
Up in the cockpit, Alex installed a set of no-name handlebars and grips, with Motor Rock mini switches and a generic digital speedo. Lower down are custom rider and passenger foot pegs, mounted on one-off brackets.
Under the hood, Alex ditched the air-box for a couple of cone filters, then relocated the electronics to the now empty space.
The motor was left alone, as were the exhaust headers—but Alex built a new stainless steel box muffler. The embossed lines on it are echoed further back, on a custom-made radiator coolant overflow reservoir, wedged into the swingarm. The radiator guard is custom too, and has the Cowboy's Chopper laser-cut into it.
Most of the rest of the Kawasaki's running gear is stock, but nothing's left completely untouched.
The frame and swingarm were powder coated in semi-gloss silver, the stock yokes were polished, and the wheels and fork lowers were done in black. The only color is a simple olive green paint job on the tank and fenders.
The stock Kawasaki ER-6F isn't likely to pull a second glance, but this one's going to bend necks. Only one question remains: what's Alex going to loan to his clients now?
 
Cowboy's Chopper Facebook | Instagram | Images by Bart Kang

India unveils steel policy aiming for 604 million tonne capacity by 2047
Let’s begin with India’s steel sector. India has released a draft steel policy targeting 604 million tonnes of capacity by 2047, up from 220 million tonnes currently. The policy aims to cut emissions and secure raw materials by pursuing overseas mining assets, joint ventures and diversified iron ore and coal supplies.

Next up, a flash flood in Nepal’s Gorkha district swept away more than 35 houses and bridges on Sunday, prompting authorities to urge residents to move to higher ground. Trump also announced a deal for Russia to supply diesel to the US amid rising fuel prices, drawing criticism from Ukraine and European allies, who warned that easing pressure on Moscow could help fund its war effort.

What will stop me getting my cashback
Cashback for Everyday Market from Woolworths is eligible only on items that display the orange Everyday Market logo and ""sold by"" branding. Cashback is ineligible on any grocery items, packaging, tobacco, and delivery subscriptions.
Cashback is ineligible if using any Woolworths Rewards points or gift cards as payment. Cashback is also ineligible if applying any coupon codes or vouchers.
Orders less than $50 are ineligible for cashback.
When a cap is specified in the cashback rate, it will apply per member and/or per Woolworths customer for the length of the promotional period. Woolworths can decline transactions at its discretion based on these terms. TopCashback has no control over this process.
Any orders made from outside Australia or perceived to be made from outside Australia (ie. using a VPN) will have cashback declined. This is at the discretion of Woolworths (not TopCashback).
Cashback is ineligible on purchases made using Woolworths business accounts, or perceived to be business accounts. This is at the sole discretion of Woolworths.
Cashback is ineligible on use of any coupon codes not advertised on our platform.
Cashback is ineligible on use of gift cards, vouchers, or store credit for partial or full payment. Cashback is also ineligible on purchase of gift cards.
Cashback is ineligible on purchases made via the retailer's mobile app.

50,2026-10-11,"Crypto has dealt with multiple inflation bugs this year, one of the many signs that vulnerabilities found in industry projects are becoming more serious."
51,2026-10-11,"The US-Israel war on Iran and its inflationary impact will dominate the agenda.
Save
Share
Finance officials from around the world will gather in Bangkok this week under the shadow of a widening war in the Middle East, the biggest-ever energy supply shock and rising interest rates that together pose daunting risks to already-sluggish global economic growth.
The US-Israel war on Iran, now in its eighth month, and its inflationary impact are slated to dominate the agenda and sideline conversations during the annual meetings of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) and World Bank, held outside Washington for the first time in three years.
IMF Managing Director Kristalina Georgieva told Reuters News Agency that 18,000 people were registered to attend the meetings, 4,000 more than the last off-site meetings held in Morocco in October 2023.
Notably absent will be United States Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, who dispatched two senior officials in his stead while he handles some “domestic engagements”, a US official said. Federal Reserve Board Chairman Kevin Warsh will attend and is slated to participate in a public event with Georgieva on October 16.
Several other finance ministers were also staying home due to domestic budget and election duties, but most central bankers would attend, Georgieva said.
Bessent’s decision to skip the high-profile gathering and a meeting of the Group of 20 (G20) major economies, which the US leads this year, may frustrate counterparts amid rising tensions over the Iran war, Ukraine’s battle against Russia’s invasion, and the US move to impose sanctions on the International Criminal Court.
The Group of Seven (G7) countries have agreed to release 100 million barrels of diesel and crude oil from emergency reserves under pressure from US President Donald Trump, who is keen to see lower petrol prices before November elections that could see his Republican Party lose control of Congress.
Trump on Friday announced a deal with Russia that would provide even more diesel to global markets and a temporary waiver of US sanctions designed to deprive Moscow of revenues for its ‌‌war on Ukraine. The move drew swift criticism from Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy.
More than one billion barrels of oil have been released, mainly from onshore commercial inventories, since the start of the war on February 28. But industry executives say the amount of oil in storage that is accessible to the global market is running low, making the market more fragile and fuelling pressure on prices.
 

 
The IMF has signalled little change in its forecast for three percent global growth in 2026, and may edge its forecast for next year slightly higher. But some countries will see downgrades, including Ukraine, now in its fifth year of war against Russia’s invasion, and Gulf countries hit by Iranian strikes and sharply reduced energy exports.
IMF research released on Tuesday showed that sharp spikes in food and energy prices are an increasingly common source of crises that drive inflation expectations higher for longer, worsen poverty and threaten economic stability.
One headache for policymakers is the growing public debt burden that is sapping growth and adding inflationary pressures. The IMF says public debt is at the highest level since World War II and will exceed 100 percent of gross domestic product (GDP) before 2030.
Advanced economies, led by the US, have the highest debt-to-GDP ratios, but emerging markets and low-income countries are particularly vulnerable, given a perfect storm of challenges: capital outflows in search of higher US rates, weather extremes triggered by the El Nino climate phenomenon, and a lack of investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has mitigated negative supply shocks in the US and other rich countries.
Developing countries are particularly vulnerable given high public debt levels that will have to be renegotiated at higher interest rates. Together, they face $400bn in debt payments to external creditors in 2026, and interest payments already exceed 10 percent of revenue on average.
Many lower-income countries are worried about new IMF recommendations for loan programmes that call for fewer but deeper reforms as a condition for approving lending, a change that many fear will lead to painful austerity measures.

Rise and shine with a sunrise alarm 

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The mornings are just starting to draw in, and if the idea of waking up to darkness fills you with doom and gloom, perhaps it's time to invest in a sunrise alarm clock (sometimes called a wakeup light). The idea is simple: these lamps are designed to brighten gradually in the morning, to rouse you gently from your slumber in a way that mimics the sun.
They're also meant to help you dodge that groggy feeling you get when you're awoken aggressively from deep sleep, so you're more alert and better equipped to tackle the day. I've tested a few sunrise alarms, and can confirm they're a whole lot gentler and more soothing than an aggressive phone alarm bleeping you awake.
There's a wide range of options on the market. At the cheapest end of the scale you'll find wakeup lights that just have a basic, brightening setting. More advanced options let you choose the duration of brightening and can offer a range of sounds to go with the light; Lumie's priciest options offer a massive range of sounds, from rainfall to kittens purring, cafe noises, tree frogs and even ping pong. My top tip is not to pick a noise that might naturally occur in your bedroom — I chose birdsong, and mistook seagulls having a midnight chat for my call to start getting ready for work.
Many sunrise alarms also have features to help you drop off peacefully on those dark winter evenings. Look for a sunset feature — which dims gently over a period, to help you wind down — and white noise options, if that's something that helps you drop off. If you're hunting for an all-in-one solution, a number of models have radio built-in too.
Lumie's Bodyclock range is one of the most popular sunrise alarm lineups. I've tried a couple of these and was impressed, but they're not the only option on the market. Scroll down for a roundup of well-reviewed wakeup lights from respected brands including Philips Hue, Beurer and Hatch.

GTA 6 leaker CyberLeek is back with another video, this time with a naked Lucia taking a parachute jump off a rooftop.
The five-minute clip was posted on ResetEra a few days after CyberLeek's 25-minute video of Jason, and a day after the leaker threatened to release a full playable build.
Note: there are some mild spoilers pertaining to what you might do in GTA 6 below.
The video - which I'm afraid I'm not going to post here - reportedly opens with CyberLeek driving through Vice City in a black-and-white car, before Lucia gets out in nowt but her birthday suit.
As with Jason, nobody reacts to her, and as we noted last time, whoever is playing seems to have access to a dev menu, which means a naked Lucia could be a debug feature rather than something you'll be able to do in the open world.
Lucia then heads up some stairs to an ATM, where she beats up a passer-by and takes their money. The machine shows a $15,000 limit and a Buckme logo (thanks, TheGamer).
She goes into a nearby building, where a construction worker asks if she's with the guy on the roof, and takes the elevator after a short exchange. Following a phone call, she makes her way to the roof and is handed a parachute before jumping off.
CyberLeek isn't a natural at that part of the game. The first jump drifts well off course and ends in a crash into a building, while the second gets closer to the target but sails past the landing point. The video also includes an in-game ad for Buckme.
CyberLeek has said they'll release the full build if their cryptocurrency coin reaches a market cap of $30 million.
Rockstar has previously issued statements regarding the leaker, noting that the leaks are ""heartbreaking"" and that ""this is obviously not how we intended for you to see the game after all this time"". Take-Two, Rockstar's parent company, has been hunting for the person behind the leaks, and has tried to subpoena Discord for information pertaining to the leaker's identity.

54,2026-10-11," The solar trade publication Solar Power World reported that a single sodium-ion unit's 9.25-kWh capacity in a comparable design is less than the 15-kWh lithium models common on the residential market [3]. AuroraCell says the system combines stable chemistry, built-in battery management and passive thermal protection, according to Interesting Engineering [4]. The manufacturer also lists up to 30,000 cycles on a related product page, a figure marked with an asterisk [5].Sodium-Ion Chemistry and Market Context Sodium is more abundant and lower-cost than lithium, and sodium-ion cells can be manufactured without cobalt or nickel, according to a technical brief from the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), which states that ""ambient temperature sodium batteries have the potential to meet growing worldwide demand for electrical energy storage, due in part to sodium's widespread availability and low cost"" [6]. Market conditions have shifted in sodium-ion's favor. CATL says sodium-ion batteries should match lithium iron phosphate costs by the end of 2026, according to SodiumBatteryHub [7], and Chinese and U.S. manufacturers are expanding output for grid storage, data center backup and renewable integration, according to NaturalNews.com [8]. Sodium battery technology ""targets grid storage first"" and also supports low-speed electric vehicles and other cost-sensitive uses, the industry outlet reported [9]. Advocates of decentralized energy argue that home batteries support household self-reliance at a time when the grid's reliability is under question.Industry and Regulatory Response The manufacturer's stated NCO chemistry is designed to improve battery life, stability and performance across a wider temperature range; the U.S. Department of Energy has separately identified sodium-ion as a chemistry that could be produced domestically with locally sourced materials [10]. Installers and utilities are evaluating sodium-ion products, but mass residential adoption depends on price and proven performance, analysts said. One Australian distributor, Foton Pty Ltd, said it sold a 100-kilowatt, 200-kilowatt-hour sodium-ion commercial system to a paying customer in 2026, a transaction the company described as possibly the country's first commercial sodium-ion battery sale [11]. In the U.S., Peak Energy selected Sacramento, California, for a grid-scale sodium-ion factory backed by $71 million in investment, with production scheduled to start in early 2027 [12]. 55,2026-10-11,"Posted in: CORSAIR, Pop Culture, Technology | Tagged: headset, HS80 v2 Max Wireless Headset
CORSAIR has upgraded one of their gaming headsets as they have launched the new HS80 v2 Max Wireless Headset onto the market.
CORSAIR has a brand-new gamer headset on the market this week, as they have launched the HS80 v2 Max Wireless Headset. This version of the model was designed to be a high-end headset with upgraded 50mm non-Newtonian laminate drivers, as well as an upgraded broadcast-grade microphone with a 10mm capsule, and a simultaneous 2.4GHz and Bluetooth wireless setup with up to 110 hours of battery life. We have the finer details about it for you here, along with some images, as it's going for $200.
Hearing in-game cues clearly can make the difference between victory and defeat. After a loud sound like a gunshot or explosion, residual vibration in a driver's diaphragm can obscure quieter details, such as footsteps or reloads. The upgraded 50mm non-Newtonian laminate drivers in the HS80 v2 Max Wireless help reduce that residual vibration, allowing the diaphragm to settle quickly, keeping quieter details clearer as the action unfolds. The result is powerful bass, crisp highs, and rich, full-range audio that helps you hear the cues that matter.
On PC, Dolby Atmos spatial audio builds on that foundation by placing every sound precisely in three-dimensional space. Players can hear the footstep above them, the reload behind them, and the distant gunfire to the left. Being heard matters as much as hearing. The HS80 v2 Max Wireless' broadcast-grade 10mm microphone capsule captures clear, natural voice comms, while a built-in pop filter helps reduce harsh popping sounds. Flip the mic up to mute instantly. On PCs with a compatible NVIDIA GeForce RTX graphics card, AI-powered NVIDIA Broadcast mic enhancement helps reduce keyboard clatter, fan noise, and room ambiance, keeping your voice clear and your squad focused on your callouts.
Stay comfortable through every session with plush memory foam ear cushions wrapped in breathable fabric and a floating adjustable headband that helps distribute weight evenly and minimize pressure points. The cushions hold their shape session after session for lasting comfort, while a durable aluminum frame keeps the 370g headset feeling secure and comfortable. Simultaneous 2.4GHz wireless and Bluetooth let players game on one device while taking a call or streaming music from another. The HS80 v2 Max Wireless is compatible with PC, Mac, PlayStation, Nintendo Switch, and mobile devices.
The HS80 v2 Max Wireless delivers up to 110 hours of battery life on 2.4GHz wireless, or up to 130 hours over Bluetooth. When a quick top-up is needed, 15 minutes of charging gives six hours back. On-ear controls keep the essentials within reach, including a volume scroll wheel with multifunction button, a power button, and a dedicated Bluetooth button. CORSAIR iCUE software puts sound and style under the player's control. Enable NVIDIA Broadcast features, customize EQ settings, load pre-tuned profiles, and personalize the headset's RGB lighting to match the rest of the setup.

56,2026-10-11,"Ledger said the incident appeared to be isolated to a single reseller and its Southeast Asian market. 
Hardware wallet maker Ledger, which has been investigating reports of crypto losses involving its devices purchased from a Southeast Asian reseller, confirmed that one of the affected users’ devices contained an unauthorized hardware implant.
Ledger said in a Saturday post on X that it was contacting users as part of its ongoing investigation into the losses, which investigator Specter estimated may exceed $86 million across Bitcoin, Ethereum and Tron.
Ledger asked that individuals who have information regarding the investigation to contact its bounty program at bounty@ledger.fr.
As a measure of precaution, reseller CryptoBilis confirmed in the post that it had ceased sales of all hardware wallet inventory until the investigation is concluded. Ledger said it is in active communication with CryptoBilis on next steps.
Related: Ledger investigates fund losses linked to Southeast Asian reseller, warns users
Ledger recommends users who purchased one of their devices from this reseller to not initiate set-up if they have not yet done so. If they have set up their Ledger device, they should consider moving assets to a new Ledger signer, including using a new seed.
Cointelegraph reported on Friday that CryptoBilis was listed as an authorized Ledger reseller in Indonesia, Malaysia and the Philippines.
Ledger has not confirmed how many customers may be affected or the value of the reported losses.
In a statement to Cointelegraph, Ledger said the incident appeared to be isolated to the single reseller and its market. 
“Ledger’s infrastructure, systems and services were not compromised,” the company said, adding that its investigation remained ongoing.
For assistance or questions, contact Ledger customer support through official channels at http://support.ledger.com.

57,2026-10-11,"Wide foldables are surging in popularity thanks to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 and the iPhone Duo. While folding phones with a squarish form factor have never garnered wide appeal, casual users are starting to see the benefit of carrying a short and stout foldable in their pocket. When shut, they're short devices that are easy to maneuver. Open them up, and they're multipurpose devices superb for video playback in landscape mode or productivity in portrait mode.
The price of foldables is still the main barrier to entry for most people. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 costs $1,899, whereas the iPhone Duo costs $1,999. One of those options might be the best wide foldable money can buy, but it isn't the best value. The latter title belongs to the semi-wide foldable that started it all: the original Google Pixel Fold. It runs Android 17 and costs under $500 in 2026, making it the perfect wide foldable for people interested in the segment for the first time. 
It's easy to swoon over the iPhone Duo, but consider these Samsung wins first.
Right off the bat, it's important to note that the original Google Pixel Fold is not nearly as wide as the Galaxy Z Fold 8 or the iPhone Duo. Currently, the U.S. foldable market features two main form factors for those who want large screens. You have tall foldables such as the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra, Motorola Razr Fold, or Google Pixel 11 Pro Fold that unfold to a nearly square aspect ratio. The short-and-wide Galaxy Z Fold 8 and iPhone Duo are in the other category, and the first-generation Pixel Fold falls somewhere in the middle. 
For those solely buying a foldable to watch widescreen video, the Pixel Fold's aspect ratio is a downgrade compared to the newer wide foldables. However, it has key perks that make it a solid alternative to both wide and square foldables. The Pixel Fold's 5.8-inch cover screen is larger than the one you'll find on both the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and the iPhone Duo. It sports a taller aspect ratio, making the outer display less compromising — it's easier and more familiar to use for basic smartphone tasks. 
 In the photo above, you can see how the Pixel Fold compares to standard Pixel and Galaxy foldables. It's shorter, but the cover screen is still usable with one hand. You still get many of the benefits of the wider folding display. The original Pixel Fold can fit more widescreen video content on its foldable screen than the brand-new Pixel 11 Pro Fold, thanks to the more forgiving aspect ratio. It's a 7.6-inch folding display, the same size as the Galaxy Z Fold 8 and iPhone Duo. 
As a Galaxy Z Fold 8 user and Pixel Fold tester, I can confirm that the Galaxy Z Fold 8 does fit more widescreen video on its inner screen. The same will be true of the iPhone Duo. That being said, there are apps that don't run well on either Galaxy Z Fold 8 screen due to the odd aspect ratios. As this is Apple's first foldable, apps that aren't optimized for the iOS 27.1 SDK may not run properly on the iPhone Duo's displays at launch. 
With the Pixel Fold splitting the middle between the two modern form factors, it's a more versatile foldable. 
With square-shaped foldables, rotating the device while it's unfolded barely makes a difference. Wide foldables can be used like a small tablet when you open them up — the screen shapes are similar to an iPad Mini, but smaller. This means you get a true portrait mode, and this is how I've found myself using the Galaxy Z Fold 8 most often. The cover screen isn't ideal for apps that require vertical space, such as social media or reading apps. 
Meanwhile, the folding displays aboard wide foldables are excellent for scrolling, reading, and note-taking. Phones like the Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra and the Pixel 11 Pro Fold can't be used in the same way, because their display is squarish regardless of their orientation. Crucially, the Pixel Fold's aspect ratio also accommodates unique portrait and landscape viewing modes.
By the numbers, the Pixel Fold has a 6:5 inner screen aspect ratio. That's not as wide as the Galaxy Z Fold 8's 4:3 aspect ratio or the iPhone Duo's 1.41:1 aspect ratio. But as I've already mentioned, the middle ground allows the Pixel Fold to deliver many of the perks offered by wide foldables without some of the drawbacks. 
The original Pixel Fold is still receiving software updates, and it runs Android 17. You get all the latest Android multitasking features on the Pixel Fold, including the useful 90:10 split-screen views. These allow you to jump between two apps quickly while viewing the primary app at nearly full size. Android 17 specifically brought app bubbles to Pixel phones, and on foldables like the Pixel Fold, they live conveniently in the Taskbar.
You can use up to five apps in floating windows above a split-screen pair for incredible multitasking workflows, which is something the iPhone Duo can't do. 
 The Pixel Fold is far from perfect, and its hardware is a far cry from what you'd get from a Galaxy Z Fold 8 or iPhone Duo. It's very heavy, it's not as durable, and it's powered by a weak Tensor G2 chip. But the Pixel Fold is also available in refurbished condition for roughly a quarter of the price of those new phones. It's easy to accept those compromises when you're saving as much as $1,550 compared to buying new. The value proposition can't be beat. 
Who should buy the Pixel Fold in 2026? To me, the answer is clear: someone who wants a wide foldable as a second phone. It's not a coincidence that Apple launched iPhone Handoff — a feature that automatically moves an active cellular connection between two iPhones — right before the iPhone Duo's release. Apple thinks that buyers will want a ""normal"" phone and a wide foldable, and it's probably right. It's easier to justify a $500 secondary phone than a $2,000 one. 
Should you trust renewed and refurbished phones? Refurbished phones typically go through more checks than the average used device, and are usually backed by a longer warranty or return window. However, what ""refurbished"" means can vary from site to site. Check out our complete guide to buying refurbished before picking out a renewed or refurbished phone.
People curious about wide foldables, but who are still hesitant, should also consider the Pixel Fold. At about half the price of a traditional flagship, buying a refurbished Pixel Fold is low risk. You can try one out during the return period, of course. And if you decide to keep it, you'll get to live with a wide foldable for an extended period before investing thousands of dollars in a brand-new one. 
I bought a Galaxy Z Fold 8 with my own cash, and heck, I might just grab an iPhone Duo too. I know better than anyone how good the experience of buying a 2026 foldable can be. But after testing the Galaxy Z Fold 8 alongside the Pixel Fold, I also know the best-value wide foldable is Google's original model from 2023.
The Google Pixel Fold is a folding phone powered by the Tensor G2 chip with a wide aspect ratio. It's upgradeable to Android 17 and is available for around $500 in 2026 a few years post-release.

58,2026-10-11,"Medical services in Taiz risk shutting down as fuel, medicines, and supplies run alarmingly low under Houthi blockade.
Save
Share
Yemen’s internationally recognised government has warned of an “imminent health and humanitarian catastrophe” in the besieged city of Taiz, in the country’s southwest, as the Houthis continue their siege and “obstruct essential supplies”.
Yemen’s Ministry of Public Health and Population said in a statement on Saturday that medicines, intravenous fluids, medical supplies and fuel at the city’s health facilities were “nearing depletion”.
It said emergency care, surgery, intensive care, dialysis and neonatal services could be forced to stop if the situation continued, putting patients, injured people, pregnant women and newborns “at immediate risk”.
Fuel shortages also threaten electricity generators, ambulances and the cold chains that keep medicines and vaccines usable, which could cause “severe complications and preventable deaths”, the ministry said.
It demanded the siege be lifted immediately, and supply routes reopened, with safe passage for patients, ambulances, health workers and aid.
It called on the United Nations and humanitarian organisations to “deliver medicines, intravenous fluids, medical supplies, oxygen and fuel” to the city’s health facilities.
“Every delay increases the risk of service interruption and loss of life,” the ministry said.
 

 
Al Jazeera’s Yousef Mawry, reporting from Sanaa, said southwestern Taiz is a ‘major battleground’ between the Houthis and troops of the internationally recognised Yemeni government.
Both sides claim to be gaining ground. Mawry confirmed that the strategic city of Mocha, the district of Dhubab, and Bab al-Mandeb remain under the control of the Houthi group.
Fellow at the Future Security Program of the New America think tank, Adam Baron, told Al Jazeera, Taiz could become “the real game changer” in the conflict.
But he warned, “its not just about military dynamics—the incipient siege on Yemen’s third largest city leaves roughly a million people trapped, creating real risks of a devastating humanitarian crisis.”
The Houthis, an armed group backed by Iran and also known as Ansar Allah, seized Yemen’s capital, Sanaa, by force in 2014 and later imposed their own government. They control the country’s north and northeast.
Taiz endured a devastating Houthi siege between 2015 and 2016, when Yemeni government forces were confined to an enclave in the city centre.
The siege was broken in 2016, though the Houthis kept a partial blockade through other access points. In 2024, they agreed to reopen a route from the east, but it has been closed after fighting resumed.
The renewed siege took shape in early October.
The Houthis launched an offensive along the Red Sea coast of the Taiz governorate in September, capturing the port city of Mocha, and by October 4 had taken the town of At Turbah, cutting the last main road between Taiz city and other government-held areas.
 

 
Yemeni government forces, backed by Saudi Arabia, began a counteroffensive in early October.
Nabil Jamil, director-general of the Yemeni government’s planning and development office in the Taiz governorate, told Al Jazeera the current siege is “more comprehensive and its impact more severe than in 2015”.
He said about 2.8 million people live in government-held areas of the governorate, along with 200,000 who have recently been displaced.
Fuel has become scarce, and on the black market, the price of a 20-litre (5.2-gallon) container of petrol has risen from 30,000 Yemeni riyals ($19) to 90,000 ($58).
“We are still waiting for a response or intervention on the ground,” Jamil said.
The UN refugee agency and the World Health Organisation have warned that the fighting is putting Yemen’s fragile health system in peril.
“Civilians must never be turned into a means of exerting pressure in any conflict,” said Nabil Sharyan, a lawyer and head of the Justice for Rights and Development Organisation.

Photo Credit: A scene from Skift's Data + AI Summit in London on October 6, 2026 Skift

                Skift's Data + AI Summit in Europe this week just showed that AI may be changing travel distribution and internal operations, but booking volume is another story.            
Europe's independent hotels face a new AI visibility problem: travelers are using AI to plan but few are booking through it, and the clearest measurable returns are showing up in operational uses rather than autonomous trip planning.
Those pressures are particularly visible in Europe. The region has a hotel market dominated by independents, while the EU AI Act is giving companies another reason to formalize how AI tools are reviewed and deployed internally.
At Skift's Data + AI Summit in London last week, the conversation was less about what AI could do than what companies could prove — who gets seen, who books, and where the economics work.
Europe's independent hotels risk being invisible in AI travel answers before they ever compete for a booking. Roughly three-quarters of European hotels are independentl

At Skift's Data + AI Summit in London, the evidence pointed in one direction: AI is already changing who gets discovered, but almost no one is booking through it. Stiplo's analysis of 27,360 AI travel answers across 659 properties in Barcelona found independent hotels are eight times more likely not to be known by an AI system than chains — though once known, they were recommended at roughly the same rate. That matters most in Europe, where about three-quarters of hotels are independently owned, according to 2023 research from Booking.com and Statista using CoStar data. Stiplo founder Carlo Del Mistro said Google review volume was the strongest factor associated with discoverability. On the demand side, Booking Holdings has disclosed that less than 1% of its room nights originate from AI channels, and Bernstein estimates about 1.6% at Marriott. Skyscanner CEO Bryan Batista cited a global survey in which 51% of travelers said they distrust the reliability and accuracy of AI data and recommendations. Executives said the measurable returns are landing in operations instead — Travelport CTO Andrew Jordan warned that saved hours alone just add an AI cost line to the P&L, while OAG CEO Filip Filipov pointed to United Airlines' Connection Saver at Denver, which he said saved 54,000 passenger connections. The EU AI Act is giving European companies another reason to formalize how AI tools are reviewed and deployed internally."
60,2026-10-11,"
Exactly a month and 2 days ago, John Ternus unveiled Apple’s first foldable phone. It’s been a decade in the making, Apple’s done a whole bunch in the interim, hit $1 trillion in 2018, and subsequently grown to a $3 trillion brand, shifted from a hardware company to a hardware AND services company, launched a spatial computing headset, pretty much worked on and shelved their Apple Car. Rumors are tracking a few more devices that Apple’s been secretly working on. A smart ring that does the same job as a watch, or alternatively a screen-less Watch Air that competes with the likes of Whoop and Fitbit, and even a foldable single-screen laptop modeled on the same design of the iPhone Duo.
I’m not entirely sure whether Apple’s truly working on a larger folding device, but scaling the iPhone Duo up to a larger hardware unit isn’t a good idea for a bunch of reasons. Aside from the fact that it would cannibalize a whole slew of existing products, it would also create a bit of a UX nightmare for the iPadOS developer community. An iPad Duo (let’s just call it that) is nothing but a MacBook Neo with extra screens. The MacBook Neo ITSELF is designed to be compact and handle compact tasks, giving it a foldable design with maybe iPad Pro-level power achieves nothing. Here are a few reasons why the foldable iPad Duo isn’t the best idea.







Samsung, Xiaomi, Huawei, Google, Motorola, and a bunch of other makers have folding phones on their roster – yet nobody’s tried to push a folding tablet beyond maybe just 1 iteration. Huawei unveiled the MateBook Fold last year and hasn’t done much to work on a v2.0. It cost around $3,300 when it launched and occupied headlines for precisely a month before becoming yesterday’s news (partially because of its China-only distribution platform). The point I’m making is that companies haven’t explored the foldable tablet space because there’s little merit to it. Lenovo’s worked on a ThinkPad X1 Fold too, but here’s the hilarious bit – they’re marketing it more as a laptop than as a traditional tablet.
Tablets aren’t designed to be carried in pockets, therefore a smaller tablet that turns into a bigger one isn’t really a pressing need. Even if they were, the size of a pocket confines you to a very limited bounding box, giving you a folding phone template rather than a folding tablet one. Tablets themselves aren’t as popular as phones are, and folding phones are already a niche to begin with – making folding tablets mainstream would cost more money than it’s worth.







Of all the companies that have worked in the foldable space, there’s always been one question they’ve struggled to answer. What aspect ratio do we make? Because a foldable device exists in two states – a shut state and an open state. With phones, there are broadly 3 templates – the flip style foldable, which offers a square aspect ratio that turns into a vertical screen when opened. The second is the long book ratio that turns squarish when opened sideways. The third one, which Apple’s popularizing, is the middle ground – a book-shaped design that’s short and stout and has a repeating aspect ratio like the A4 and A3 paper so that the OS can scale meaningfully.
At least in this concept, we’re looking at what I believe is one of the most bizarre aspect ratios ever. This concept by Krishnavi Anand Krishnavi opts for an almost 1:1 ratio when shut, opening to reveal what feels like a widescreen 16:9 device. Both versions have their caveats – a 1:1 tablet doesn’t exist and it shouldn’t. Nobody works on a square screen, content isn’t optimized for it, heck even physical notebooks are vertical (who writes on square paper). Open it and you have a socially acceptable 16:9 device, but tablets are usually one of two things – they’re either big and 4:3 or they’re small and 16:9. Nobody makes a large 16:9 tablet because carrying it is a nuisance – you might as well just buy a laptop with a convertible design. A large 4:3 foldable tablet would fold down to a small 3:2 device, which still feels palpable, but continue reading and you’ll realize why the odds are stacked against a foldable tablet.

Apple’s poured hundreds of millions, if not billions into R&D for its titanium hinge. Every company has its own workaround for a hinge, ensuring durability, reliability, and the ability to conceal/remove the crease. It works well in flip phones, it’s tricky with book style foldables but companies have still come a long way in all these years. Scale that up to a device that needs a 7-8-inch long hinge and the engineering problems compound significantly. The hinge needs to uniformly open from one end to the other. Any discrepancy and the screen could stretch/tear. Any damage and the device could get stuck in a closed or open position for life. Laptops have suffered from hinge problems for years now, they’re the first mechanical part to fail on a laptop), so you can only imagine how much more work a tablet has to do – while still ensuring you can open/close it 10-20 times a day.
Not only is the hinge a problem, the screen is too. The screen used on a foldable phone is very different from the one used on you 15.6″ laptop. Companies work with suppliers to find the right kind of OLED that can take daily bending without the pixels suffering failure. The crease has to vanish when open and allow the screen to meaningfully bend when shut. A laptop display also doesn’t get touched hundreds of times a day the way a tablet or phone does. So it’s easier for a company to commission hundreds of thousands of small 6″ or larger OLED displays. Scale that up to a 12-15″ OLED display and you aren’t doubling the price, you’re quadrupling it (hold your phone to your laptop and see, four of its 6″ screens will fit in your 13″ laptop). A 4x pricier display eats up all your profits on a folding tablet.

This might be the biggest problem yet. We’re still struggling to find a mainstream audience for folding phones – so you can imagine how difficult it’ll be to find someone willing to shell out upwards of $3k on a folding tablet. The audience doesn’t exist because the use-case doesn’t exist and neither does the appetite (more on the appetite later). What would you do on an iPad Duo that you can’t already do on a laptop or a tablet? Phones need to be compact for a reason so the idea of folding your phone for easy carrying still holds up logically – folding a tablet makes absolutely no sense when it isn’t fitting into a constrained space like your pants pocket.
People buy their pants regardless of the phone they carry so the phone has to work backwards to fit into pants. With tablets and laptops, it’s the other way around – you buy a sleeve or a backpack based on the size of device you have. A larger laptop demands a larger bag, a sleeker, smaller tablet works great with a sling. Nobody buys the sling before they own the tablet. But I digress – the foldable tablet doesn’t exist because the market doesn’t. In over 10 years of working for Yanko Design, I’ve seen dozens of folding phone concepts, but even concept designers don’t work on foldable tablet concepts because there’s honestly nothing to desire there!

This year, when Apple launched the MacBook Neo, we wrote about how it proved Apple wrong in one fundamental way. Apple’s always priced their products at a premium, thinking that they get to choose their audience – it worked when the economy was in a steadier state, when diesel wasn’t more than $6 a gallon. When Apple launched the MacBook Neo, the laptop literally flew off shelves, proving that there was a VERY strong demand for affordable computing. Especially when RAM and storage prices are already so high thanks to this AI boom that nobody asked for.
The MacBook Neo’s success with the Vision Pro’s silent failure proved that it’s time for tech to be democratic again. To focus less on shareholder value and more on actual consumer value. The iPhone Duo already costs upwards of most people’s salaries (globally speaking), so building an iPad Duo that would probably touch $3.5-4k in price makes no sense for a product that’s supposed to be for consumers, not enterprises. The iPad Duo doesn’t exist, and from the rumor mill, it probably won’t for the aforementioned reasons. John Ternus has proved to be an astute consumer-centric design lead, realizing what products fill the current gaps in Apple’s offerings. Speaking of which, I’m still waiting on that smarter Siri 2.0…
The porcupine is one of nature’s better-engineered designs. It’s compact, self-sufficient, and its entire defense system lives in its skin rather than in added bulk….
Somewhere in a closet, a perfectly good LEGO minifigure is staring through a plastic bag at the back of a cardboard box. It has a…

Editorial Director/Box Office Editor

	The China release of Sony/Constantin’s Resident Evil propelled the move not only to the top of the global weekend box office in its fourth frame with an overall $62.7M WW, but the pic also became Zach Cregger’s highest grossing movie ever (as we first told) with $320.2M. That beats the previous high of his Oscar winning Weapons ($270M WW) last year.

	China delivered $40.6M, which repped the best opening for a horror film in the last two years. 

Resident Evil invaded its native country of Japan with a $3.6M opening. Other top per performing market cumes are UK $17.0M, Mexico $11.8M, Australia $8.5M, France $8.5M, Germany $7.3M and Spain $7.1M.

	Why wow? 	Stateside, that’s Jessica Chastain’s sixth highest opening after It: Chapter Two ($91M), The Martian ($54.3M), Interstellar ($47.5M), X-Men: Dark Phoenix ($32.8M) and Mama ($28.4M), which was another solo horror vehicle for here. If you want to be technical about it, most of these films were ensembles, hence, Other Mommy can be argued as her second-highest solo start stateside. Point is, audiences clearly flocked to see Chastain in a spooky movie. Other Mommy was No. 1 in UK/Ireland, Italy, Mexico, U.A.E., other Middle East, Taiwan, Malaysia, Indonesia, Argentina, Central America, Chile, Greece, Turkey, Israel, Norway, Singapore, Ecuador, Venezuela, Uruguay, Lebanon, Trinidad, Pakistan and Iceland. The spiritual scary nature of the film is the reason for its rally in Catholic countries, specifically Latin America.

	










	Chastain counted $2.6M at 625 screens in UK & Ireland, surpassing the lifetime box office of The Boogeyman to become Rob Savage’s highest-grossing theatrical release. The weekend result is above Exorcist: Believer, Evil Dead Rise, Smile and Speak No Evil, and more than double Black Phone.

	Italy was molto bene with $2.2M at 315 screens, repping 31% of the weekend’s marketshare. The weekend result is above Five Nights at Freddy’s 2, Backrooms, Resident Evil, Scream 7, Exorcist: Believer, more than double Evil Dead Rise, M3GAN and Insidious: Out of the Further, more than 5x Smile, and more than 8x The Black Phone.

Other Mommy counted $1.9M in Mexico for 24% of the weekend marketshare, a result that’s above Evil Dead Rise, M3GAN and Smile.

	Australia’s $1.1M for the Savage pic ranked No. 2 at 246 screens, with a healthy 14% market share. The weekend result is above Black Phone, Exorcist: Believer, Insidious: Out of the Further, Smile and Evil Dead Rise.

	Spain opened to a near $1M, ranking No. 2 behind local hit La Bola Negra. The weekend result is in line with Exorcist: Believer and Evil Dead Rise, above M3GAN, Black Phone and Smile.

	Brazil’s $800K at 726 screens ranked 3rd in the country behind Verity and local film Minha Amiga, with a healthy 13% share of business. The weekend result is in line with Black Phone and above Smile.

	U.A.E.’s great $700K No. 1 was above Black Phone, Exorcist: Believer, Smile and Evil Dead Rise, and more than double M3GAN.

	Saudi Arabia had an excellent start with $600K, above Evil Dead Burn, Exorcist: Believer, Evil Dead Rise, Insidious: Out of the Further and M3GAN. Other Middle East was strong at $500K, taking the total for the Middle East region to an excellent $1.7M. Great starts in Southeast Asia with the region counting $2.2M from openings in Indonesia ($476K), Taiwan (+$424K), Malaysia ($416K), India ($327K), Philippines ($248K) and Vietnam ($217K). The opening result across the region is above M3GAN, more than double Black Phone and Smile.

	Amazon/MGM Studio’s Verity is third with $30.1M second global weekend ($11.5M domestic, $18.6M int’l at 76 territories). Domestic of $53.9M and international B.O. of $60.1M put this Dakota Johnson, Anne Hathaway, Josh Hartnett thriller at $114M WW. New openings for Verity are CIS region with $237K on 155 screens for a No. 1 rank. Taiwan grossed $140K from 89 screens and opened to No. 7. This is the biggest opening for a Colleen Hoover book adaptation in the country.

Amazon can Danke schön Germany where Verity is still No. 1 with $2.5M at 695 screens, -17% for a $7.2M cume. Other No. 1s included Brazil ($2M at 1,289 screens, -42%, for a near $7M cume), Australia with $1.5M at 500 screens, -39% for a $5.8M cume. 

	Also No. 1: Portugal with $381K from 60 screens, down -40% and a $1.3M cume. Switzerland with $325K at 65 screens, -6% and an $820K tally. Austria with $315K at 69 screens, +4% with an $850K cume, and Hungary with $260K at 75 screens, -15% for a $655K running total. 

	France with $1M at 385 screens, -30%, ranked No. 5 with a $3.1M cume. Mexico with $860K at 915 screens, -40%, ranked No. 4 with a $3M cume. 

	










	Paramount’s The Heart of the Beast had a third worldwide weekend of $21M ($13.9M abroad, $7.1M North America). Domestic’s $49.66M and foreign’s great $81.7M adds up to a $131.3M tally around the world. That’s now ahead of the reported of another Hollywood star and dog survival movie, 2020’s The Call of The Wild starring Harrison Ford which finaled at $111.1M WW. Those countries still moved by this green beret movie are France ($2.1M third weekend at 750 locations, still No. 1 in the market, -29% for a $10.2M cume). Mexico’s third frame is $1.4M at 841 sites, No. 2, -27% for a cume of $7.2M. UK’s $1.1M third frame at 593 locations, ranks No. 3 and down 27% from last week for a $7.2M running cume.

	If it’s a bomb here, then typically it’s a bomb in the rest of the world, and it was clear going into this weekend that Aaron Sorkin’s sequel to his Oscar winning written The Social Network, The Social Reckoning, would have no friends with a $9M WW opening ($5.5M stateside, $3.5M overseas which is lower than the $6M hoped for ala the start of Lionsgate’s Bombshell in like-for-likes). The Mikey Madison and Jeremy Allen White movie was booked in 29 foreign territories on 4,100 screens. Why did it tank? The movie isn’t prescient enough in its recap of the Jan. 6 riots and why Facebook is bad for teens. Everyone nowadays wants to know if AI will kill them in a decade, which gives Luca Guadagnino’s upcoming Artificial more of a pulse.

	Just a bit higher than Social Reckoning is the second weekend of Tom Cruise’s disastrous Digger, with $9.6M WW ($3.2M domestic, $6.4M abroad) for a running global cume on the $160M+ net production of $35.3M ($20.9M int’l, $14.4M domestic). At $50M WW, Digger loses $150M. Social Reckoning was much cheaper, around $40M, so the red ink won’t be so bad.

	We will have more updates tomorrow.

62,2026-10-11,"
					By Liz Shackleton 
pmc-media-editor-2024

	Is the world ready for a giant worm going to work on the subway? Korean director Kim Sung-soo thinks so and is making a film on this very subject, Worm Train, inspired by Franz Kafka’s short story Metamorphosis.

	Kim, whose credits include major Korean blockbusters such as Asura: The City Of Madness and 12.12: The Day, talked about how he’s using Kling AI tools to make the short film on a panel at Busan’s Asian Contents & Film Market (ACFM) today.

	Kling AI, which recently released its 4.0 image generation model, also invited Chinese animation filmmaker Li Wei (Big Fish & Begonia); Harry Hyun, a director and CEO of Korea’s MooAm Co; and Lee Sang-wook, a producer and team manager at Korea’s MBC C&I AI Contents Lab, to present recent projects made using Kling AI tools, as well as discuss the latest trends in this new mode of filmmaking.

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Ann Hui, Busan Camellia Award Recipient, Talks Learning From Women Bosses & Hong Kong New Generation		
			















	News
	



	
		
					Sakha Filmmakers Association & Producers Guild of Korea Pact For Co-Productions		
			







	After presenting a teaser for Worm Train, Kim said AI has changed the way he thinks about filmmaking, but that “one thing never changes, which is what makes a film cinematic”.




			Watch on Deadline		






	“Now everyone has similar tools and anyone can access Kling, but while some will make a masterpiece, others will make a film that is mediocre,” Kim said. “Initially, I thought AI would destroy the industry, but I don’t think so any more, because technology does not replace creativity. The value of images still comes from human intervention.”

	Hyun, who has made AI films including Crypto Man and Genfluencer, expanded on this topic. “I don’t think AI production compared to traditional production is really that different – the difference is efficiency and simplicity – but if you don’t have experienced filmmakers on set, everything can end up looking the same.

	“That’s why AI images may look impressive at first, but not later, because everyone is using the same lighting, same everything,” Hyun continued. “You need an art director who can do the fine tuning, a director and cinematographer who have really studied and learned when to use a close shot, wide shot, how to frame an image. These are long discussions we have on set, they’re not random decisions. Only when you’re working with experts will you be able to create something different with AI – not just cookie cutter deliverables.”

	










	Li Wei, who is making animated feature Born Of The Tide with Kling AI tools, said one useful aspect of AI filmmaking is that results can be seen right away, so it’s easier to get feedback from multiple people on the crew. “There’s going to be a lot of trial and error in AI filmmaking, but effective communication can be used between the various production members, so everyone has a chance to feed back into the production,” Li said.

	Lee presented a teaser of upcoming series Rendering Love, produced by MBC C&I and Barunson E&A, which combines live-action scenes filmed the traditional way with AI generated characters to tell the story of a virtual K-pop group. He observed that different roles and ways of working are emerging in AI production crews.

	“All staff members working on a project must fully understand the technology. That’s the only way to have high quality content. If you’re updating to a new model, the team leaders need to analyse the new features and share that understanding so all staff members are on the same page. The technology is changing constantly and if anyone is lagging behind, overall quality can decrease.”

	The speakers also discussed whether there was a need to introduce industry standards in AI filmmaking, but Kim suggested it was still too early, at least in South Korea. “Creators in Korea have their own ideas and imagination and want to unleash that using AI tools. We need to see where all this is heading before we start talking about regulations and standards.

	“But one thing I’m very aware of as a filmmaker is thinking about whether you’re infringing somebody’s rights. That’s something we need to be conscious of from the early stages.”

	The panel, entitled New Cinematic Possibilities: Reimagining Filmmaking with Kling AI, took place as part of the InnoAsia track at Asian Contents & Market (ACFM), which is running until October 13.

					By Liz Shackleton 
pmc-media-editor-2024

EXCLUSIVE: The Producers Guild of Korea (PGK) and the Sakha Filmmakers Association (SFA) have signed a memorandum of understanding (MOU) aimed at creating a direct route to international co-productions and professional exchange between the Korean and Sakha screen production sectors.

	The Sakha Republic (Yakutia), in Russia’s Far East, has built one of the country’s most procative film industries, making films mostly in the Sakha (Yakut) language and developing a strong local theatrical audience. Its films have been drawing growing attention at festivals and markets across Asia and internationally.

	











	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Netflix's 'The Blue Road' Director Han Jun-hee On Taking Risks With Stories & Shooting In Japan		
			















	News
	



	
		
					AI Tools Bring Efficiencies But Have Limitations, Says Weta CTO Kimball Thurston: ""Tech Companies Should Collaborate More With Creatives""		
			







	The MOU with PGK was signed at the Sakha Filmmakers Association booth during the Asian Contents & Film Market (ACFM) in Busan, with the aim of starting a long-term industry partnership between producers from the two territories.




			Watch on Deadline		






	Under the agreement, PGK and SFA will establish a new framework for co-production between Korean and Sakha producers, aimed at developing projects together and sharing information on public financing available in both territories.

	The partnership will include project showcases, producer-to-producer meetings and B2B sessions designed to identify projects with international potential and move them towards co-production.

	The two organisations will also bring the partnership to major international markets, including ACFM, Hong Kong Filmart and the Cannes Marché du Film, creating new opportunities for Sakha projects to connect with the wider Asian and international industry.

	For PGK, the agreement continues a programme of international partnerships built since the pandemic, alongside MOUs with the Canadian Media Producers Association (CMPA), the Hong Kong Film Production Executives Association (HKFPEA), the Indonesian Film Producers Association (APROFI) and the Brazilian Audiovisual Industry Union (SIAESP).

	PGK President Lee Dong-ha said: “Sakha cinema is one of the most distinctive voices to emerge in Asia in recent years, and it belongs in the conversation among Asian producers. Through this MOU, PGK and SFA open a direct channel between our two production communities – one that we intend to turn into actual co-productions.”

	










	Stepan Burnashev, SFA’s Executive Director, said: “Sakha cinema grew from a small regional community into a strong local film culture because our filmmakers kept telling stories in our own language and from our own perspective.

	“Korea is an important example of how a strong local identity can connect with audiences around the world. This agreement gives our producers a direct working relationship with the Korean industry. We want it to lead not only to exchange, but to projects developed and produced together, and to a lasting connection between our filmmaking communities.”

A portrait doesn’t need to be shot on film to look like it. Our Analog Film Presets Pack is built around a set of distinct film-stock emulations, each one tuned to a different grain structure, color cast, and contrast curve, so a single digital portrait can be pushed in a handful of genuinely different directions without touching another slider.
We shot this portrait set specifically to show the range. The same raw file gets run through each preset in the pack, and the differences aren’t subtle: warmer highlights on one, cooler shadows and heavier grain on another, a flatter and more muted palette on a third. That range is the whole point of buying a pack instead of a single preset.
Different subjects call for different film character. A studio portrait can carry a cleaner, lower-grain emulation, while an outdoor or editorial shoot might want something punchier and grainier. Having the full Analog Film Presets Pack means you’re not locked into one mood for every shoot.
Every preset in the pack works in both Lightroom and Capture One, and like the rest of our presets, they’re built to hold up across Canon, Nikon, Sony, Leica, Panasonic Lumix, Olympus, OM System, and Fujifilm files.
The Analog Film Presets Pack is available from our presets shop, priced between $30 and $40 depending on the bundle you choose."
65,2026-10-11,"This is a Mint Premium article gifted to you.Subscribe to enjoy similar stories.
The economist Robert Solow’s famous 1987 adage about the computer age—it can be seen everywhere except the productivity statistics—also applies to generative artificial intelligence (AI).
The IT revolution did make workers more efficient globally, especially after the internet spawned entirely new business models. The changes, however, arrived in waves, long after the initial investments. They were hard to measure in real time. 
Something similar is going on now. In less than four years since the first large language model was released, annual AI investment is approaching $1 trillion. Yet, where is the evidence that firms using AI are getting more juice out of their human resources, leading to economy-wide gains?

A recent paper by Alex Blumenfeld at the University of California, Berkeley, with Jonathon Hazell at the London School of Economics and two other economists, has tried to fill the gap by putting a number on one crucial area where AI is having the most impact: software engineering.
Suppose there are two e-commerce platforms, but only one is redesigning its app so that Claude or ChatGPT agents can shop as effortlessly as humans. Before long, its job listings will be seeking more software developers skilled at using AI to complete projects faster than was previously possible.
Stock markets already have some sense of which firms are making the best use of AI. As the tech advances, investors will increasingly price the expected gains into the shares of those best placed to profit from it. 
The researchers compare firms’ stock returns with an AI Index, with other factors constant, then examine how the returns vary with spending on programmers’ pay. The result was striking: Between November 2022 and December 2025, AI raised the market’s estimate of the present value of software-engineering productivity by the equivalent of a permanent 32.6% increase.
To arrive at their findings, Blumenfeld et al studied companies ranging from online marketplaces like eBay to electronics firms and travel platforms like Airbnb. 
What they have in common is that software engineering is key to their businesses, rather than being the product itself. To differentiate AI’s effect on productivity, the researchers excluded software companies and firms in the semiconductor supply chain.

The same pattern appears to hold true for some of Asia’s biggest consumer companies. I ran a back-of-the-envelope test, measuring the sensitivity of the US-listed shares of Sony, Coupang and Grab to the Robo Global Artificial Intelligence Index, and comparing the result against their software engineering exposure. 
Without access to payroll data, I used the share of software-related vacancies on company careers pages as a proxy. Think of it as a peek under the hood. A statistical investigation into AI’s productivity gains across Asian companies will need a much broader sample than three handpicked ones. Still, the trio offers a suggestive glimpse. 
Coupang is South Korea’s answer to Amazon; Grab is a Southeast Asian super-app. Sony, a global powerhouse spanning hardware, gaming and Hollywood, defies easy analysis. My model excluded a senior technical program manager for PlayStation from the ranks of coders. Yet, the job description says the role touches every phase of the software-development life cycle. Such cases suggest Sony’s actual exposure to software engineering may be considerably higher than my 12% estimate.
Glitches like this may help explain why we keep underestimating AI’s productivity gains. Improvements will emerge in roles and corners of firms that no one is watching closely. By the time they become obvious, businesses may already be operating very differently. Even my crude exercise points in the same direction as the more rigorous Blumenfeld study: Software development is where the efficiency story is unfolding first. 

The same effect should, in theory, be visible at Chinese tech giants. Alibaba illustrates why it can be hard to spot. The company’s shares move sharply to everything from US semiconductor export restrictions to competition in cloud computing from rivals such as ByteDance’s Douyin, distorting the stock’s relationship with the AI index. But failure to isolate productivity gains doesn’t mean that they don’t exist.
“Software is eating the world,” Marc Andreessen wrote in 2011. He was right. Technology and tech-adjacent companies now account for roughly 95% of the market capitalization of the world’s 10 largest businesses. But software itself is undergoing another revolution. Until now, every line of code had to be written by a human. No longer. “When agents write 90% of the code, engineers can literally do 10 times as much,” Andreessen noted recently.
Official stats may not have caught up yet, but stock prices and job ads suggest the productivity boom has begun. ©Bloomberg
The author is a Bloomberg Opinion columnist covering industrial companies and financial services in Asia.

67,2026-10-11,"By providing your information, you agree to our Terms of Use and our Privacy Policy.
				We use vendors that may also process your information to help provide our services. This site is protected by reCAPTCHA Enterprise and the Google Privacy Policy and Terms of Service apply.
In 2010, “The Social Network” arrived in theaters on a wave of anticipation. Two of the biggest names in movies, director David Fincher and writer Aaron Sorkin, were joining forces to explain the age of interconnectedness.
The timing was perfect. Twitter was gaining steam, YouTube was transforming the way people watched stuff, and Facebook, the subject of “The Social Network,” had established itself as a social media juggernaut, leaving MySpace and other pretenders in the dust. But six years after it launched, Mark Zuckerberg, Facebook’s geeky, 20-something founder, remained an enigma, one that Fincher and Sorkin were determined to unravel. 

 








Related Stories ‘Wildwood’ Review: Laika’s Epic Stop-Motion Wonder Lacks the Magic of the Studio’s Best Movies ‘The Pitt,’ ‘Take Me Home,’ and More Win at The DARCYS 2026 
And the film‘s marketing campaign could not be ignored. Its initial teaser, set to a choir mournfully singing Radiohead’s “Freak” as dozens of Facebook profiles flashed across the screen, is still startlingly innovative, as is its poster, a shot of Jesse Eisenberg (as Zuckerberg) with the words “You Don’t Get to 500 Million Friends Without Making a Few Enemies” superimposed on his face. (Today, Facebook has 3 billion users.)
The film delivered on its promise. Fincher’s cool, penetrating camera captured the shadowy, morally compromised world of Silicon Valley and Harvard’s old-money elitism, while Sorkin’s crackling dialogue kept the film’s deposition-heavy plot humming. And the script grounded the story in themes that were immediately recognizable, despite its exotic settings. 
“What attracted me to [the film] had nothing to do with Facebook,” Sorkin said in an interview with the Writers Guild of America. “The invention itself is as modern as it gets, but the story is as old as storytelling; the themes of friendship, loyalty, jealousy, class, and power.”

 









“The Social Network” was a box office success, as well as an Oscar winner, and it is now considered to be one of the greatest films of the 21st century. That won’t be the fate of its follow-up, “The Social Reckoning,” which bombed in its opening weekend, collecting a mere $5.5 million. Its predecessor earned $22.1 million in its debut and closed out its run with $225 million globally. Sony wisely kept “The Social Reckoning’s” budget at $30 million, meaning it won’t face losses on the scale of a “Digger.” (Ousted Warner Bros. chiefs Michael De Luca and Pamela Abdy probably should have taken a page from Sony’s Tom Rothman, who greenlights ambitious movies while keeping costs low.)
“The Social Reckoning” isn’t just a financial failure, it’s a critical one, too. The reviews were blistering, with many critics bemoaning the fact that Fincher had passed on directing the film, leaving it to Sorkin to slide behind the camera.
“Sorkin doesn’t look beyond the words on the page, and the film itself appears made with heads down,’ The New Yorker’s Richard Brody lamented.
Eisenberg also opted to sit this one out, reportedly because he was worried about glamorizing the tech mogul. He’d become disturbed after fans approached him quoting lines of dialogue from “The Social Network” or asking him to sign cards that read “I’m the CEO bitch.” Instead, he was replaced by Jeremy Strong. That made the film more difficult to market. With only one member of its creative team returning, Sony Pictures shied away from selling it as a sequel, but the studio still sprinkled bits of “The Social Network’s” DNA into promotional materials. There was the title, an obvious nod to the first Zuckerberg film, and ads used bits of Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross’ score. Posters also referenced the “The Social Network’s” marketing campaign, noting “We’re Way Past Making Friends” in similar, blocky script.

 









But there wasn’t the same urgency in the story being told. “The Social Reckoning” focuses less on Zuckerberg, and more on data engineer Frances Haugen’s decision to blow the whistle on how Facebook’s algorithms were promoting political content that radicalized users, as well as the company’s awareness of how its products were contributing to a rise in bullying and teen suicide. Haugen’s decision was courageous and the public had a right to know about the darker side of the tech giant, but “The Social Reckoning” arrives as a much greater threat has emerged from Silicon Valley. 
Artificial intelligence is dominating the headlines, as fears mount about the economic disruptions it will unleash when millions of jobs are replaced by machines, and, graver still, its potential to trigger a world-ending event. It seems like every day a new Cassandra emerges warning of a potential apocalypse, be it an AI mogul or a disillusioned executive. And it’s not like Haugen’s testimony led to lasting change at Facebook. We’re still drowning in a sea of misinformation, with a federal government that has put the lazy in laissez-faire with its regulatory approach.
As IndieWire’s Ryan Lattanzio put it, “‘The Social Reckoning’ argues that the aftermath of their whistleblowing is still somehow felt today. In actuality, living as we do in 2026, it pretty much feels like… no one cared, and nothing changed?”

 









Instead, two other films may emerge to spark the kind of urgent debates that “The Social Reckoning” thought it would inspire. “Musk,” which opened in limited release this week earning roughly $70,000 across four locations, and “Artificial,” a look at Sam Altman and OpenAI that was one of the breakouts of the New York Film Festival.
The four-hour documentary about Elon Musk probably won’t be a blockbuster, but it should factor into the awards conversation with its scathing look at the world’s richest man. As for “Artificial,” it already generated controversy after its original distributor Amazon MGM Studios abruptly dropped it days after the company signed a $50-billion deal with OpenAI. It was later acquired by Neon, which will release it at Christmas. The idea that “Artificial” was too hot to handle could make it irresistible to audiences worried about the way that AI is reshaping our world. 
Or maybe it will cut too close to home. Sometimes we just go to the movies to escape, not to be reminded of how technology that was designed to connect us instead drove us farther apart.
