68,2026-10-11,"Searching...

                The Ethereum treasury firm repurchased 21 million shares while continuing to stack ETH, a two-track strategy management says is about undervaluation            
Share
BitMine Immersion Technologies just spent about $350 million buying itself back.
Executive Chairman Tom Lee says the company repurchased 21 million shares of its own stock. He calls it the largest buyback ever executed by a crypto stock. For a company best known for hoarding Ethereum, it’s a notable shift in where the cash is going.
The repurchases ran from mid-July through August 17, 2026, when the program wrapped up. Over that window, BitMine (NYSE: BMNR) retired 21 million common shares for roughly $350 million.
The pace was uneven. At its peak, weekly buying reached close to 6 million shares, and later company updates kept reporting additional millions.
This wasn’t an improvised move. The purchases fall under a broader share-repurchase authorization that BitMine expanded from $1 billion to $4 billion in April 2026.
The stated logic is straightforward. BitMine’s leadership believes the stock trades below the value of the digital assets sitting on its balance sheet.
Lee has framed the buyback as a defensive success. By his comparison, BMNR fell about 3% while ETH declined approximately 10%.
For a company whose fortunes are tied to Ethereum, that gap is the point. Treasury stocks usually behave like leveraged bets on their underlying asset, amplifying both rallies and selloffs.
Daily. Free. Join 34,000+ readers across crypto, finance, and policy.
We respect your privacy. Unsubscribe anytime.

Lee credits the repurchases as a key reason the stock didn’t fall harder.
None of this replaces BitMine’s core identity. The company launched its Ethereum-focused treasury strategy on June 30, 2025, and has bought ETH on a weekly basis since then.
The result is a stockpile of roughly 6 million ETH. That represents nearly 4.9% of Ethereum’s total supply and makes BitMine the largest corporate Ethereum treasury.
BitMine isn’t just letting those tokens sit idle. About 87% of its holdings are staked through the MAVAN platform, meaning the ETH is locked up to help secure the Ethereum network in exchange for rewards.
Depending on yield levels, BitMine projects annualized revenue of between $250 million and $363 million from this activity.
BitMine has also tapped investors through preferred equity. Its BMNP shares, a 9.5% Series A perpetual preferred, have climbed roughly 24% from their $80 issue price.
At current levels, BMNP yields 9.56%.
For shareholders, retiring 21 million shares means each remaining share represents a slightly larger claim on BitMine’s ETH holdings and staking income.
There are trade-offs worth watching. Every dollar spent on buybacks is a dollar not spent on more ETH, and the right balance depends on how wide the gap between share price and asset value actually is.
The remaining capacity under the $4 billion authorization is the number to track. How much more BitMine chooses to deploy, and when, will show how seriously management means it when it calls the stock undervalued.

69,2026-10-11,"Tokenized Stocks Show Real Demand But Remain Volatile and Illiquid, IMF Finds
Tokenized stocks may be a small market, but their traders aren’t keeping Wall Street hours.
More than half of tokenized equity trading happens outside regular U.S. market hours, according to the International Monetary Fund’s latest Global Financial Stability Report. About 80% of trades involve less than a full share, a sign of strong demand for fractional ownership among retail investors.
Tokenized stocks are blockchain-based tokens that track the shares of publicly traded companies. The IMF studied the five most liquid tokenized U.S. equities, including tokens tracking Tesla, Nvidia, and the S&P 500, across 11 trading venues over 365 days.
The data suggest onchain prices carry real information. When markets reopened, traditional shares absorbed 87% to 99% of the overnight price moves their tokenized counterparts had made, the IMF found.
The drawbacks are just as clear. Tokenized equities were roughly 1.5 times more volatile than the underlying stocks and far less liquid, particularly on decentralized exchanges, where prices strayed further from traditional markets.
The market is also tiny and concentrated: it’s worth about $2.3 billion, and Ondo Finance and Backed Finance’s xStocks account for more than 70% of it. Both issue synthetic tokens that offer price exposure without direct share ownership, according to the IMF.
Given the market’s early stage, the IMF said its findings “should be interpreted with caution,” and that systemic risks remain limited for now. It urged regulators to consider circuit breakers for 24/7 trading and to monitor links to traditional markets more closely. Earlier this year, the IMF warned that tokenized finance and stablecoins could amplify financial crises.
The report lands as U.S. heavyweights race into the tokenization space.
The NYSE plans to reach crypto investors through Blockchain.com, and NYSE owner ICE is working with OKX on 24/7 tokenized stock trading under the SEC’s new innovation exemption.
Securitize has also recently brought tokenized stocks—including Nvidia, Apple and Amazon shares—to Solana, with more blockchain networks to come. Cboe has struck an S&P deal for tokenized options, and Coinbase launched tokenized stocks for non-U.S. users on Base in August.

ABC Rural
Topic:Road Transport Industry
Cooper Creek over the Birdsville Track, in June 2025. (Supplied: Marree Roadhouse)
The Birdsville Track has been closed for 400 days of the past two years.  
The road is a major freight and tourism route cutting through outback South Australia and Queensland. 
Part of the track is expected to reopen next week, while northern sections could remain closed for many months. 
One of Australia's most iconic outback roads has been closed for 400 days out of the past two years due to rain damage, with remote businesses feeling the pinch. 
Stretching more than 500 kilometres, the Birdsville Track connects Birdsville in south-west Queensland, to Marree in far-north South Australia. 
It provides access to local pastoral properties to move cattle, allows stock to flow to the South Australian market and has become increasingly popular with outback tourists. 
The track is largely unsealed, meaning flooding and heavy rain have periodically closed the track over the years — but never for this long. 
Despite the challenges, Lyall Oldfield is ""very happy"" living in the outback. (Supplied: Marree Roadhouse)
Marree Roadhouse owner Lyall Oldfield said his business was ""probably one third of what it normally is"".
""It's just dreadful what's happening with the roads. It's not even a joke.""
Business at the Marree Roadhouse is one third of what it normally is.  (Supplied: Marree Roadhouse)
About 230km north of Marree is the Mungerannie Hotel, which was taken over by new owners in 2023. 
Three years later, the business has not opened all year, with road closures stopping the daily water deliveries it relies on. 
Cattle coming off Clifton Hills Station on the Birdsville Track in South Australia after flooding.  (Supplied: Pete Nunn)
Co-owner Andrew Norman said it was a similar tale in 2025, when there was ""no sort of season whatsoever"".
Mr Norman said the closure north of Mungeranie is having the biggest impact, deterring tourists from making the trip. 
The Birdsville Track has sustained significant damage from rain events.  (Supplied: Department for Infrastructure and Transport)
The owners of the Mungerannie Hotel on the Birdsville Track will now truck 13,000 litres of water to the pub each day, after being told they can no longer use a nearby bore.
""Nobody's really coming up to Mungeranie to turn around and go back,"" Mr Norman said. 
""If they can get that road open just north of us and get the Birdsville Track open, next year should be fine. 
""If the track is open, we'll be open. But without a track, we're not open.""
The SA Department for Infrastructure and Transport hoped to reopen the 200km section between Marree and Mungeranie ""next week"", but the route north of Mungeranie is expected to remain closed for some time.
Large parts of the Birdsville Track remain underwater. (Supplied: Department of Infrastructure and Transport)
Regional north and outback road maintenance manager Juanita Chester said with some northern parts of the Birdsville Track still underwater, it would be ""several more months before [the department] can even consider reopening"".
While the construction of a single-lane access route has been considered, Ms Chester said the local pits where materials are sourced have flooded too. 
The track has sustained damage from several significant rain events in the past 24 months. (Supplied: Department for Infrastructure and Transport)
""They're like a dam when it rains, they fill with water,"" she said.
""If we can't get in there to get material, there's very little we can do short of carting material from a long, long way away, which then impacts the roads.""
The department is assessing the scope and price of the remaining work, which will then require approval before it can begin, and Ms Chester said this would be unlikely in the next two months.

75,2026-10-11,"Sanaa has come under intense bombing since the Yemeni government announced a campaign to take the capital from the Houthis.
Save
Share
Sanaa, Yemen – At midnight on Tuesday, air strikes rattled the Yemeni capital Sanaa, a city that has been mostly spared bombing since a truce was announced between the internationally recognised government and the Houthis in 2023.
The head of Yemen’s Presidential Leadership Council, Rashid al-Alimi, had recently announced a new military offensive was being launched, named Operation Dawn of Yemen, to take Sanaa from the Houthis, who have held the city since September 2014.
Government ground forces remain hundreds of miles away from the capital and embroiled in fighting with the Houthis on Yemen’s west coast and around Taiz.
This means attacks on Houthi positions in Sanaa must come from the air, but the air campaign has caused civilian casualties.
An air strike on Monday hit a location in the Sa’wan area of Sanaa governorate, killing a child and wounding seven other civilians.
The Yemeni government has pledged to continue its attacks until it achieves its objectives, notably the recapture of Sanaa. Regardless of the political opinions of Yemenis in the capital, most fear their city, far from the front lines, will become a war zone once again.
Strong memories remain of the Saudi-led coalition’s Operation Decisive Storm, launched against the Houthis in March 2015, which saw hundreds of civilians in Sanaa killed in air strikes.
Since the start of the new Yemeni government offensive, dozens of strikes have hit the capital, including an attack on Sanaa’s international airport, which killed two workers. Both operations were aimed at Houthi sites.
Ghamadan Abood, a 30-year-old courier and Sanaa resident, said the new wave of attacks on the city has heightened fear among residents.
“When I learned about the new operation on Sunday, my feeling of security shrank,” he told Al Jazeera. “At any time and anywhere, air strikes may hit, and civilians could become the next casualties.”
On Monday, Abood was travelling on his bike when air strikes hit Sa’wan, just two kilometres from where he was. With Houthi military facilities believed to be positioned in the city, and the government sometimes hitting residential areas, the bombing highlights the vulnerability of civilians in Sanaa.
“The strikes could be aimed at missile launch pads, military equipment or facilities, but some of these facilities could be near neighbourhoods, farms or roads. So, innocent people could be killed, injured or left terrified,” Abood said.
Saudi Arabia, which supports the Yemeni government, has also come under repeated missile attacks by the Houthis, including two strikes on King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh, the latest on Saturday killing 12 people.
On Tuesday, the Saudi-led coalition said Sanaa, Saada, and Amran were among the primary sources of Houthi missile threats. The coalition has noted that ongoing military operations are aimed at curbing the group’s ability to launch missiles on the kingdom.
More than 200,000 people have been displaced since fighting in Yemen escalated in recent weeks, according to the International Organization for Migration.
Most of those forced to leave their homes have come from Taiz governorate in southwestern Yemen, where the most intense ground fighting between government and Houthi forces has taken place.
Saleh Ayidh, a 50-year-old resident of Sanaa and father of five, fears the new air strikes will also force people from their homes in the north.
“Today, we see Sanaa as a safe place and distant from the front lines. For me, no place is safe in Yemen, particularly in the north, and war can spread anywhere,” he told Al Jazeera.
At the start of the Saudi-led coalition’s operation against the Houthis in March 2015, Ayidh’s family left the city for a home in the countryside. They lived there for six months, far from the assault on the Yemeni capital.
“Now we do not know what will happen to us. We will decide whether we need to leave the city if the air strikes keep intensifying,” Ayidh said. “Without safety, life is hard.”
The recent air strikes are widely viewed in Sanaa as a sign that war has returned to the capital, prompting residents to stock up on food and fuel.
When Operation Decisive Storm began in 2015, the cost of goods and fuel rose due to disruption to logistics and panic buying. The same trend is being seen again in Sanaa after the recent resumption of hostilities.
Grocery owner Nashwan, 24, has boosted his inventory of essentials such as rice, cheese, milk, biscuits, cooking oil and sugar, before they become too difficult to find.
He also stocked up on petrol, buying 100 litres to use for his minibus if a fuel crisis hits the capital.
“These commodities may become expensive in the coming days. If the war keeps escalating, buying them would cost me more,” Nashwan said. “I do not want to feel the impact of the war immediately.”
Any road closures or a decline in imports caused by the war and a Saudi naval siege on Houthi areas will present major challenges for civilians.
“Rising prices of food and fuel are a real worry for the entire population amid the war and will worsen the humanitarian situation in Yemen,” Wafiq Saleh, executive director of the Taiz Centre for Yemeni-Gulf Studies, told Al Jazeera.
Last week, the UN World Food Programme warned that three-quarters of families in parts of Yemen were going hungry.
There are fears an escalation of the fighting will lead to a more intense food crisis across Yemen, which the WFP has already described as “absolutely devastating”.
Despite the optimism of the internationally recognised government when announcing Operation Dawn of Yemen, few in Sanaa believe it has the ability to recapture the capital from the Houthis any time soon.
Raiman al-Hamdani, a London-based Yemen analyst, said government forces are big on paper, but remain scattered across multiple fronts, while soldiers are using outdated gear.
“They can hold their lines but can’t push the Houthis out of their mountain strongholds,” Raiman told Al Jazeera last week.
Fuad Ali, 40, a university graduate in Sanaa, said the Saudi-backed government has lost the public’s trust, particularly its claim that it could capture the capital after it suffered a series of embarrassing military defeats in recent weeks on the coast.
“The government has failed to seize the coastal Hodeidah province or Al-Jawf desert province. The fight there could be easier given the topography. How can it recapture the mountainous Sanaa?” Ali told Al Jazeera.
Operation Dawn of Yemen will bring more “destruction to the country’s facilities and increase civilian casualties”, he added.
Ali doubts the operation will return the government to Sanaa. But he also believes the government and Saudi-led coalition have learned their lessons from 2015, when huge civilian casualties from air raids drew international condemnation.
“I am still optimistic that Operation Dawn of Yemen will not damage Sanaa as Operation Decisive Storm did,” Ali said.

78,2026-10-11,"Fossil fuels produce 81 percent of global energy. We look at the Middle East’s role and what happens when supplies are cut.

A major energy conference is going ahead in Riyadh from Sunday despite a Houthi attack on the city’s King Khalid International Airport that killed 12 people and injured 309, according to Saudi Arabia’s General Authority of Civil Aviation.
Saudi ⁠Arabia’s Ministry of Energy said on Sunday that the 25th ⁠World Petroleum Council Energy Congress has begun ‌in Riyadh. The 17th International Energy Forum (IEF) Ministerial will be held on the sidelines of the conference, called Riyadh Energy Week, which is to run until Thursday.




But Italy, the cohost of the IEF, will join the meeting via videolink. Nigeria, the other cohost, is sending a representative to the event.
The IEF brings together 68 countries that account for more than 90 percent of global oil and gas supplies and demand. Its members include Saudi Arabia, the United States and Russia. The meeting comes as the US-Israel war on Iran has disrupted energy flows and pushed governments to reassess their energy security.
In this visual explainer, Al Jazeera looks at where the world’s energy comes from, how much is produced in the Middle East and which countries have strategic reserves to fall back on.
Energy heats our homes, fuels our cars, keeps the lights on and runs the factories that make what we buy. It comes from fossil fuels such as oil, coal and gas as well as nuclear power and renewables.
Despite rapid growth in renewables, fossil fuels still account for nearly 81 percent of global energy consumption. Oil is the largest source at 31.4 percent, followed by coal at 25.9 percent and natural gas at 23.5 percent. Traditional biomass, nuclear power and other sources make up the rest.
Production is concentrated in a handful of regions, so disruptions in any of them ripple through global markets.
The Middle East is the largest oil-producing region, and North America is the largest natural gas producer. Russia and Central Asia are also major producers of both.
The Middle East’s importance to energy markets is also about geography. Three narrow waterways connect its producers with consumers around the world.
Before the Iran war, about 27 percent of global seaborne oil trade and almost 20 percent of liquefied natural gas trade transited through the Strait of Hormuz on the eastern side of the Arabian Peninsula.
On the peninsula’s western side, the Bab al-Mandeb strait links the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden, and the Suez Canal connects the Red Sea with the Mediterranean. As the Iran war has spilled over into other areas of the Middle East and Yemen’s civil war has ramped up, traffic has been reduced through both the strait and canal, forcing some energy shipments on longer routes around Africa.
“That’s probably the first time we’ve really seen a major constriction of a chokepoint,” said Richard Matthews, director of consultancy and research at Gibson Shipbrokers, a London-based shipbroking and maritime advisory service.
He told Al Jazeera that what makes the Strait of Hormuz different from other chokepoints is “there is no alternative maritime route. There are some pipelines, but there’s no alternative, which is why it’s been so significant in terms of cargo volume.”
Gulf ports are where much of the region’s energy begins its journey to the rest of the world.
Further down the supply chain, people and businesses are feeling the disruption through the rising cost of essentials.
Countries that rely on oil, gas and fertiliser from the Gulf face higher prices, longer waits for shipments and the need to find other suppliers. Even where deals have kept goods moving, the extra costs are being passed down the chain.
Eritrea and Madagascar are the most dependent on Middle Eastern oil, each sourcing about 90 percent of their supply from the region. They are followed by Pakistan (78 percent), Japan (77 percent) and Kenya (77 percent).
Gas has been even harder to replace than oil because most of it travels as liquefied natural gas from Qatar and the United Arab Emirates through the Strait of Hormuz.
Countries that depend on these shipments are paying more for fuel and electricity and competing for a smaller pool of cargoes. Poorer importers with little storage have struggled the most to find alternatives.
The countries that rely most heavily on Middle Eastern gas are mostly in Asia. South Korea sources 31 percent of the gas it uses from the region, followed by India (29 percent), Pakistan (27 percent) and Taiwan (26 percent).
Countries keep emergency oil stocks for when supplies are cut off or reduced, and these have been the world’s main cushion during the war. That cushion is now wearing thin.
Western countries have little left to release, energy industry leaders warned, and the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at its lowest level since 1982.
“Estimates suggest less than 6 billion barrels of commercial inventories remain today with the vast majority not practically available,” Amin Nasser, head of Saudi Aramco, told the Energy Intelligence Forum in London on Monday.
The International Energy Agency, which coordinates emergency stocks for its members, released a record 400 million barrels of oil in March. It is now preparing to release another 100 million barrels of crude and diesel to ease soaring diesel prices although some of this may be oil from the March release that has yet to reach the market.
A storm in the Gulf of Mexico and attacks in Saudi Arabia are now threatening supplies again, keeping oil prices above $100 a barrel.
Before this year’s releases, China held by far the largest reserves, an estimated 1.4 billion barrels, more than the rest of the list combined. The US was second with 413 million barrels, followed by Japan with 263 million.

79,2026-10-11,"Citroën has unveiled a concept electric version of its classic 2CV, aiming to bring the vehicle to market in 2028 for just €15,000.
For the entire second half of the 20th century, the Citroën 2CV (“Deux Chevaux,” French for “two horses”) held a special place in Europe’s automotive landscape.
The affordable, comfortable, yet underpowered vehicle had enormous popularity, selling millions of units over its production run.
Its cute, quirky look was unique and eye-catching, with its tall, rounded, cartoon-like passenger cabin, jaunty circular headlights, and pop-up windows.
It was a small vehicle, yet still designed to be comfortable for a family of four, with a unique and soft suspension design intended to allow drivers to cross a field in rural France with a basket of eggs without breaking them.
The car ended production in 1990, but well-loved versions of it can still be seen driving in many parts of the world (I’ve even seen a DIY electric conversion here in California).
And now, it’s back with an official electric concept, and one which Citroën says it intends to have for sale and on the road in 2028.
The model was shown in advance of the Paris Motor Show, starting this week.
it retains the general shape of the original Deux Chevaux, though with a less jaunty cockpit, a taller and boxier front hood, much lower wheel arches, roll-down windows, and no rear fender skirts.
So, it’s basically the new, electric-y, modern-y, tech-y concept version of the 2CV. There’s definitely a lot that has changed, and it’s not nearly as unique as the old car was. But the call of nostalgia is still there in spades.
Notably, it does retain several key features: the giant fabric roof which can roll all the way open, the 2CV’s tiny size (the new 2CV concept is in fact four inches shorter than the old 2CV), and an affordable price: Citroën is targeting a starting price of “around €15,000” (just under $17k).
Being small helps keep the price down, and the new 2CV is not just short, it’s also light. Citroën is aiming to keep weight around 1,000kg (2,205lbs), which isn’t just light for an EV, but light for any car (lighter than the current Mazda MX-5, for example).
We don’t know anything about specs yet, but given the badge on the back of the car, we can expect it to be light on power.
The original Deux Chevaux did have more than two horses of power, but not by much – it ranged from 9 to 29hp over the course of its 42-year production run (the name came from France’s vehicle tax system, which had a strange formula for calculating “tax horsepower”).
So expect the EV version to be more powerful than any 2CV ever, but still no speed demon. It will sit between the 8hp Ami and 113hp C3 in terms of vehicle sizes, so that seems like a good upper and lower bound for where we can expect the 2CV to slot in.
Battery size is another open question. The original 2CV was meant as a rural vehicle, allowing four people to drive across the French countryside, including room for their luggage. That would suggest a battery big enough to get you from charger to charger for trips.
But today, a tiny car like this is thought of more as a city car. And those typically need smaller ranges, since they’re not driven on long trips, and chargers aren’t as spread out.
We don’t know which direction Citroën has gone here, but the low wheel arches suggest a vehicle designed for pavement rather than off-roading. Maybe that’s a hint at a more city-focused design, or simply a commentary on how much improvement French roads have seen since 1948.
Or maybe we should look at the competition – similar small urban EVs tend to have ~30kWh batteries and ~250km (~150mi) of range, so perhaps expect something around that.
The interior is cute but spartan, though the vibrant colors of the concept will surely not be brought entirely over to the production version (nor the hanging lights).
But interior photos give us an idea of the direction Citroën is going – a sparse, open interior, seeming to make the most of its small space to allow room for people, instead of additional tech flourishes.
The only tech visible is a small tablet-like screen attached to the steering column, and what looks like a wide driver display across the dash. There’s a removable radio/speaker module on the passenger side.
The basket in the middle may or may not make it to production, potentially in a more sedate form as a covered interior storage space. But in the concept, its design is meant to harken back to the original 2CV’s “basket of eggs” design brief.
That’s not the only reference to eggs – the concept’s door pockets even have cutouts for a half-dozen eggs, keeping them safe in a way that the new 2CV’s much shorter suspension travel is perhaps less suited for than the original’s was.
We don’t know what will happen for the production version yet, but given that Citroën wants to get this car out in 2028, we can’t imagine it will change all that much. As usual, expect some of the more out-there design touches to be moderated before production.
This new European e car class looks promising and 2cv seems like a great one to bring back for it. A real city car bev for €15k is greatly needed.  
Which might not be a bad thing after all. The fewer custom parts Citroën needs for this car, the more it can keep cost down, which is the true essence of what the Deux Chevaux always was: it was the French answer to the Volkswagen Beetle, the everyman’s workhorse vehicle that just did what it needed to do to get you around at a reasonable price.
If Citroën can do that, while still maintaining any amount of the cuteness and quirkiness of the original, it could have an instant classic on its hand.
What do you think of the new Deux Chevaux? Let us know in the comments.

80,2026-10-11,"While companies often seek to list their shares on stock exchanges, there can be instances when they choose to go the other way and withdraw their shares from the exchanges. This process is known as delisting.
Such an event limit a shareholder's ability to buy or sell the specified company's shares through the usual route, leaving them wondering what happens to the shares they hold and whether they can recover their investment.
The process of delisting securities for any company is governed by the markets regulator, Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI).
A listed company’s shares can be delisted from stock exchanges for several reasons. These include a company having insufficient market capitalisation, violating regulatory norms or facing financial distress leading it to file for bankruptcy, according to a blog post by Groww.
Delisting can also be voluntary, when a company or its promoters decide to withdraw the shares from the stock exchanges. This happens when a company merges with another entity, undergoes amalgamation or is going through a long period of non-performance.
When a company delists its shares, investors continue to own the shares they hold, but they can no longer trade them through the stock exchange (NSE or BSE or both) where the shares were listed.
Shareholders can still sell their holdings through the over-the-counter market, which involves finding a buyer outside a recognised stock exchange.
The impact on shareholders also depends on whether the delisting is voluntary or involuntary. The process, exit options available and implications for investors can differ depending on the circumstances of the delisting.
Here are your money recovery options if it's a voluntary delisting:
When a company voluntarily opts for delisting, it usually offers its investor a buyback at a premium price. This can result in a significant gain only if the investor acts on time as it is a temporary opportunity. Once the buyback window closes, the price of the stock is likely to drop, as per the blog post.
Here are your money recovery options if it's a involuntary delisting:
A delisted stock can sometimes be allowed to trade on the stock exchanges again only if SEBI permits it. If the stock was delisted voluntarily, then the company has to wait for five years to get relisted again.
If a company has been delisted compulsorily, it will have to wait for 10 years before they can be listed again on the exchanges.

81,2026-10-11,"What a week of college football. Week 6 didn’t disappoint, and there was wire-to-wire action all throughout the day on Saturday. Nebraska appeared to have Indiana under wraps, but the Hoosiers’ massive fourth quarter allowed them to stay undefeated on the season.
Both Dylan Raiola and Arch Manning made some crucial mistakes, allowing Oregon and Texas, respectively, to get taken down. Kentucky took down LSU in overtime, and USC got a much-needed win over Penn State.
Then the big game didn’t disappoint.
Georgia and Alabama went down to the wire, with the Bulldogs coming out on top with a 20-17 win. With the Longhorns going down, Georgia is likely going to be No. 1 on Sunday.
Here is whose stock has gone up and whose stock has gone down.
MORE: ESPN College GameDay Week 7 location announced, breaks 17-year hiatus
We wondered if the hurricane was going to mess with this game, but it didn’t, and the game lived up to the hype. Georgia would win in Tuscaloosa, 20-17, and the Bulldogs’ ground game was the big winner. Shockingly, star running back Nate Frazier didn’t play, but he didn’t need to with both Dwight Phillips Jr and Bo Walker leading the way for the ‘Dogs. 
Keelon Russell played great for Alabama, throwing for 185 yards and a score but he did have a costly fumble in the game. However, the game-changer was the no-pass interference call on Alabama’s last drive of the game. It wasn’t called and Kalen DeBoer wasn’t happy. 
And Georgia walked away with a massive win. 
Nebraska entered the game against Indiana 5-0, but there were many questions about who the Huskers had beaten. It didn’t appear to matter early in the game, as Nebraska looked much better than Indiana. Quarterback Anthony Colandrea was running all over the Hoosiers’ defense, and Nebraska found itself up 17-6 in the fourth quarter. 
But the rest of the quarter belonged to Indiana, and the Hoosiers would go on to win, 20-17. The loss to Indiana now gives Nebraska 31 straight losses against AP Top 25 opponents. The Huskers’ defense allowed two long touchdown receptions by Nick Marsh and Nebraska couldn’t capitalize on offense. 
Although Nebraska had 391 yards compared to Indiana’s 328, along with 25 first downs to the Hoosiers’ 13 — Indiana won in the only stat that matters. 
It was supposed to be a high-scoring affair in Eugene, but it turned out that the defenses dominated. But it was UCLA’s defense that really dominated. Oregon didn’t have starting quarterback Dante Moore, who sat out this game after sustaining a concussion two weeks ago against USC. 
Dylan Raiola drew the start, and the Ducks’ offense had no answers for UCLA. The Bruins forced seven turnovers in the game and UCLA walked away with a 17-15 win. The Bruins remain undefeated at 5-0, and UCLA’s schedule isn’t all that daunting. 
Quarterback Nico Iamaleava exited the game and didn’t return. His status will loom large, but running back Wayne Knight carried the load, rushing for 103 yards and a score in the win. 
Florida’s 52-28 win over Ole Miss seems like an eternity ago. That’s because the Gators were humbled last week against Missouri, after moving up to No. 8 in the AP Top 25 poll. And this week, at home against South Carolina, the Gators lost to a two-win Gamecocks team, 38-19. 
It’s clear Jon Sumrall’s defense is a big issue, and it’s going to be hard to win consistently in the SEC with a bad defense. South Carolina quarterback LaNorris Sellers threw for 279 yards and four touchdowns in the game. Offensively, running back Jadan Baugh was stifled once again. 
Baugh was among the best running backs in the country through four weeks, but in the last two weeks, Baugh has run for 13 and 259 yards, respectively. 
One week after Pitt head coach Pat Narduzzi told AP Voters to wake their a**** up, his team fell to North Carolina. The Tar Heels won at the No. 25 Pitt Panthers, 21-17. Both teams had two turnovers, and the Panthers dominated with 369 total yards compared to North Carolina’s 269, but Bill Belichick’s team won. 
In fact, it’s Belichick’s first win over a ranked opponent, and in typical Belichick fashion, his post-game answer wasn’t all that interesting. 
“Just get ready for Duke,” Belichick said of the win over Pitt. He will need his quarterback to play a little cleaner to beat Duke, which suffered its first loss of the season on Saturday to Georgia Tech. 
Dylan Raiola came into the game against USC and played tremendously to propel the Ducks to victory. With Moore sitting another game, it was Raiola who drew the start, and it was supposed to be a big game for the Nebraska transfer, playing against a poor UCLA passing defense. 
But the Bruins dominated, and Raiola was pulled after going 6-for-19 for 43 yards and two interceptions. He also lost a fumble. So Dan Lanning turned to third-string Brock Thomas to finish the game. 
And then there is Arch Manning. He threw for 260 yards and three touchdowns. But Manning’s fourth quarter was terrible. The potential No. 1 pick in the 2027 NFL Draft allowed Oklahoma to get back into the game after tossing a pick-six, and then throwing a late touchdown.
His second interception allowed the Sooners to walk off with a 47-yard field goal to win 27-24. The Red River Rivalry was great, but that was another poor performance from Manning.

83,2026-10-11,"This 1995 Suzuki Cappuccino is a Japanese-market kei-car that was imported to the US in June 2026. The car is powered by a turbocharged 658cc inline-three linked with a three-speed automatic transmission, and it wears an older repaint in blue. Equipment includes a removable hardtop, Dark Gray and Charcoal cloth upholstery, Alatis Zero-1 wheels, a cassette radio, and R134a-based air conditioning. Recommended work includes replacing the spark plug wires, water pump, and upper radiator hose. This Cappuccino shows 88k kilometers (~55k miles) and is now offered at no reserve in California with a Virginia title in the seller’s name.

Finished from the factory in Mercury Silver Metallic (1VN), the car was repainted in its current shade of blue in Japan. The sale includes a multi-piece removable hardtop, and signs of wear in the paintwork are shown in the gallery below.

The 14″ Alatis Zero-1 wheels are mounted with Bridgestone winter tires. Braking is provided by vacuum-assisted discs at each corner.

The right-hand-drive cabin features bucket seats upholstered in Dark Gray and Charcoal cloth, and interior appointments include a cassette radio, power windows, lockable glovebox storage, and R134a-based air conditioning. Corrosion is noted in the right door sill, and imperfections in the upholstery, dashboard, and door panels are shown in the gallery below.

The three-spoke steering wheel frames a 140-km/h speedometer, a tachometer with an 7,500-rpm redline, and gauges for coolant temperature and fuel level. The five-digit odometer shows 88k kilometers (~55k miles).

The seller attributes an intermittent non-start condition to the spark plug wires, and recommended work includes replacing them along with the water pump and upper coolant hose, which is torn. An oil change was performed in December 2025. Spots of corrosion are noted in the engine bay and on the muffler.

Power is routed to the rear wheels through a three-speed automatic transmission.
The Virginia title lists the Odometer Brand as “Actual.”
The car is located in California, but it is titled in Virginia. It has not attempted a California smog check, and it has not been inspected by the Bureau of Automotive Repair. It cannot be sold to an in-state, private-party buyer.

84,2026-10-11,"Emergency

中文新闻
BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
Topic:Oil and Gas
Liquefied natural gas ships from Curtis Island in Queensland. (Supplied: Photopia Studio)
Gas market players say the federal government's draft gas reservation scheme has been weakened by loopholes.
They argue that changes to the policy favour the Santos-led GLNG venture, which has been blamed for rising prices on the east coast.
The government is expected to release legislation for the scheme in the coming weeks.
A giant LNG plant blamed by critics for driving up east coast gas prices could largely or totally escape a federal scheme designed to protect local buyers, observers have warned.
The Albanese government last month released a draft plan to force gas exporters to set aside up to 20 per cent of their production for the domestic market in a bid to force prices lower.
But analysts and industry players say the draft is full of loopholes that will allow some exporters to reduce or even eliminate their exposure.
They point to the Gladstone LNG project, a plant on Curtis Island in Queensland that is operated by Adelaide-based Santos, as the exporter most likely to benefit.
An ABC investigation revealed GLNG had sucked up and exported the equivalent of 20 per cent of eastern Australia's domestic gas demand over the past 10 years.
This was because the venture built an LNG plant with a capacity of 7.8 million tonnes a year despite not having nearly enough gas of its own to fill it.
By contrast, the other two LNG projects in Queensland — run by global super-major Shell and Australian company Origin along with US firm ConocoPhillips — had overwhelmingly been supplying the local market in net terms.
Paul Farrow is unsympathetic to Santos. (ABC News: Kit Mochan)
Australian Workers' Union (AWU) national secretary Paul Farrow said ""Santos-sized loopholes"" threatened to turn the government's reservation plans into a joke.
Mr Farrow said that, compared with earlier iterations of the plan, the draft had been watered down in significant ways that appeared to favour GLNG above all others.
""Australian industry and Australian consumers deserve to derive an advantage from Australia's gas because it's our sovereign wealth,"" Mr Farrow said.
""The government is so close to getting it right.
""But the exposure draft, as it stands, contains a bunch of Santos-sized loopholes.
""That's a major, major worry.""
Curtis Island in Gladstone is home to three gas plants that export Australian gas. (Supplied: Nearmap)
Under the draft policy, the Australian Energy Regulator would be able to reduce an exporter's supply obligation below 20 per cent, while the minister could cut it to zero.
But critics say a raft of other provisions in the bill could enable Santos and its GLNG partners to slip the net themselves.
They say the draft, unlike earlier iterations, effectively allows GLNG to count contract extensions as ""existing contracts"" that are exempt from consideration.
Crucially, this would allow GLNG to extend a supply agreement with South Korea's KOGAS for five years from 2030.
The current deal is for 3.5 million tonnes of LNG a year, an amount equivalent to about 40 per cent of annual east coast gas demand.
Observers also say the draft will give rise to ""take-or-pay"" deals that allow Santos to generate paper sales to the domestic market without actually supplying any gas.
And they argue the government has given too much ground to Santos over questions about whether the company can ""reasonably"" find enough gas without breaking export contracts.
Mr Farrow said the provision should require Santos to explore all practical options, such as buying gas from other providers in the local market, substituting LNG cargoes from its international operations or using flexibility in its contracts to reduce overseas sales.
Instead, he said the draft appeared to allow Santos itself to set those terms, which he argued the company would do so however it liked.
""If a massive project like GLNG wants to claim that it simply doesn't have enough gas to share with the Australian market, it should have to prove it's actually explored all practical options to get hold of that gas,"" Mr Farrow said.
""These options might incur a small cost for Santos' shareholders.
""That's OK.
The government hopes its reservation plans can encourage more gas wells. (ABC News: Hamish Harty)
As well as these provisions, Mr Farrow said the draft contained other clauses that would allow GLNG off the hook.
Among them was a so-called ""additional gas"" test.
While Mr Farrow said the test was theoretically supposed to encourage new production and increase supplies, in reality he said it was likely to be gamed.
He said an exporter might simply be able to buy a company supplying the local market, count its production as ""additional"" and even export volumes that would have stayed in Australia.
On top of this, the AWU and others have also noted the draft does not carry forward reductions from a producer to future years, meaning deficits may be extinguished.
The ABC contacted Santos and federal Resources Minister Madeleine King for comment.
The widening gap between the supplies taken out of the domestic market by GLNG and those provided by the other two Queensland producers has led to growing tensions that have spilled into the open.
Last month, Dan Clark from Origin's and ConocoPhillips's Australia Pacific LNG project took the rare step of publicly, albeit obliquely, singling out the GLNG venture.
In an opinion piece, Mr Clark said exporters taking gas out of the local market should ""first have to contribute at home with no carve-outs, leave passes or exemptions"".
He also noted domestic prices had fallen after ""one exporter"", widely interpreted as GLNG, ""chose to reduce domestic purchases"".
Santos chief executive Kevin Gallagher, in turn, hit out at his rivals.
""It would appear that I'm living rent-free in a few of the heads of our competitors around Gladstone at this point in time,"" Mr Gallagher said last month.
Kevin Gallagher is known as a formidable lobbyist. (ABC News: Felicity James)
Adding to the drama, China has taken the step of voicing its concerns about the changes that are being made to the reservation plan.
The Australian Embassy of the People's Republic of China, in a submission to the government, called on Canberra to ensure its interests were treated fairly compared with those of other countries.
Through its state-owned oil and gas producers Sinopec and CNOOC, China is a major owner — and customer — of both Asia Pacific LNG (APLNG) and Queensland Curtis LNG (QCLNG).
GLNG counts among its financial underwriters two other Australian trading partners: South Korea through KOGAS and Malaysia via its oil and gas arm PETRONAS.
""We hope that the draft legislation will uphold the principle of fairness and apply the relevant requirements consistently to all LNG exporters, while ensuring equitable treatment of Australia's trading partners and foreign investors,"" the Chinese embassy wrote.
Saul Kavonic says GLNG has always been the problem child of east coast gas exporters. (ABC News: West Matteeussen)
MST Financial oil and gas analyst Saul Kavonic said China's warnings were extraordinary and unprecedented, and that, until recently, Beijing did not have a problem with the reservation idea.
Mr Kavonic said any support the policy enjoyed would crumble if the government failed to design a scheme that was equitable and fair.
He said the government seemed to be failing that test and had put forward a proposal that was closer to the ""spot restriction policy"" taken to the last federal election by the Coalition.
""Whether Queensland LNG projects share the domestic reservation burden equally, or if one gets special treatment and protections, remains one of the most contested areas of the policy,"" Mr Kavonic said.
""[It] presents wide-ranging ramifications for how and if the policy will work.
""Special treatment would see the policy function less like a true reservation policy and more like a spot cargo restriction policy.""

In September, Samsung updated its Galaxy Tab lineup with the new Galaxy Tab S12 Series. These tablets focus on high-end performance, huge battery life, and the latest Galaxy AI functionalities. Of the two new options, the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is the best tablet Samsung has ever made, with an ultra-thin design, 23 hours of battery life, a 3D Vapor Chamber, and an IP68 rating for water and dust resistance. In other words, this tablet, which starts at $1,399, represents what's best on the Android tablet market. However, if you're looking for something that fits your budget, you can go with something like the Samsung Galaxy Tab S12+, or even other interesting options from brands like TCL and Lenovo.
The selected tablets are cheaper than Samsung's latest flagship, but they can still meet most users' expectations. Each tablet has unique features that could make it a strong replacement for the Tab S12 Ultra, even if it isn't as powerful. Still, as companies like Google have completely dismissed their tablet business and OnePlus has moved away from the U.S. and European markets, Android customers are left with fewer options.
Since the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is so pricey that even Apple's latest iPad Pro M5 could be a cheaper option, we'll focus on the Android counterparts, which are great for those who also have Android smartphones or non-Apple computers.
Announced in September alongside the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra, the Samsung Galaxy Tab S12+ is the younger sibling of this massive high-end tablet. Priced slightly lower at $1,199, it also remains in the remium tier of the Android tablet ecosystem—which seems dominated by Samsung. Under the hood, this tablet features a MediaTek Dimensity 9500 chipset found in the Ultra model, a minimum of 12GB of RAM, and a huge 10,600mAh battery that can last for up to 21 hours.
The tablet is fairly similar to its older sibling. It features a stunning 12.6-inch Dynamic AMOLED 2X display with a sharp 2800 by 1752 resolution, which is great for media consumption and for creative people to draw, edit, and multitask. Focused on power users, professional illustrators, and creators, the Galaxy Tab S12+ includes an integrated S Pen, so you don't have to carry a separate device.
Those not willing to pay an extra $200 for the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra can rest assured that the Plus model will deliver most of the features expected for this model, including a fast processor, long software support of at least seven years (unlike a few previous models), and other perks. The only real compromise is trading the Ultra's massive 14.6-inch display for a slightly more manageable 12.6-inch option, which many users might see as a benefit rather than a downgrade.
Released in September, Lenovo's Yoga Tab (Gen 2) enters the market as a true heavyweight contender in the high-end productivity space and for streaming users. Starting at $729, it offers a much better price than current premium tablets, while still maintaining an impressive specification sheet. Lenovo's Yoga Tab (Gen 2) features an 11-inch 4K display with a 144Hz refresh rate and a sharp 2560 by 3840 pixels resolution. With a big 11,000mAh battery, it runs Android 17 and is ready for the most demanding days.
Lenovo has designed the Yoga Tab (Gen 2) for creative professionals, digital artists, and entertainment enthusiasts who demand visual fidelity for their workflows and media consumption. One of the tablet's most compelling features is Lenovo's commitment to longevity: it promises seven major Android operating system upgrades and security support through 2033.
For customers unwilling to spend $1,399 on a tablet, the Lenovo Yoga Tab (Gen 2) is a masterclass in value and an alternative premium option. By saving up $600, users still have a beautiful 4K display, exceptional long-lasting software, and great specs, as this tablet features a power-efficient MediaTek Dimensity 9500s processor, up to 12GB of RAM, and up to 256GB of storage, with the possibility of expanding it to up to 2TB via a microSD card.
Hitting the market in spring 2026, Motorola's Moto Pad focused on what an affordable tablet should be. Priced at $249, you could buy this tablet for your whole family for less than the Samsung Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra. Still, it offers important features for casual customers. The Moto Pad features a premium aluminum unibody design with an 11-inch IPS LCD, 2.5K resolution, and a 90Hz refresh rate. It's powered by the MediaTek Dimensity 6300 chipset, 8GB of RAM, and 128GB of internal storage. Customers can also expand storage via a microSD slot up to 2TB. A 7,040mAh battery also helps to keep the tablet running all day.
For media consumption, the Motorola Moto Pad features quad speakers tuned by Dolby Atmos, which should deliver rich, room-filling sound. What also makes this tablet special is its standalone 5G connectivity out of the box, a feature usually absent at this price point. Motorola designed this tablet for students, frequent travelers, and media lovers who want an affordable device for everyday use and casual web browsing.
That said, if the exorbitant $1,399 cost of the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra seems completely unjustifiable for your everyday use, the Moto Pad could be the perfect option for those willing to get a newer tablet, as it has a great display, improved audio capabilities, and mobile network freedom for a lot less than most companies charge users just to have the possibility of 5G.
Released in 2025, the TCL Nxtpaper 11 Plus remains one of the most interesting tablets of 2026 because of its unique display technology. Originally priced at $369.99, you can find it around $227.98 on Amazon. What makes TCL's tablet unique is the fact that it abandons the traditional glossy screen in favor of visual comfort. With an 11.5-inch IPS display, a 1440 by 2200 resolution, and a 120Hz refresh rate, it's coated in TCL's proprietary Nxtpaper technology for a matte-textured display. As a result, customers get drastically reduced blue light and glare, mimicking the comfortable, strain-free experience of reading on paper or an e-ink reader while still retaining full color.
Powered by MediaTek's Helio G100 processor, 8GB of RAM, and 256GB of storage, this tablet features an 8,000mAh battery with 33W fast charging. TCL made this tablet for voracious readers, students who often rely on digital textbooks, and users who frequently suffer from eye fatigue when staring at standard reflective displays for long hours.
If you don't want to pay at least $1,399 for a tablet and don't care about the shiniest specs, the TCL Nxtpaper 11 Plus can still be a great option for anyone focused on reading, taking notes, or browsing the web. By choosing TCL's tablet, you won't get the fastest tablet or one with an out-of-this-world OLED display, but you will still get a product engineered to protect your eyes and deliver a comfortable reading experience.
Last but not least, those looking for a tablet can find a great option in the Samsung Galaxy Tab A11+ instead of the pricier Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra. This device is the premier entry-level option for customers committed to the Galaxy ecosystem. If you already have a Samsung Galaxy smartwatch, Galaxy Buds, and perhaps a Galaxy Watch, then your next step could be the Tab A11+. Priced around $299.99, it lowers the barrier for those looking for a tablet and Samsung's polished software environment. Featuring an 11-inch LCD and a 90Hz refresh rate, users get smooth navigation during everyday usage.
The Samsung Galaxy Tab A11+ features a power-efficient MediaTek Dimensity 7300 processor, up to 8GB of RAM and 256GB of storage. A 7,040mAh battery powers this device, with 25W wired charging. Samsung designed this tablet for budget-conscious families, casual multitaskers, and everyday users who simply need a reliable screen for social media, YouTube, and light productivity tasks.
This tablet is the smartest way to stay in the Samsung ecosystem while protecting your wallet. The Galaxy Tab A11+ gets Samsung's full OneUI experience and the company's commitment to seven major Android OS updates. Users also get cross-device synchronization across other Galaxy products without spending over $1,000 to enjoy the company's ecosystem. While this tablet doesn't come with a built-in S Pen, users can improve the experience by getting a generic stylus for drawing, writing, and taking notes.
The Android tablet segment does not seem very enticing. In a market dominated by Apple, which accounted for over 50% of total global sales in the first quarter of 2024, Samsung remains the second-largest vendor, with over 24% of tablets sold. That said, while we could've added a bunch of Samsung tablets to this list as cheaper options for the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra — after all, you could get last year's Tab S11, a Tab S10, and so on –, we also thought it would be interesting to give customers some other options from other brands.
On the cheaper side, these other tablets still offer great value, and some options, like TCL's tablet, give users a new way to think about a tablet: as a good e-reader and university-friendly option without costing over $1,000. With the recent memory crisis driving manufacturers to raise prices, finding a good tablet at a reasonable price can be challenging, but this list helps by offering recent releases from trusted brands reviewed by thousands of customers.
While BGR doesn't necessarily review each of these tablets, our staff has expertise with these brands, including hands-on time with TCL's Nxtpaper options. We also encourage readers to consider alternatives, including Apple's iPad lineup. Rumors suggest a new iPad mini with an OLED display is in the works, and the company recently updated the iPad Pro and iPad Air tablets, which compete head-to-head with Samsung's best."

File: Nigerian Exchange trading floor
Kindly share this story:
As the Nigerian financial markets continue to navigate shifts in investor sentiment and macroeconomic adjustments, stakeholders closely monitor weekly trading flows, institutional participation, and corporate disclosures, JIDE AJIA reports.
 The Nigerian Exchange experienced a downturn during the trading week ended on Friday, October 9, 2026, as broader market indicators pulled back. Investors traded a total of 2.715 billion shares worth N158.280bn in 209,986 deals, reflecting a shift in market momentum compared to the previous week.
Market participants navigated a dynamic trading landscape characterised by sector-specific movements, high-profile corporate listings, and capital reconstructions.
Sector and market activity 
Trading activity was heavily anchored by the Financial Services Industry, which led the activity chart by volume with 2.005 billion shares valued at N82.924bn traded across 94,483 deals. This sector alone accounted for 73.84 per cent of total equity turnover volume and 52.39 per cent of its total value.
The Consumer Goods Industry followed in second place with a turnover of 152.345 million shares worth N11.425bn in 20,926 deals, while the ICT Industry captured third place with 117.296 million shares worth N15.256bn in 22,556 deals. Individual market heavyweights Access Holdings Plc, Zenith Bank Plc, and United Bank for Africa Plc drove a significant portion of this volume, combining for 1.020 billion shares worth N50.520bn in 29,382 deals.Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources
Index movements, price variations        
The benchmark NGX All-Share Index and market capitalisation both depreciated by 0.97 per cent to close the week at 248,363.55 and N161.260tn respectively. Most sector indices followed a similar downward trajectory, with the notable exception of the NGX Industrial Goods Index, which managed a slight appreciation of 0.02 per cent.
Market breadth closed negative as twenty-four equities appreciated in price, down from forty-four in the previous week, while fifty-four equities depreciated, and sixty-eight remained unchanged. Livestock Feeds Plc led top gainers with a 47.75 per cent increase, moving from 8.90 to 13.15 naira, whereas Critical Minerals Financing Corp Plc led decliners after sliding 20.27 per cent from 4.49 to 3.58 naira.
Corporate actions restructured
Corporate milestones shaped a busy week for regulatory listings and structural adjustments on the Exchange. The Federal Government of Nigeria officially listed its September 2026 FGN Savings Bonds on October 7, 2026, featuring two-year and three-year instruments with coupons of 14.12 per cent and 15.12 per cent, respectively.
Additionally, Guinea Insurance Plc expanded its equity structure by listing over 6.30 billion ordinary shares arising from a private placement at 1.45 naira per share, pushing its total paid-up capital to 17.45 billion shares.
In major structural updates, the suspension on Multi-Trex Integrated Foods Plc was lifted following the submission of overdue financial statements, while Sterling Financial Holdings Company Plc successfully executed its share capital reconstruction by delisting its old shares and listing 6,850,233,171 newly reconstructed ordinary shares of 50 Kobo each at 77.00 naira per share.
Foreign investment trends 
   
Foreign investors accounted for just 4.89 per cent of equity transactions on the Nigerian Exchange in August 2026, marking the lowest monthly participation share recorded during the year as foreign trading contracted to N62.03bn. According to the NGX Domestic and Foreign Portfolio Investment Report for August 2026, total monthly equity transactions fell 46.38 per cent to 1.27 trillion naira from N2.37tn in July.
Despite this monthly contraction, turnover remained 39.75 per cent higher than the N908.4bn recorded in August 2025. Foreign equity transactions dropped 53.23 per cent to N62.03bn from N132.62bn, outpacing a 45.98 per cent decline in domestic trading. Domestic investors dominated market activity with approximately N1.21tn in transactions, nearly 19 times the value handled by foreign participants.
For the first eight months of 2026, domestic investors drove 89.77 per cent of total equity turnover, growing their trading value by 117.64 per cent to N11.89tn compared to N5.46tn during the same period in 2025. The August slowdown was primarily driven by domestic institutional investors, whose transactions fell 60.39 per cent to N654.61bn from N1.65tn, accounting for roughly 90.9 per cent of the total monthly market turnover reduction.
Conversely, retail trading showed resilience by dipping just 5.08 per cent to N552.88bn, which elevated the retail share of domestic transactions to 45.79 per cent.
Overall, domestic investors closed August with purchases of N615.83bn against sales of N591.66bn, yielding a net buy position of N24.17bn.

The Consumer Goods Industry followed in second place with a turnover of 152.345 million shares worth N11.425bn in 20,926 deals, while the ICT Industry captured third place with 117.296 million shares worth N15.256bn in 22,556 deals. Individual market heavyweights Access Holdings Plc, Zenith Bank Plc, and United Bank for Africa Plc drove a significant portion of this volume, combining for 1.020 billion shares worth N50.520bn in 29,382 deals.Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources
Index movements, price variations        
The benchmark NGX All-Share Index and market capitalisation both depreciated by 0.97 per cent to close the week at 248,363.55 and N161.260tn respectively. Most sector indices followed a similar downward trajectory, with the notable exception of the NGX Industrial Goods Index, which managed a slight appreciation of 0.02 per cent.
Market breadth closed negative as twenty-four equities appreciated in price, down from forty-four in the previous week, while fifty-four equities depreciated, and sixty-eight remained unchanged. Livestock Feeds Plc led top gainers with a 47.75 per cent increase, moving from 8.90 to 13.15 naira, whereas Critical Minerals Financing Corp Plc led decliners after sliding 20.27 per cent from 4.49 to 3.58 naira.
Corporate actions restructured
Corporate milestones shaped a busy week for regulatory listings and structural adjustments on the Exchange. The Federal Government of Nigeria officially listed its September 2026 FGN Savings Bonds on October 7, 2026, featuring two-year and three-year instruments with coupons of 14.12 per cent and 15.12 per cent, respectively.
Additionally, Guinea Insurance Plc expanded its equity structure by listing over 6.30 billion ordinary shares arising from a private placement at 1.45 naira per share, pushing its total paid-up capital to 17.45 billion shares.
In major structural updates, the suspension on Multi-Trex Integrated Foods Plc was lifted following the submission of overdue financial statements, while Sterling Financial Holdings Company Plc successfully executed its share capital reconstruction by delisting its old shares and listing 6,850,233,171 newly reconstructed ordinary shares of 50 Kobo each at 77.00 naira per share.
Foreign investment trends 
   
Foreign investors accounted for just 4.89 per cent of equity transactions on the Nigerian Exchange in August 2026, marking the lowest monthly participation share recorded during the year as foreign trading contracted to N62.03bn. According to the NGX Domestic and Foreign Portfolio Investment Report for August 2026, total monthly equity transactions fell 46.38 per cent to 1.27 trillion naira from N2.37tn in July.
Despite this monthly contraction, turnover remained 39.75 per cent higher than the N908.4bn recorded in August 2025. Foreign equity transactions dropped 53.23 per cent to N62.03bn from N132.62bn, outpacing a 45.98 per cent decline in domestic trading. Domestic investors dominated market activity with approximately N1.21tn in transactions, nearly 19 times the value handled by foreign participants.
For the first eight months of 2026, domestic investors drove 89.77 per cent of total equity turnover, growing their trading value by 117.64 per cent to N11.89tn compared to N5.46tn during the same period in 2025. The August slowdown was primarily driven by domestic institutional investors, whose transactions fell 60.39 per cent to N654.61bn from N1.65tn, accounting for roughly 90.9 per cent of the total monthly market turnover reduction.
Conversely, retail trading showed resilience by dipping just 5.08 per cent to N552.88bn, which elevated the retail share of domestic transactions to 45.79 per cent.
Overall, domestic investors closed August with purchases of N615.83bn against sales of N591.66bn, yielding a net buy position of N24.17bn.

91,2026-10-11,"Most Widely Read Newspaper
L-R: Vice President, Association of Issuing Houses of Nigeria, Gbadebo Adenrele;
Group Chairman of the Nigerian Exchange Group, Umaru Kwairanga; Executive Secretary, Wakeel Olawale; Secretary, Administration, Onyebuchim Obiyemi; Secretary, Treasury, Taiwo Olatunji; and President, Kemi Awodein, during the AIHN 30th Anniversary Award Dinner in Lagos recently. Photo: Plexus
Kindly share this story:
The Nigerian Exchange Group is targeting a market capitalisation of N300 trillion and pushing for more government-linked companies to list on the exchange as the capital market seeks a larger role in driving Nigeria toward a $1 trillion economy.
Speaking at the 30th anniversary and annual awards dinner of the Association of Issuing Houses of Nigeria in Lagos, Chairman of NGX Group, Umaru Kwairanga, highlighted the need to attract more retail investors, deepen market participation, and expand investment opportunities. He noted that market capitalisation had risen from below N50 trillion three years ago to more than N160 trillion, reflecting the market’s growth and its capacity to mobilise capital for national development.
“Market capitalisation has risen from below N50 trillion three years ago to over N160 trillion, and we are aiming for N300 trillion,” Kwairanga said.
To achieve this, the exchange has urged President Bola Tinubu’s administration to encourage more state-linked entities, particularly in the energy and technology sectors, to list on the market. Kwairanga also identified the ongoing initial public offering of the Dangote Refinery as a major catalyst for expanding domestic retail participation. The offer targets 10 million investors, a shift that could significantly boost engagement in a market that has recorded fewer than six million investors since Nigeria’s independence. 
Furthermore, the NGX chairman revealed that the exchange had raised the prospect of fintech company OPay listing in Nigeria before pursuing an overseas listing, adding that President Tinubu had directed the Governor of the Central Bank of Nigeria to examine the matter while OPay works toward a domestic listing. He noted that technology had already transformed the market from manual processes to digital transactions, emphasizing that continued innovation would be essential to attracting younger investors.  Related News  Nigeria imports petrol as Dangote exports to W’Africa – Report  NGX turnover hits N158.29bn ahead Q3 earnings  NGX targets broader ownership  as OPay considers local IPO   
Reinforcing this outlook, President of AIHN, Kemi Awodein, stated that the association would continue working with regulators, the government, and investors to deepen the market, promote transparency, and encourage innovation. She observed that the market had evolved considerably over the past 30 years, with instruments such as green bonds and book-building becoming integral to its development, serving as a critical channel for mobilising growth capital and creating jobs.
Similarly, Group Deputy Managing Director of Afrinvest, Victor Ndukauba, highlighted the pivotal role investment banking firms have played in shaping Nigeria’s corporate landscape through bank consolidation, mergers and acquisitions, and bond issuances. He cited landmark transactions such as the merger that created the present-day United Bank for Africa, the formation of Stanbic IBTC Bank, GTBank’s international expansion, and Lagos State’s pioneering bond issue as clear evidence of the sector’s impact. Looking ahead, Ndukauba noted that digital platforms will increasingly dominate capital raising, enabling issuers to reach investors efficiently and allowing individuals to participate directly from their homes, as demonstrated by the digital-first approach of the Dangote IPO.        
At the event, AIHN recognised several firms for their contributions to theHere is the revised news report formatted in standard news writing style to fit The PUNCH Business and Economy editorial policy—strictly using narrative paragraphs without sub-headers, bullet points, or tables, while retaining the exact quotes provided.
                    
Speaking at the 30th anniversary and annual awards dinner of the Association of Issuing Houses of Nigeria in Lagos, Chairman of NGX Group, Umaru Kwairanga, highlighted the need to attract more retail investors, deepen market participation, and expand investment opportunities. He noted that market capitalisation had risen from below N50 trillion three years ago to more than N160 trillion, reflecting the market’s growth and its capacity to mobilise capital for national development.
“Market capitalisation has risen from below N50 trillion three years ago to over N160 trillion, and we are aiming for N300 trillion,” Kwairanga said.
To achieve this, the exchange has urged President Bola Tinubu’s administration to encourage more state-linked entities, particularly in the energy and technology sectors, to list on the market. Kwairanga also identified the ongoing initial public offering of the Dangote Refinery as a major catalyst for expanding domestic retail participation. The offer targets 10 million investors, a shift that could significantly boost engagement in a market that has recorded fewer than six million investors since Nigeria’s independence. 
Furthermore, the NGX chairman revealed that the exchange had raised the prospect of fintech company OPay listing in Nigeria before pursuing an overseas listing, adding that President Tinubu had directed the Governor of the Central Bank of Nigeria to examine the matter while OPay works toward a domestic listing. He noted that technology had already transformed the market from manual processes to digital transactions, emphasizing that continued innovation would be essential to attracting younger investors.  Related News  Nigeria imports petrol as Dangote exports to W’Africa – Report  NGX turnover hits N158.29bn ahead Q3 earnings  NGX targets broader ownership  as OPay considers local IPO   
Reinforcing this outlook, President of AIHN, Kemi Awodein, stated that the association would continue working with regulators, the government, and investors to deepen the market, promote transparency, and encourage innovation. She observed that the market had evolved considerably over the past 30 years, with instruments such as green bonds and book-building becoming integral to its development, serving as a critical channel for mobilising growth capital and creating jobs.
Similarly, Group Deputy Managing Director of Afrinvest, Victor Ndukauba, highlighted the pivotal role investment banking firms have played in shaping Nigeria’s corporate landscape through bank consolidation, mergers and acquisitions, and bond issuances. He cited landmark transactions such as the merger that created the present-day United Bank for Africa, the formation of Stanbic IBTC Bank, GTBank’s international expansion, and Lagos State’s pioneering bond issue as clear evidence of the sector’s impact. Looking ahead, Ndukauba noted that digital platforms will increasingly dominate capital raising, enabling issuers to reach investors efficiently and allowing individuals to participate directly from their homes, as demonstrated by the digital-first approach of the Dangote IPO.
